**Summary.** This paper analyzes non-Hermitian generalizations of kinetically constrained models, such as those describing Rydberg arrays. Using Krylov complexity, the authors demonstrate that non-Hermiticity causes the left and right eigenstate ensembles to exhibit fundamentally different dynamical behaviors. This highlights the necessity of treating left and right eigenvectors separately when characterizing non-Hermitian quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the complex interplay between non-Hermiticity, quantum dynamics, and ergodicity breaking in lattice models relevant to Rydberg arrays, which is a key area in AMO physics and condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the breakdown of thermalization and ergodicity in non-Hermitian quantum many-body systems, specifically focusing on the dynamical differences between left and right eigenstate ensembles.

**Main result.** Non-Hermiticity causes distinct dynamical behaviors in the left and right Krylov ensembles, such as different trends in Krylov complexity and the emergence of atypical low-entanglement eigenstates in the left ensemble.

**Method.** The authors employ Krylov complexity analysis, utilizing Arnoldi's algorithm to construct independent left and right Krylov bases for non-Hermitian Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on non-Hermitian generalizations of paradigmatic kinetically constrained models, such as the PXP and PPXPP models, which are effective descriptions for a 1D Rydberg atom array under blockade.

**Key observables.** Krylov complexity, eigenstate structure, entanglement entropy, and the behavior of Arnoldi coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of non-Hermiticity ($\gamma$) and the system size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires treating the left and right eigenstate ensembles separately due to the inherent asymmetry introduced by non-Hermiticity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distinct Krylov complexity evolution (oscillations vs. saturation) for left and right ensembles, and compare entanglement entropy between the two ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the non-Hermitian models, applying the Arnoldi algorithm to generate left/right bases, and then comparing the resulting Krylov dynamics and eigenstate properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermalization in closed quantum systems and its breakdown are central to understanding the emergence of statistical behavior in isolated many-body systems. Kinetic constraints can hinder thermalization by restricting the allowed dynamics and giving rise to non-ergodic behavior. While various aspects of ergodicity breaking in kinetically constrained models have been extensively studied in Hermitian systems, their behavior in non-Hermitian kinetically constrained models remains less explored. In particular, the inequivalence of left and right eigenstate ensembles in non-Hermitian systems raises the question of whether they exhibit qualitatively different dynamical and eigenstate properties. In this work, we study non-Hermitian generalizations of the paradigmatic PXP and PPXPP models, focusing on their Krylov complexity, eigenstate structure, and dynamics. By exploiting the Arnoldi's algorithm, we find distinct behavior of Krylov complexity in the left and right eigenstate ensembles as non-Hermiticity is increased: in the right ensemble, it exhibits persistent oscillations with an increasing period, while in the left ensemble, it gradually saturates. The characteristic arch-shaped profile of the Arnoldi coefficients is also modified differently in the two ensembles, with a Hermitian-like arch re-emerging in the left ensemble at large non Hermiticity. Additionally, in the non-Hermitian PXP model, the right ensemble retains the characteristic scar structure, while the left ensemble develops additional non-scar eigenstates with anomalously low entanglement and large overlap with the vacuum state. These findings reveal that non-Hermiticity can affect Krylov dynamics and eigenstate structure differently in the left and right ensembles, demonstrating the need to treat them separately when characterizing non-Hermitian kinetically constrained systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that applying local bit-flip error correction protocols can significantly extend the memory lifetime of time-crystalline order in a noisy quantum simulation. By implementing these error correction circuits on a superconducting processor, the authors show that the system's ability to maintain spatiotemporal order is robustly protected, confirming the utility of QEC for stabilizing exotic quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges the physics of non-equilibrium quantum matter (time crystals) with the practical challenges of quantum computation, demonstrating how QEC techniques can stabilize exotic quantum phases against noise.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to extend the operational lifetime of time-crystalline memory in a noisy quantum processor by implementing local error correction protocols.

**Main result.** Local bit-flip error correction significantly prolongs the memory decay time of the time-crystalline response, increasing it by a factor of up to two compared to bare dynamics.

**Method.** The study uses superconducting quantum processors to simulate a driven Ising model, applying stochastic local bit-flip error correction (repetition codes of distances three and five) to mitigate environmental decoherence.

**Model / system.** The system is a driven, spatially disordered two-dimensional Ising model simulated on a superconducting quantum processor using a honeycomb lattice. The dynamics are governed by a Floquet drive, and the error correction involves ancilla qubits and syndrome measurements.

**Key observables.** Temporal memory decay ($	au$), spatial alignment ($A_Z$), connected correlations ($G_c(d)$), domain-wall fraction ($D$), and quantum negativity ($\mathcal{N}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error correction distances (3-site and 5-site), the probability ($p$) of activating the stochastic drive/correction, and the lattice size (up to 68 physical qubits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the effectiveness of local bit-flip error correction and assumes that the measured physical time is an accurate conversion from the cycle-domain decay scale.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare connected correlations and domain-wall fractions for bare vs. recovered dynamics, showing recovery maintains order; Figure 2 shows temporal memory loss curves; Figure 3 compares error distributions ($W$ and $K$); Figure 5 compares entanglement (Negativity) across protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the time-crystal model and the challenge of decoherence. It then introduces the error correction methodology, comparing the decay of temporal and spatial order under bare dynamics versus various local recovery protocols. Finally, it quantifies the improvement in memory lifetime and spatial stabilization achieved by the conditional correction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Discrete time crystals are non-equilibrium phases of matter with spatiotemporal order and a response that breaks the time-translation symmetry of a periodic external drive. Interactions, spatial disorder and correlated random driving can in theory stabilize this order. In experiments, however, coupling to the environment limits the time crystal lifetime. Here we use stochastic local bit-flip error correction to prolong the time-crystalline response of a driven, spatially disordered two-dimensional Ising model on a superconducting quantum processor, using a repetition code with distances three and five on a honeycomb lattice with up to 68 physical qubits. Even though ancilla qubits, midcircuit syndrome measurements and decoding increase the required quantum and classical resources, the stochastic drive increases the memory decay time by a factor of up to two. At late times we observe that recovery stabilizes spatial order across the lattice. The spin-configuration distributions explain this separation, as recovery suppresses disagreeing bonds while broadening occupies completely reversed states. Syndrome measurements without correction accelerate the memory decay, demonstrating the importance of conditional correction. Our results show that local bit-flip error correction can preserve time-crystalline memory over more drive cycles than the bare dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces DeepMesoD, a neural network method that learns effective mesoscopic field equations directly from microscopic simulations. It bridges the gap between detailed, slow microscopic models and overly simplified bulk descriptions by preserving crucial spatial patterns. The method is shown to accurately model diverse magnetic systems, offering dramatic speedups while capturing complex physics like domain wall interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, data-driven tool for simulating complex, non-equilibrium dynamics in condensed matter systems where traditional analytical or purely microscopic methods fail due to computational cost or complexity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop efficient methods to simulate mesoscopic dynamics that accurately resolve spatial patterns, bridging the gap between computationally limited microscopic simulations and pattern-erasing bulk descriptions.

**Main result.** The DeepMesoD framework successfully learns effective mesoscopic field equations for complex magnetic systems, achieving massive computational speedups while accurately reproducing known physical limits and capturing subtle material effects like interlayer coupling changes.

**Method.** A novel neural network approach (DeepMesoD) is used to learn the effective mesoscopic field equation directly from microscopic trajectories, constrained by fundamental physical principles like the fluctuation-dissipation relation.

**Model / system.** The study applies to general dissipative dynamics, focusing on magnetic systems like the Ising-Kac model, 2D Heisenberg ferromagnets, and layered CrBr3 films. The dynamics are modeled via stochastic field equations incorporating learned free-energy functionals and dissipation operators.

**Key observables.** Mesoscopic field evolution, free-energy functional, dissipation operator, spectral power, and correlation rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coarse-graining scale, temperature (T=0.75, T=1.5), and fault geometry/placement in layered materials.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are assumed to belong to a general class of dissipative dynamics satisfying the fluctuation-dissipation relation; the learned model assumes locality for certain interaction factors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow from microscopic to mesoscopic learning, show the recovery of known hydrodynamic limits, and demonstrate the ability to model complex fault structures in layered magnets.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces DeepMesoD, validates it on the Ising-Kac model, extends it to the Heisenberg ferromagnet, and applies it to layered CrBr3, detailing the mathematical constraints and the computational efficiency gains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microscopic simulations resolve spatial patterns but are feasible only over small domains and short timescales, whereas bulk descriptions erase those patterns through spatial averaging. Mesoscopic fields preserve this spatial organisation, but their effective equations are known only for selected microscopic systems and scaling limits. We introduce Deep Mesoscopic Dynamics (DeepMesoD), which uses neural networks to learn an effective mesoscopic field equation from microscopic trajectories at a prescribed coarse-graining scale. For the nonconserved dissipative dynamics studied here, DeepMesoD learns the free-energy functional with a spatial interaction kernel and a state-dependent dissipation operator constrained by the fluctuation-dissipation relation. We first validate the construction in the Ising-Kac model, where the learned mesoscopic equation recovers the known hydrodynamic limit and rollouts remain stable far beyond the training horizon. We then treat two systems without closed-form mesoscopic descriptions. For a two-dimensional Heisenberg ferromagnet governed by atomistic spin dynamics, DeepMesoD learns a mesoscopic model whose predictions match atomistic references up to 1 ns, and a rollout with an unseen initial condition remains bounded up to 25 ns. For layered CrBr$_3$, the learned kernel captures the suppression and sign reversal of interlayer coupling at a stacking fault whose location and strength are not supplied as inputs. Controlled changes to the learned interaction show how fault number and placement shape multistep magnetic responses, complementing our magneto-photoluminescence measurements. Across the two atomistic systems, DeepMesoD achieves a three-to-four-order-of-magnitude reduction in wall-clock time compared with atomistic spin dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a generalized theory for Dynamical Quantum Phase Transitions (DQPTs) in non-Hermitian systems by deriving a non-biorthogonal Born rule. It shows that the transition rate can be robustly calculated using the overlap of left and right eigenstates, even when the standard normalization fails. The framework successfully classifies different types of DQPTs, including those occurring at exceptional points.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical framework connecting non-Hermitian physics, open quantum systems, and phase transitions, which is highly relevant for understanding dissipation and measurement processes in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a rigorous probabilistic interpretation for non-Hermitian quantum mechanics, particularly in the context of Dynamical Quantum Phase Transitions (DQPTs). It addresses the ambiguity arising from the standard biorthogonal normalization.

**Main result.** A new 'non-biorthogonal Born rule' is proposed, which uses the mutual overlap of left and right states to define a physically accessible transition rate. The theory classifies DQPTs, including exceptional-point variants (EP-DQPTs), based on the vanishing of the subsystem evolution operator's determinant.

**Method.** The analysis involves generalizing DQPT diagnostics from a reference state to a physical subspace, utilizing the resolvent method, and analyzing the Loschmidt echo and the determinant of the projected propagator, $\det U_{SS}(t)$.

**Model / system.** The study models dynamics using a globally Hermitian Hamiltonian $H = H_S + H_E + H_{S-E}$, where a small subsystem $S$ is coupled to a large environment $E$ (modeled as a tight-binding chain).

**Key observables.** Loschmidt Echo ($L_\psi(t)$), Transition Probability ($W_1$), $\det U_{SS}(t)$, and the overlap $|\langle L_1|R_1angle|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strengths and parameters defining the phase diagram (e.g., $g/\lambda, J_c/\lambda$) are crucial for identifying different DQPT regimes (Orthogonal, Non-biorthogonal, EP-DQPT).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on generalizing DQPTs to a physical subspace $S$ and contrasts the results with the assumption of bounded time-local generators for the subsystem dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram mapping different DQPT regimes (A-D) and show critical-time Loschmidt-echo landscapes and transition probability dependence on input state overlap.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of the standard Born rule, then generalizing DQPT diagnostics to a physical subspace, deriving the non-biorthogonal Born rule, and finally classifying the resulting transitions, including the EP-DQPTs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Hermitian dynamics possesses distinct left and right eigenstates, but their normalization freedom obscures a direct probabilistic interpretation.   Starting from a globally Hermitian system governed by the standard Born rule, we generalize dynamical quantum phase transitions (DQPTs) from a reference state to a physical subspace.   This generalization provides a clear probabilistic interpretation of left and right states: when each state is normalized to itself, their mutual overlap determines a directly accessible dynamical transition rate.   We discuss the new phenomena governed by this emergent non-biorthogonal Born rule and further identify a class of exceptional-point DQPTs (EP-DQPTs), at which the eigenstates of the critical propagator coalesce.   Our theory places the probabilistic interpretation of non-Hermitian quantum mechanics on the firm footing of Hermitian quantum mechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a scalable method to deterministically create complex multipartite entangled states (like W and GHZ states) in mechanical resonators coupled to superconducting qubits. By combining optimal control techniques with quantum state tomography, the authors not only prepare these states but also develop a robust sensing protocol that isolates collective noise effects from local decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of a solid-state mechanical system (HBAR) with superconducting qubits to generate and test complex entangled states is highly relevant for scalable quantum hardware development and quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the deterministic and programmable generation of multipartite entangled states among mechanical modes, which is crucial for quantum simulation and sensing.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrated the preparation of W, GHZ, and cluster states in a mechanical system and developed a method to quantify inter-mode phase correlations by isolating them from local decoherence.

**Method.** The protocol uses optimal-control-engineered isometries implemented via a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to a high-overtone bulk acoustic wave resonator (HBAR). State characterization involves quantum state tomography and entanglement witnesses.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a circuit quantum acoustics (cQAD) setup involving a transmon qubit coupled to multiple phonon modes in an HBAR. The dynamics are analyzed using Hamiltonian descriptions and master equations to model decoherence.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F$), Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME) witnesses ($	ext{Tr}[\hat{W}ho]$), and the ratio $R_N(t)$ used to estimate collective dephasing rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension two (for MPS), coupling strengths ($g_k$), and decoherence rates ($\gamma^{(k)}_1, \gamma^{(k)}_\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on the two lowest Fock states ($\{|0angle_k, |1angle_k\}$) of each mode, and the decoherence analysis relies on specific master equation forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the HBAR device, the algorithm for MPS preparation using isometries, and show experimental/simulated fidelities for W, GHZ, and cluster states for various particle numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from demonstrating the physical protocol for state preparation (using isometries and tomography) to analyzing the theoretical robustness of these states against decoherence (comparing W vs. GHZ decay rates).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multimode mechanical resonators offer a large set of densely packed, long-lived bosonic modes within a single compact device, making them attractive for quantum simulation, quantum information processing, and quantum-enhanced sensing. Many such applications rely on multipartite entanglement, yet deterministic mechanical entanglement has so far been largely limited to pairs of modes. Here we demonstrate the deterministic and programmable preparation of multipartite entangled states among the phonon modes of a high-overtone bulk acoustic wave resonator coupled to a superconducting transmon qubit. By sequentially applying optimal-control-engineered isometries between the qubit and successive modes, we generate matrix product states of bond dimension two, including $W$, Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ), and cluster states. We fully characterize these states via quantum state tomography and certify genuine multipartite entanglement among up to four modes. We further demonstrate correlated-noise sensing by exploiting the complementary decoherence responses of the stored $W$ and GHZ states, isolating inter-mode phase correlations from local decoherence and placing quantitative bounds on collective dephasing. Our results establish a scalable approach to preparing multipartite entangled states in mechanical systems, with promising applications in multiparameter sensing and tests of quantum mechanics with massive objects.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on achieving coherent optical control in a massive, three-dimensional trapped-ion crystal. By employing broadband cooling techniques, the researchers managed the dense spectrum of collective motional modes, enabling the crystal to function as a high-fidelity quantum register. This work significantly advances the feasibility of large-scale quantum simulation and metrology using trapped ions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the scalability bottleneck in trapped-ion quantum computing by demonstrating control over a highly complex, many-body motional bath, which is a major frontier in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving coherent optical control in large, three-dimensional (3D) trapped-ion crystals because the optical transition couples to a quasi-continuum bath of collective motional modes.

**Main result.** The authors successfully cooled thousands of motional modes near the ground state and demonstrated global coherent control, achieving an ensemble optical coherence time of 21.6 ms and reducing frequency uncertainty significantly towards the projection-noise limit.

**Method.** The research combines advanced cooling techniques (continuous cooling band spanning five octaves using parallel-EIT cooling) with high-precision spectroscopy (RSB/BSB) and coherent manipulation (Rabi oscillations) on a large ion crystal.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D Coulomb crystal of over 1,200 trapped ions. The complexity arises from the dense, quasi-continuous spectrum of $\approx 3N$ collective motional modes, which must be managed for global quantum operations.

**Key observables.** Ensemble optical coherence time (21.6 ms), frequency uncertainty ($\sigma/2\pi$ reduced from 389 Hz to 12 Hz), and mean phonon occupation ($ar{n} < 0.3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ion number ($N_{	ext{ion}} \approx 1,265$), cooling bandwidth (50 kHz to 2.1 MHz), and the scaling of spectroscopic uncertainty ($N^{-0.48}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the robustness of the spectral envelope and mode frequencies despite variations in the metastable equilibrium configuration of the crystal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 3D crystal configuration and mode density, the broadband near-ground-state cooling process via sideband spectroscopy, and the scaling of frequency uncertainty with increasing ion number.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental platform, the necessity of broadband cooling to manage the dense mode spectrum, the successful implementation of global coherent control, and the quantitative demonstration of enhanced precision scaling in the large ensemble.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Leading quantum platforms are largely limited to one or two dimensions, whereas three-dimensional Coulomb crystals of trapped ions self-assemble into dense qubit arrays with no rearrangement overhead. Coherent optical control of such crystals has nonetheless been out of reach, because the optical transition couples to nearly all $3N$ collective modes, i.e., a thermally occupied quasi-continuum bath. Here we cool about 3,600 modes of a crystal of over 1,200 ions below single-phonon occupation, using a continuous cooling band that spans more than five octaves in vibrational spectrum, from about 50 kHz to 2.1 MHz. The crystal then serves as a register of optical qubits, allowing global initialization, coherent manipulation and collective detection. We demonstrate ensemble optical coherence of 21.6 milliseconds and reduce the optical-transition frequency uncertainty from 389 Hz for a single ion to 12 Hz for over a thousand ions, approaching the expected projection-noise scaling. Our approach could be extended to even larger crystals with phonon-mediated many-body couplings, opening a route towards entanglement-enhanced precision metrology and three-dimensional quantum simulation, particularly with spatial-shaped illumination for region-resolved collective control.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive quantum process model for photodetection that rigorously incorporates both photon number and spectral degrees of freedom. By deriving modified Kraus operators, the model accurately simulates realistic detector inefficiencies and spectral filtering. This tool is essential for advancing quantum optical experiments and quantum information processing by providing a 'plug and play' framework for complex measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, generalized theoretical tool for quantum optics, allowing researchers to move beyond idealized photon-number measurements to model real-world detector limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Theoretical models of photodetection often fail to account for spectral characteristics, limiting their applicability in modern quantum optics experiments.

**Main result.** The authors developed a complete quantum process model using Kraus operators that successfully incorporates both photon-number statistics and frequency-dependent detector response.

**Method.** The model is formulated using the operator-sum representation (Kraus operators) derived from a beam-splitter interaction, allowing it to function as a general Positive Operator Valued Measurement (POVM).

**Model / system.** The system models photodetection by treating the interaction between the incident field and the detector through a beam-splitter, accounting for realistic detector inefficiencies and finite spectral windows.

**Key observables.** Detection probabilities ($P_{j=1}, P_{j=0}, P_{jj'}$), which depend on the overlap between the detector's spectral response and the incident photon's spectral profile.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector efficiency $\eta(\omega)$, incident spectral profile $\psi(\omega)$, and spectral bandwidths ($s_A, s_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the detector can be described by a beam-splitter interaction and uses the operator-sum representation to handle realistic imperfections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (beam-splitter model) and plot detection probabilities ($P_j$) versus varying spectral bandwidths for single-photon and coherent state detection, including HOM interference results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, derives the modified Kraus operators via the beam-splitter formalism, and then applies this comprehensive model to three canonical scenarios: single-photon detection, coherent state detection, and Hong-Ou-Mandel interference.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photo-detection is a fundamental component of experimental photonics, yet theoretical models often treat detectors solely in the photon-number basis, neglecting other relevant degrees of freedom such as spectral characteristics. We present a complete quantum process model for physically realistic photodetection that incorporates both photon-number effects and frequency-dependent detector response, described through explicitly derived Kraus operators in the operator-sum representation. This formalism accommodates realistic inefficiencies, models detection within finite spectral windows, and can be directly substituted for idealized models in quantum optical analyses. The model leads to identifying the suitable detection window of the detectors relative to the spectral distribution of incident photons and conversely, in order to maximize the observability of quantum effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This study analyzes how coherent phonons are generated in semiconductors when excited by ultrafast lasers. By comparing experimental measurements across the band gap with theoretical models, the authors show that the process is controlled by the specific electronic transitions (transition selectivity) rather than the total thermalized carrier density. This highlights the importance of non-equilibrium carrier dynamics in condensed matter response.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, first-principles understanding of non-equilibrium carrier dynamics, showing how transient, non-thermalized populations dictate macroscopic material responses like lattice vibrations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the physical mechanism governing the generation of coherent optical phonons in semiconductors following ultrafast laser excitation.

**Main result.** The coherent phonon amplitude depends on transition selectivity, weighted by optical matrix elements, rather than simply on the total thermalized carrier density, contradicting simpler thermal models.

**Method.** The authors combine systematic ultrafast laser measurements across the band gap with first-principles DFT calculations to model the photoexcitation dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a semiconductor (Tellurium) excited by tunable ultrafast lasers near the electronic band gap. The theoretical framework compares thermalized carrier models (DECP) against a transition-resolved prethermal force model.

**Key observables.** Coherent amplitude of the fully symmetric A1 phonon mode, and the spectral dependence of this amplitude on the excitation photon energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Excitation wavelength tuning across the band gap, and the resulting excited transition state lifetime (estimated to be a few femtoseconds).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The prethermal force model assumes the force is governed by the sum over optical transitions weighted by coupling strengths and transition probabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the spectral evolution of the coherent A1 response vs. fluence, comparing the measured responsivity against predictions from thermalized models and the successful fit using the prethermal force model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the problem (phonon generation mechanism), testing existing models (DECP theory), demonstrating their failure against spectral measurements, and finally proposing and validating a transition-resolved prethermal force model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultrafast laser excitation of carriers in semiconductors often results in the creation of fully symmetric coherent optical phonons, typically understood in terms of a thermalized transient carrier population. We test this understanding with systematic measurements and first-principles calculations of the photon-energy dependence of ultrafast-laser-driven coherent phonon generation in tellurium, using excitation wavelengths around the electronic band gap. We observe that, contrary to the expectations of models assuming a thermalized carrier population, the coherent amplitude of the fully symmetric $A_1$ mode changes only weakly when tuning wavelength across the band gap. The measured spectral dependence is instead reproduced by a transition-resolved prethermal force weighted by optical matrix elements and band- and momentum-dependent $A_1$ deformation potentials. Fitting this model to the data yields an excited transition state lifetime of a few femtoseconds. These results identify transition selectivity, rather than a thermalized total carrier density, as a key factor controlling coherent-phonon generation in semiconductors excited near a band gap.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the development and experimental implementation of advanced noise characterization techniques on a superconducting quantum processor. By revising benchmarking protocols and extending tensor-network tomography, the authors successfully map the noise structure and validate noise models against physical circuits. This research is crucial for understanding hardware limitations and estimating the reliable scale of quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, hardware-specific methodologies for quantifying noise in superconducting qubits, which is foundational knowledge for any theoretical physicist working on open quantum systems or quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the critical need to accurately characterize noise sources in superconducting quantum processors to enable robust error mitigation and estimate achievable circuit sizes.

**Main result.** The developed noise characterization techniques allow for the extraction and validation of approximate sublayer noise models, demonstrating that the total estimation error can be kept below 13% for moderately deep circuits.

**Method.** The authors employ revised cycle-benchmarking protocols and extended tensor-network tomography methods to map the noise structure by measuring the decay of Pauli expectation values.

**Model / system.** The work is performed on a superconducting quantum processor with a square-lattice topology (VTT Q50), utilizing transmon-based qubits and flux-tunable couplers.

**Key observables.** Pauli expectation values (decay curves), gate infidelities (PRX, CZ, RO), and the total estimation error in benchmark circuits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise rates ($\lambda_k$), circuit depth (L=15), and width (N=50) for performance estimation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating single-qubit gate errors as negligible compared to two-qubit errors, and the noise channel acting before each layer of Clifford gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the coupling map and fidelities of the processor, the cumulative distribution of gate infidelities, and comparisons between measured signal decay and predictions from single-layer versus jointly fitted multi-layer noise models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the necessity of noise characterization, detailing the revised cycle-benchmarking and tensor-network methods, and finally applying these tools to map the noise structure and validate the learned models against physical and chemical benchmark circuits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Noise characterisation not only enables error mitigation but also provides valuable information about the intrinsic performance of a quantum device, identifying dominant error contributions and bounding noise stability well beyond the aggregated calibration data. Deploying such methods on a specific hardware topology must combine efficiency and accuracy, assess noise stability, and validate the reconstructed noise model. Here we develop and implement these methods on a superconducting processor with square-lattice topology. First, we revise the cycle-benchmarking protocol and reduce the bias arising from unlearnable degrees of freedom via a regularised cost function. Second, we extend a previously developed tensor-network tomography method to match the device layout and show how its accuracy can be improved by repeated layer execution. Both methods allow approximate sublayer noise models to be extracted from parent ones. Using noise characterisation as a tool, we map the noise structure, track drift, and validate the adequacy of the learnt noise models against experimentally observed noisy estimations in benchmark physical and chemistry circuits. These results inform further improvements in the underlying technology and provide estimates of the size of noise-mitigated circuits that the device can support.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a macroscopic fluctuation theory to describe classical lattice gases undergoing continuous monitoring. It shows that measurement introduces a confining term into the system's effective action, fundamentally altering transport properties. The theory predicts that monitoring can suppress dynamical phase transitions and provides detailed scaling laws for current fluctuations near boundaries.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between continuous monitoring (a measurement process) and the modification of macroscopic fluctuation theories is highly relevant to open quantum systems, as measurement back-action fundamentally alters system dynamics and steady states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a macroscopic fluctuation theory for diffusive classical lattice gases subjected to continuous coarse-grained monitoring.

**Main result.** Observation adds a quadratic confining term to the MFT action, and monitoring can suppress dynamical phase transitions, potentially changing their order.

**Method.** The authors use Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT) within the hydrodynamic limit to analyze large-deviation functions for monitored stochastic systems.

**Model / system.** The system is a diffusive classical lattice gas on a bounded domain, modeled as a continuous time Markov process. The analysis focuses on the coarse-grained density and current fields.

**Key observables.** The current large-deviation function, the MFT action, the particle density profile, and the cumulants of the current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement regime (typical-outcome/quenched), the mobility ($\sigma(ho)$), and the boundary conditions (e.g., vanishing mobility at boundaries).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the coarse-grained (hydrodynamic) limit and assumes the typical-outcome (quenched) regime for measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figure 19 illustrates the convergence of the rate function $\phi_\gamma(J)$ for a monitored SSEP, showing both bulk convergence and boundary-layer profile convergence after appropriate rescaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the theory by establishing the MFT framework for monitored systems, deriving the effect of observation on the action, and then applying this to specific models like the SSEP to analyze phase transition suppression and current statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a macroscopic fluctuation theory for diffusive classical lattice gases subject to continuous coarse-grained monitoring. Treating measurements in the typical-outcome (quenched) regime, we show that observation adds a quadratic confining term to the MFT action that pins the density to its hydrodynamic profile. We derive sufficient conditions under which, in the presence of monitoring, the current large-deviation function remains convex, the additivity principle holds, and dynamical phase transitions are excluded. For particle-hole-symmetric one-dimensional models we find that monitoring suppresses dynamical phase transitions and can change their order from second to first, as we demonstrate in the Katz--Lebowitz--Spohn model. In the strong-measurement limit, we find that the current distribution becomes Gaussian and determine the scaling of its cumulants: they follow simple power laws when the mobility remains nonzero throughout the system, but exhibit anomalous logarithmic scaling when the mobility vanishes at the boundaries.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'Lifting,' a novel framework designed to improve Quantum State Discrimination for noisy mid-circuit measurements on superconducting qubits. It tackles two key issues: the fundamental error limit (Bayes risk) and state deterioration caused by measurement history. By combining repetition encoding with context-aware, in situ calibration, the framework significantly boosts the reliability of quantum operations for error correction and computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on hardware-aware error mitigation techniques like in situ calibration and advanced readout encoding directly impacts the feasibility of scaling quantum computation, which is of high interest to theorists studying open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately performing Quantum State Discrimination (QSD) for Mid-Circuit Measurements (MCMs) in superconducting quantum hardware, which is degraded by noise and state deterioration over time.

**Main result.** The proposed 'Lifting' framework, which combines repetition encoding, neural decoding, and in situ calibration, successfully improves circuit success rates across various MCM workloads compared to static calibration methods.

**Method.** The authors developed a framework that uses joint In-phase and Quadrature (I-Q) readout signals from auxiliary qubits (repetition encoding) and trains a neural network decoder using calibration data acquired contextually during the actual circuit execution (in situ calibration).

**Model / system.** The work is implemented on superconducting quantum processors (e.g., IBM processors), utilizing dispersive readout interactions characterized by a Hamiltonian involving resonator and qubit coupling. The core process involves mapping continuous I-Q measurement signals to discrete quantum states.

**Key observables.** Bayes Risk (fundamental error limit), I-Q readout distributions, Whole-trace accuracy, and final-syndrome correctness for quantum error correction circuits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error rate is fundamentally bounded by the Bayes risk; state deterioration occurs on microsecond timescales during circuit execution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Static calibration based on isolated measurements is insufficient because it fails to account for state deterioration that occurs during the actual, dynamic workload execution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate state deterioration in qubit-reuse stabilizer workloads, comparing initial SPAM references to later, degraded readout distributions, and demonstrate the workflow of the Lifting framework using joint I-Q signals.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of MCM noise and state deterioration relative to the Bayes risk limit. It then introduces the Lifting framework, detailing repetition encoding and in situ calibration. Finally, it validates the framework through experimental results on superconducting processors using stabilizer measurements, parity checks, and teleportation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing promises new capabilities beyond classical computation. Mid-circuit measurements (MCMs) enable error correction and outcome-dependent control, but their reliability is limited by noise on superconducting hardware. These operations depend on accurate quantum state discrimination (QSD), which assigns measured signals to state labels. Neural network-based (NN-based) QSD improves this assignment, yet its error remains bounded below by the Bayes risk of the available readout distributions. A complementary challenge is state deterioration as successive measurements accumulate and circuit execution advances, making static calibration less representative of the workload. We present Lifting, a QSD framework that addresses both challenges through repetition encoding, neural decoding, and in situ calibration. Encoding supplies joint in-phase and quadrature (I-Q) readout signals from the target and auxiliary qubits, while contextual calibration trains the decoder for the conditions encountered during execution. Our theoretical analysis shows that retaining additional channels from a fixed joint distribution cannot increase Bayes risk and establishes sufficient conditions for contraction of an overlap-based upper bound. Experiments across three IBM processors evaluate the framework on repeated stabilizer measurement, parity-check, and teleportation workloads.

</details>

### [Observation of Photon Bunching in Broadband Multimode Thermal Light Enabled by Temporal Imaging](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17228v1)

**Authors:** Filip Sośnicki, Michał Mikołajczyk, Sanjay Kapoor, Dmitri B. Horoshko, Mikhail I. Kolobov, Michał Karpiński

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel technique called pre-detection temporal imaging to overcome detector limitations when measuring photon bunching in broadband thermal light. By temporally magnifying the optical field, the authors directly measured the expected thermal bunching signature ($g^{(2)}(0) = 2$) on ultrafast timescales. This establishes a general platform for advanced statistical optics measurements in quantum and biophysical contexts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general platform for broadband statistical optics, directly addressing the measurement limitations in quantum optics and biophysics that plague traditional correlation measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is observing the temporal photon bunching in broadband multimode thermal light, which is severely limited by the temporal resolution of conventional detectors.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the direct observation of thermal photon bunching ($g^{(2)}(0) = 2$) on sub-3.5 picosecond timescales using a novel technique.

**Method.** They propose and implement 'pre-detection temporal imaging' using an electro-optic time lens to temporally magnify the optical field before measuring the intensity autocorrelation function in a standard Hanbury Brown-Twiss setup.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) to generate correlated photon pairs, treating the signal field as a thermal state. The core platform is the temporal imaging setup, which acts as a time lens with a demonstrated magnification factor of 660.

**Key observables.** The normalized second-order autocorrelation function, $g^{(2)}(	au)$, and the zero-delay correlation value, $g^{(2)}(0)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temporal magnification factor (e.g., 660), spectral bandwidth (up to 250 GHz), and time resolution (sub-3.5 ps).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurement relies on the assumption that the temporal imaging technique successfully overcomes the mode-averaging bottleneck inherent in measuring broadband thermal light.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time-resolved second-order autocorrelation measurement, the experimental setup involving temporal imaging components, and quantitative results showing $g^{(2)}(0) \approx 2$ for both multimode and near-single-mode regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical limitations of measuring broadband thermal light bunching, introduces the temporal imaging platform, details the experimental implementation using SPDC and time lenses, and presents quantitative measurements confirming the observation of $g^{(2)}(0)=2$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photons emitted by incoherent sources exhibit temporal bunching, a fundamental manifestation of Bose-Einstein statistics first observed by Hanbury Brown and Twiss (HBT). While intensity correlation measurements originated in astronomy and are now central across quantum technologies, biophysics, and spectroscopy, observing temporal bunching in broadband thermal light remains limited by detector temporal resolution. Conventional approaches - such as streak cameras or optical upconversion - introduce severe trade-offs in efficiency, bandwidth, or setup versatility. Here, we propose and demonstrate a third route: pre-detection temporal magnification of the optical field prior to measuring its intensity autocorrelation function in a conventional HBT setup. Using an electro-optic time lens with a temporal magnification of 660, we resolve the temporal intensity autocorrelation of broadband thermal light generated via non-degenerate spontaneous parametric down-conversion with a bandwidth of 250 GHz. This enables direct observation of thermal photon bunching on sub-3.5 picosecond timescales without narrow spectral filtering, establishing pre-detection temporal imaging as a general platform for broadband statistical optics, with applications spanning ultrafast quantum optics, biophysical imaging, and potentially stellar intensity interferometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on Zeus-256, a large-scale, room-temperature NV-diamond quantum processor featuring 256 logical qubits. It demonstrates the execution of complex quantum algorithms, including Shor's factorization, using a novel, programmable biomolecular quantum bus for long-range entanglement. The results validate the system's capability for large-scale, controlled quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of a biomolecular, optically programmable bus to mediate long-range interactions in a solid-state diamond platform is highly relevant for building scalable quantum interconnects and addressing connectivity limitations in solid-state quantum computing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing scalable quantum processors that can simultaneously address qubit coherence, control fidelity, readout, inter-qubit connectivity, and overall system complexity at room temperature.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a 256-qubit processor executing complex algorithms like Shor's factorization and Grover search with high fidelity, utilizing a novel biomolecular quantum bus for reconfigurable interactions.

**Method.** The system benchmarks heterogeneous circuits (QFT, GHZ, Grover, Shor) by transpiling OpenQASM 2.0 circuits into sequences controlled by an RFSoC, involving microwave, optical, and bus-configuration pulses.

**Model / system.** The platform is Zeus-256, a room-temperature NV-diamond processor featuring 256 active logical qubits. Connectivity is mediated by an optically programmable, proprietary biomolecular quantum bus layer.

**Key observables.** Bell-CHSH value ($S = 2.8008 \pm 0.0446$), GHZ+ fidelity (99.8960%), Grover search success rate (93.55%), and successful factor recovery for 8-84 bit integers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** 256 simultaneously active logical qubits in a $16 	imes 16$ array; execution time for 84-bit factorization was 551.694 ms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The biomolecular bus mediates interactions between spatially separated fields; the reported timing metrics are limited to processor control time and do not establish quantum advantage.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the paper details performance metrics for various algorithms and correlation measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Zeus-256 architecture, details the control sequence generation, presents benchmarks across a wide range of quantum algorithms, and reports quantitative results on entanglement fidelity and factorization success.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report Zeus-256, a room-temperature nitrogen-vacancy (NV) diamond processor with 256 simultaneously active processor-level logical qubits arranged in a 16 x 16 array. Each logical qubit combines an NV electron-spin ensemble with nuclear-spin memory and ancillary resources. OpenQASM 2.0 circuits are transpiled into RFSoC-controlled microwave, radio-frequency, optical, timing, measurement and bus-configuration sequences. An optically programmable proprietary protein-chromophore layer with an internal metastable-triplet spin mediator provides a reconfigurable biomolecular quantum bus for local and long-range interactions. Benchmarks cover QFT, GHZ entanglement, Grover search, arithmetic, variational, neural-network, graph-state and Shor-family circuits. Factor recovery is reported for 11 integers of 8-84 bits, with 10 attempts per instance; the 84-bit endpoint uses 256 qubits and 40,250,342 reported gates, with a reported execution time of 551.694 ms. Bell-CHSH measurements yield S = 2.8008 +/- 0.0446. Three-qubit Grover search returns the marked state in 958 of 1,024 shots (93.55%), compared with an ideal reference of 94.53%. A separate 30,000-shot population-and-parity protocol yields fixed-phase GHZ+ fidelity of 99.8960%, an estimated standard error of 0.2885 percentage points and a conservative physical 95% interval of [98.2342%, 100%]. Timing metrics are partial and do not establish quantum advantage. Scaling to 9,984 processor-level logical qubits is an engineering target, distinct from the demonstrated register.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the experimental realization of a C-shunt flux-qubit coupler connecting two transmon qubits. By tuning the coupler flux, the researchers successfully engineered an operating point that minimizes residual ZZ interaction for long idling periods while enabling fast, high-fidelity Controlled-Z gates. This demonstrates a crucial capability for building scalable quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental blueprint for advanced quantum gate operations by solving the conflict between gate speed and idling coherence, which is a major hurdle in scaling superconducting quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is to operate superconducting quantum processors in a regime that allows for both fast entangling gates and low-error idling, as these require distinct operating conditions.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrated a C-shunt flux-qubit coupler enabling a 45 ns Controlled-Z (CZ) gate with a fidelity of 99.23% and confirmed an idle region with near-zero residual ZZ interaction.

**Method.** The study employed spectroscopy, conditional Ramsey measurements, and interleaved randomized benchmarking to characterize the coupler and measure gate fidelities.

**Model / system.** The system is a transmon-C-shunt-transmon (TCT) device where two transmon qubits are coupled via a flux-tunable, three-junction capacitively shunted flux-qubit coupler. The interaction is controlled by tuning the coupler flux to engineer the effective Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** CZ gate fidelity (99.23%), single-qubit X-gate fidelity (>99.7%), residual ZZ interaction strength ($\zeta_{ZZ}$), and effective transverse exchange ($g_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupler energy relaxation time ($T_{1,c} = 1.1 \mu$s), gate time (45 ns), and the ability to tune the system to an idle point where $\zeta_{ZZ} \approx 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation identified is the coupler energy relaxation time, which is modeled using a Lindblad master equation to estimate error contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the TCT architecture, spectral engineering maps showing the $|g_{	ext{eff}}| - |\zeta_{	ext{ZZ}}|$ plane, and randomized benchmarking results for both single-qubit and CZ gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for tunable interaction control, details the TCT device and its Hamiltonian, presents characterization via spectroscopy and mapping the idle region, and concludes with the measurement and analysis of the fast CZ gate fidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast entangling gates and low-interaction idling require distinct operating conditions in superconducting quantum processors. We experimentally realize a transmon-C-shunt-transmon (TCT) device in which a flux-tunable, three-junction capacitively shunted flux-qubit coupler connects two transmon data qubits. The C-shunt coupler provides a flux-tunable, positively anharmonic spectrum for tailoring the interactions between the data qubits. Conditional Ramsey and swap measurements identify an idle region with near-zero residual ZZ interaction strength and suppressed transverse exchange. At this bias, simultaneous single-qubit interleaved randomized benchmarking yields X-gate fldelities above 99.7%, consistent with individual operation. A 45 ns nonadiabatic controlled-Z (CZ) gate achieves an interleaved randomized benchmarking fldelity estimate of 99.23(15)%. Numerical simulations identify coupler energy relaxation as an important limitation on CZ-gate fldelity and indicate that extending the coupler lifetime can improve gate performance. These results demonstrate the experimental feasibility of C-shunt flux-qubit couplers for combining low-interaction idling with fast entangling operations between transmon qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically models the interaction between two quantum skyrmions in a frustrated magnet, finding that they form a weakly bound molecule. The binding is robustly characterized by excess number fluctuations and specific in-plane spin correlations, confirming a coherent magnon exchange mechanism. This suggests the molecule could function as a novel two-qubit element for quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a quantum skyrmion molecule and its use as a potential two-qubit element directly connects condensed matter magnetism with quantum information science, making it highly relevant for AMO/quantum optics researchers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the binding energy and coherent coupling mechanism between two quantum skyrmions in a frustrated spin-1/2 magnet.

**Main result.** Two skyrmions of natural size form a weakly bound molecule with a binding energy of $U \simeq -0.007J_1$, mediated by a coherent exchange of magnons that locks their relative helicity.

**Method.** The authors employed Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations and analyzed number fluctuations and in-plane spin correlations to characterize the binding.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 model on a triangular lattice with nearest-neighbor ($J_1$) and next-nearest-neighbor ($J_2$) interactions, subject to easy-axis anisotropy ($K$). Skyrmions are bound states of flipped spins ($N_f$).

**Key observables.** Binding energy ($U$), number fluctuations ($	ext{var}(n_A)$), and in-plane spin correlations ($\langle \mathbf{S}_{+i} \mathbf{S}_{-j} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Natural skyrmion size ($N_f^*=7$), binding energy $U \simeq -0.007J_1$, and anisotropy $K \in [0.02, 0.04]$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses finite flakes embedded in a ferromagnetic background, and the free-pair binding energy is treated as an upper bound due to DMRG convergence limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the minimum binding energy for single skyrmions at $N_f^*=7$, compare probability distributions for bound vs. independent pairs, and show non-zero in-plane correlations across the gap.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the single skyrmion size using binding energy analysis, then calculates the interaction energy for free pairs, and finally uses fluctuation and correlation measurements to confirm the coherent magnon exchange responsible for the molecular binding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum skyrmions in frustrated spin-$1/2$ magnets are bound states of a few flipped spins in a ferromagnetic background. Using exact diagonalization and density-matrix renormalization group calculations for the $J_1$--$J_2$ triangular-lattice model with easy-axis anisotropy $K$, we ask what happens when two such objects are brought together. The binding energy per flipped spin of a single skyrmion has a minimum at $\Nf^{*}=7$ for $K=0.02$--$0.04$, and skyrmions smaller than $\Nf^{*}$ do not exist as pairs: at any distance they coalesce into a single larger object, and pinning wells cannot prevent it. Two skyrmions of natural size, by contrast, form a bound molecule without pinning or external field, with binding energy $U\simeq-0.007J_1$ at $K=0.03$--$0.04$ and an equilibrium separation of $5.5$ lattice spacings that does not depend on $K$ or on the size of the simulation domain; at $K=0.02$, where the single skyrmion is itself marginally bound, the pair is unbound within our resolution. The two constituents keep their identity ($7+7$ flipped spins) and are coupled by a coherent exchange of magnons, visible as number fluctuations between the two halves of the domain in excess of those of two independent skyrmions and as in-plane spin correlations between the two skyrmion walls that reach $0.16$ across the gap. These correlations follow the vortex pattern of the two textures and lock their relative helicity, the quantum counterpart of the helicity-dependent interaction of classical skyrmions. We discuss the molecule as a self-assembled two-qubit element for skyrmion helicity qubits in a single layer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores controlling the light emitted by two coupled quantum emitters using periodic laser pulses. The authors show that pulsing allows for independent tailoring of the emission spectrum and the photon correlation statistics. This provides a powerful, versatile tool for designing quantum light sources beyond what is achievable with continuous driving.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum control in open quantum systems, demonstrating how time-domain engineering (pulsing) can decouple spectral features from photon statistics, which is a key concept in quantum light sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how periodic pulsed driving can control the collective emission spectra and photon statistics of two interacting quantum emitters, comparing these results to continuous-wave (CW) excitation.

**Main result.** Periodic pulsed driving offers a versatile control mechanism, allowing the emission spectra to mimic CW results while making the photon correlations insensitive to resonance conditions in the short-pulse limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses the master equation formalism to model the system's evolution under both CW and pulsed driving, employing the instantaneous-pulse approximation for pulsed regimes.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two identical, dipole-dipole-coupled two-level emitters. The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian and a master equation incorporating coherent evolution and dissipation rates.

**Key observables.** Collective emission spectra and second-order photon correlation functions, g^(2)(t,τ).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-pulse delay (τ_D), detuning (Δ), and the strength of the dipole-dipole coupling (Ω_12).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Born-Markov approximation and, for pulsed driving, assumes the instantaneous-pulse limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of g^(2)(t,τ) under CW versus pulsed driving, showing how the inter-pulse delay controls the statistics, while the spectrum reflects coupling and delay.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model for two coupled emitters, then analyzes the dynamics under CW driving, and finally focuses on the unique control offered by periodic pulsed driving to tailor both spectral and statistical properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the collective emission spectra and photon correlations of two identical dipole-dipole-coupled emitters under continuous-wave (CW) and periodic pulsed driving. Our results show that a periodic train of instantaneous $π_x$ pulses can generate emission spectra whose dominant features closely resemble those obtained under CW excitation. We find that the structure of the collective emission spectra reflects the interplay among the resonance conditions, dipole-dipole coupling, and the inter-pulse delay $τ_D$. We further demonstrate that finite-duration $π_x$ pulses with sufficiently high Rabi frequencies can closely reproduce the ideal instantaneous-pulse limit. In contrast to the emission spectra, the photon correlations $g^{(2)}(t,τ)$ are governed primarily by $τ_D$ and, unlike under CW driving, become increasingly insensitive to the resonance conditions in the short-pulse limit. These results establish periodic pulsed driving as a versatile means of tailoring both the emission spectra and the photon statistics of coupled two-emitter systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes variance squeezing in a V-type atom coupled to a dissipative cavity. The authors find that while squeezing alternates between $S_x$ and $S_y$ based on initial conditions, the squeezing depth of $S_x$ is robustly protected by weak environmental coupling and detuning. These findings offer practical guidelines for enhancing quantum coherence in high-precision quantum communication devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in cavity QED, providing quantitative insights into how environmental coupling and detuning can be engineered to protect fragile quantum correlations like variance squeezing, which is crucial for quantum communication.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the phenomenon of variance squeezing in a V-type atom that interacts with a dissipative optical cavity.

**Main result.** The optimal variance squeezing depth of $S_x$ is determined solely by the initial atomic state and is robustly enhanced by weak cavity-environment coupling and appropriate atom-cavity detuning.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical derivations to calculate the reduced density matrix and subsequently compute expectation values and variance-squeezing factors for the atomic spin operators.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a V-type three-level atom coupled to a dissipative optical cavity, treated as an open quantum system subject to various decay channels.

**Key observables.** Atomic spin operators ($S_x, S_y, S_z$), variance-squeezing factor ($V(S_x), V(S_y)$), and the expectation value $\langle S_z angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial state parameters ($\alpha$), cavity-environment coupling ($\kappa$), atom-cavity detuning ($\Delta$), and the SGI parameter ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats the system as an open quantum system, deriving the reduced density matrix by tracing out the environment degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of variance squeezing factors ($V(S_x)$ and $V(S_y)$) under varying initial parameters ($\alpha$), coupling ratios ($\gamma_0/\kappa$), and detuning ($\Delta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines variance squeezing, then derives the reduced density matrix and necessary expectation value formulas. It proceeds to numerically analyze the dynamics by investigating the influence of initial state parameters, system coupling strengths, and detuning.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variance squeezing of a V-type atom interacting with a dissipative cavity is investigated. First, the influence of atomic initial state parameters on the variance squeezing is explored. The results show that the atomic variance squeezing switches alternately between Sx and Sy as the initial state parameter alpha sweeps from -pi to pi. The two squeezing intervals are symmetric about alpha = pi/4, each with a width of pi/2. For alpha = pi/14, only the Sx component can generate variance squeezing and will eventually disappear with time evolution due to unavoidable environmental noise. Then, the influence of system parameters on the variance squeezing is also studied. It is found that, the optical variance squeezing depth of Sx is determined solely by the atomic initial parameters and is independent of the system parameters. Both weak cavity-environment coupling and atom-cavity detuning can significantly enhance the robustness of the Sx variance squeezing. The SGI parameter has an extremely weak influence on variance squeezing. These results are beneficial for high-precision quantum communication applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework to calculate the performance of Boson Sampling using single photons from on-demand sources, explicitly accounting for decoherence effects like dephasing. By modeling the evolution of multi-photon wave packets, the authors show how physical parameters dictate the achievable probability of generating highly entangled states, offering vital guidelines for experimental quantum optics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial quantitative link between fundamental physical noise processes (like dephasing) occurring at the single-photon source level and the measurable quantum complexity of a photonic quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes how decoherence (dephasing and decay) affects the probability distribution of N-photon entangled states generated via Boson Sampling using on-demand single photon sources.

**Main result.** The probability of generating entangled states, such as the 4-photon NOON state, depends quantitatively on the two-photon interference visibility, providing guidelines to link physical processes like temperature to quantum circuit output.

**Method.** A theoretical framework is developed that links physical decoherence sources to multiphoton correlation measurements by explicitly incorporating state symmetrization and wave packet evolution.

**Model / system.** The system models Boson Sampling using Fock states generated from arrays of on-demand single photon sources, specifically referencing Mesa Top Single Quantum Dots (MTSQDs). The theory tracks the evolution of the multi-photon wave packet through linear optical circuits.

**Key observables.** Probability of generating N-photon entangled states (e.g., NOON state), 2-photon coincidence count histogram, and the two-photon interference visibility ($V_{TPI}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Radiative decay lifetime ($T_1$), pure dephasing rate ($\gamma_{\phi}$), and the wave packet shape parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a weak-coupling limit for photonic devices and models the state evolution by transferring the symmetrization burden to the time-domain wave packet function.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MTSQD array enabling Fock state sampling, the single/multi-photon wave packet representations, and plots showing the dependence of $P_{NOON}$ and coincidence histograms on the dephasing rate ($T_2^*$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from describing the physical platform (MTSQDs) to developing a mathematical framework for multi-photon wave packet evolution, applying this framework to calculate specific observables like the 4-photon NOON state probability, and finally analyzing the dependence on decoherence timescales.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Boson sampling with array of on-demand single photons (discreet variable Fock states) as resource states provides distinct advantages in complexity scaling compared to single photon resource states mimicked by the states (continuous variable) generated in nonlinear photonics. Recent progress in realization of regular ordered array of mesa top single quantum dots (MTSQDs) enabling on-demand generation of many photon resource states with high single photon purity and indistinguishability [1,2] has opened the possibility of implementing chip-scale Boson sampling with single photons. An important question towards scale up of such circuits is how the dephasing and decay timescale of individual on-demand single photons affect the final probability distribution of the N-photon entangled state. In this work we report a framework that provides direct link between the physical sources of decoherence to the outcome of multiphoton correlation measurements. We explicitly incorporate the symmetrization of the N-photon states while accounting for the shape of the individual wave packets and dephasing. Using this framework, we show predictive estimates of the probability of generation of a 4-photon NOON state using an optical circuit initialized with 4 identical single photon wave packets, mimicking the emitted photons from MTSQD single photon sources reported in [2]. Specifically, we show how the probability of generating various 4-photon entangled states depend on the two-photon interference visibility which is determined by the pure dephasing timescale in relation to the radiative decay timescale. The results provide quantitative guidelines to measure 4 photon and higher number of entangled photon states generated in an optical circuit with on demand single photon sources and provide a link between physical processes such as temperature and the output state of the quantum optical circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to prepare and measure dark states in a single-molecule triplet using electron spin resonance coupled to an AFM tip. By modeling the system as a driven open quantum system, the authors theoretically predict and simulate the unique, time-invariant signature of the dark state. This work bridges fundamental quantum control concepts from atomic physics into the realm of molecular electronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies concepts from quantum optics (dark states, coherent driving) to a solid-state, molecular platform (ESR-AFM), providing a novel, all-electrical realization of quantum coherence control.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a protocol to prepare and probe dark states in a single-molecule electron spin resonance setup, extending techniques used for Rabi oscillations.

**Main result.** A dark state, which is a coherent superposition of two ZFS states, was characterized as a state that does not evolve in time despite continuous resonant driving. This was demonstrated by comparing its signature to that of an incoherent superposition.

**Method.** The study uses a combination of theoretical simulation (density-matrix formalism, Lindblad equation) and analogy to atomic physics to model the coherent manipulation of the system.

**Model / system.** The system is a neutral molecule with a zero-field-split (ZFS) first excited triplet, studied using single-molecule electron spin resonance via an AFM tip. The dynamics are modeled as a driven open quantum system.

**Key observables.** The total triplet population ($P_z(t)$) and the oscillation amplitudes ($\Delta P_x, \Delta P_y, \Delta P_z$) are key diagnostics for identifying the dark state signature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ZFS parameters ($D, E$), the magnetic field components ($\mathbf{B}(t)$), and the detuning ($\Delta$) are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the system as a driven open quantum system, employing the Lindblad master equation, and utilizing the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of ZFS state populations, showing the expected minimum signal for the dark state signature, which is robust against detuning.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical setup and Hamiltonian, then develops the theoretical framework using the density matrix and Lindblad equation, derives the dark state condition, and finally simulates the expected signatures using realistic parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a protocol to prepare and probe dark states in a single-molecule electron spin resonance based on atomic force microscopy (ESR-AFM), thus extending existing techniques developed to address Rabi oscillations. To this end, we simulate in analogy to atomic physics the coherent manipulation of a three-level system in the V-configuration using a multifrequency magnetic field. More specifically, we consider a neutral molecule with a zero-field-split (ZFS) first excited triplet and analyze within a pump-probe scheme the dynamics of the total triplet population. In the triplet subspace, the dark state is a coherent superposition formed by two ZFS states which does not evolve in time despite a continuous resonant driving towards the third one. We characterize the properties of this state comparing it to its fully incoherent counterpart. Within a density-matrix formalism for open systems, we then compute the expected dark state signatures both for ideal scenarios and for the realistic parameterization of ESR-AFM on pentacene.

</details>
