**Summary.** This paper details a quantum sensing protocol for trapped-ion systems that enhances weak-field measurement precision by combining detuned motional squeezing and spin-oscillator entanglement. By leveraging these two quantum resources, the protocol achieves an exponential improvement in sensitivity over standard methods. Crucially, this enhanced performance is shown to be robust against environmental noise, making it highly practical for quantum-enhanced sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum metrology using trapped ions, a canonical platform in AMO physics. The combination of squeezing and entanglement to achieve super-SQL scaling is a key theoretical advance for quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to achieve measurement precision for weak signal sensing that surpasses the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL).

**Main result.** The proposed protocol achieves an exponential improvement in sensitivity over unsqueezed cases, maintaining a distinct advantage below the SQL even in noisy environments.

**Method.** The authors propose a streamlined protocol that simultaneously exploits detuned motional squeezing and spin-oscillator entanglement to enhance phase accumulation.

**Model / system.** The study utilizes a trapped-ion spin-oscillator system. The dynamics are described by Hamiltonians involving motional modes (like the Bogoliubov mode $\hat{eta}$) coupled to the spin system ($\hat{\sigma}_z$).

**Key observables.** The weak field signal is mapped onto a measurable spin phase shift, read out via $\langle\hat{\sigma}_yangle_f$. Sensitivity is quantified using the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the squeezing degree ($r$), the detuning ($\Delta_c$), and the interrogation time ($T$). The sensitivity scales with $ar{n}_{\max}$ and the squeezing parameter $r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes $\phi_D = \phi_E = 0$ for simplicity in the core derivation, although the general case is noted.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the single-step protocol in the trapped-ion system. Figure 2 shows Wigner functions of the motional mode, illustrating the evolution of squeezing and enclosed areas.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of surpassing the SQL, introduces the physical system and the combined squeezing/entanglement protocol, derives the enhanced sensitivity scaling, and finally analyzes the robustness of this gain against realistic noise sources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving measurement precision beyond the standard quantum limit (SQL) is a fundamental object in quantum metrology. In this manuscript, we propose a streamlined protocol for weak signal sensing in a trapped-ion spin-oscillator system that simultaneously exploits detuned motional squeezing and spin-oscillator entanglement. By combining these two quantum resources, the phase accumulation in this spin system is significantly accelerated, as a result achieving an exponential improvement in the sensitivity over unsqueezed cases. Remarkably, we show that the significant metrological gain provided by our protocol persists in noisy environments, with the sensitivity remaining below the SQL. In particular, our protocol retains a distinct advantage in the short interrogation time regime, thus demonstrating the practical potential in quantum-enhanced sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a geometric measure, the fidelity distance, to quantify fermionic non-Gaussianity in interacting quantum systems. It develops a scalable, non-perturbative method to compute this distance, providing a rigorous witness that depends only on measurable two- and four-point correlators. This offers a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing strong correlations in models like the Fermi-Hubbard model.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable diagnostic tool for non-Gaussianity in strongly correlated systems, which is crucial for interpreting data from quantum simulators like those based on ultracold atoms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of quantifying fermionic non-Gaussianity, which measures how much an interacting fermionic state deviates from a simple Gaussian state.

**Main result.** They introduce a geometric measure—the fidelity distance—and derive a computable, non-perturbative witness for this non-Gaussianity that depends only on experimentally accessible two- and four-point correlators.

**Method.** The core method involves defining the distance via the fidelity to the manifold of Gaussian states and deriving rigorous, computable bounds using a sharpened operator Jensen inequality.

**Model / system.** The primary model studied is the Fermi-Hubbard model, which describes interacting fermions. The analysis focuses on the properties of the ground state $|\psiangle$ relative to the Gaussian manifold $\mathcal{G}$.

**Key observables.** Fidelity distance $D(ho)$, two- and four-point fermionic correlators ($\mu, 	ext{Var}_\psi(\Gamma)$), and the derived witness bound $D_L$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U/t$), system size ($N$), and the regulator $\epsilon$ used in bounding techniques.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing bounds that are computationally tractable, specifically avoiding full state tomography by restricting measurements to two- and four-point correlators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-Gaussianity $D(|\psiangle)$ vs. $U/t$ for the Fermi-Hubbard chain, and compare various derived bounds ($D_G, D_L, D_{\lambda\max}$) against each other and against the true distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the geometric measure (fidelity distance), then developing an iterative formalism to compute it, and finally deriving rigorous, experimentally accessible bounds using advanced inequalities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interacting fermions owe both their non-trivial correlations and their computational power to their departure from Gaussianity. Yet, quantifying that departure geometrically faces two challenges: the Gaussian manifold is non-convex, and perturbative treatments are confined to weak interactions. Here, we introduce a geometric measure of fermionic non-Gaussianity, the fidelity distance of a target state to the manifold of fermionic Gaussian states. We construct an iteration formalism to numerically access it, applicable also at strong coupling. Further, we prove a sharpened operator Jensen inequality, of interest in its own right. We use it to derive a witness that involves only two- and four-point fermionic correlators. Numerical benchmarks on the one-dimensional Fermi--Hubbard model show that the quality of the witness improves with system size and, for strong coupling, is tighter than antiflatness. Our results open a scalable route to charting non-Gaussianity of strongly-interacting fermionic quantum simulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the observation of number-controllable Majorana zero modes in a $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Te}_3/	ext{FeTe}$ superconducting heterojunction. Using STM spectroscopy, the researchers characterized a unique 'stacked-core' vortex structure derived from both superconducting layers. This establishes the heterostructure as a highly controllable platform for realizing and manipulating topological qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The controlled realization of topologically non-trivial states (MZMs) in a solid-state heterostructure provides a clean, experimentally verifiable platform for studying topological quantum phenomena, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing and controlling Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in solid-state devices for fault-tolerant topological quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors report spectroscopic evidence for number-controllable MZMs in a stoichiometric $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Te}_3/	ext{FeTe}$ heterojunction, establishing a controlled platform for topological superconductivity.

**Method.** Low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopy and spectroscopy (STM/S) were used to probe the superconducting gap, vortex structure, and bound states.

**Model / system.** The system is a stoichiometric one quintuple layer (1QL) $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Te}_3/	ext{FeTe}$ heterojunction, which exhibits interfacial superconductivity and forms a moiré superlattice due to lattice mismatch.

**Key observables.** Spectroscopic evidence of MZMs (zero-bias conductance peaks), stacked vortex core structures, and the ability to tune the number of MZMs via magnetic field and island size.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (0.4 K), perpendicular magnetic field (1.0 T), and the lattice mismatch creating the moiré superlattice.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ability to tune the MZM number is demonstrated by varying the magnetic field and the lateral size of the $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Te}_3$ islands.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the moiré pattern, anisotropic vortex profiles, and spectroscopic signatures showing both dispersive (X-shaped) and discrete (quantized) bound states within the vortex core.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence of the moiré superlattice, characterizes the distinct two-component vortex bound states (one from each layer), and culminates in demonstrating the tunability of the MZM count.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Creation and control of Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in solid devices is the key challenge in realizing fault tolerant topological quantum computation. Here we report spectroscopic evidence for number controllable MZMs in stoichiometric one quintuple layer Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe heterostructures, together with the observation of stacked vortices in superconducting islands. Low temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy and spectroscopy reveal a long period moire superlattice arising from lattice mismatch, which induces nanoscale modulation of superconducting gaps. Magnetic vortices exhibit a stacked core structure with two layer derived bound state branches: a dispersive branch evolving into an X-shaped pattern away from the vortex center, and a quantum-limit branch characterized by a spatially unsplit MZMs together with discrete integer quantized Caroli-de Gennes-Matricon states. Notably, MZMs are observed in all measured vortices, and their number can be tuned by magnetic field and the size of Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$ islands. These results establish stoichiometric 1QL-Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe as a patterned superconducting interface that enables isolated and few-vortex Majorana configurations, providing a controlled platform for fuse MZMs.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces PULSUS, a novel direct frequency-domain framework for calculating multidimensional spectroscopy signals in open quantum systems. It moves beyond the limitations of the impulsive rotating-wave approximation by using Liouville-space resolvents. The key finding is that approximations like RWA and the impulsive limit must be treated independently, as their failure modes are highly dependent on the specific pulse duration and spectral regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, systematic computational tool for analyzing complex quantum optical phenomena, allowing researchers to quantitatively dissect the contributions of different physical approximations in ultrafast spectroscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a direct frequency-domain framework for multidimensional spectroscopy of open quantum systems that accurately surpasses the limitations of the impulsive rotating-wave approximation (RWA).

**Main result.** The framework allows for a controlled comparison of various approximations (RWA, finite-pulse, impulsive), showing that the total error is nonmonotonic with pulse duration, and that the approximations cannot be assumed to improve simultaneously.

**Method.** The authors utilize a Liouville-space representation and calculate the third-order response directly in the frequency domain using the causal Liouvillian resolvent, avoiding time-domain Fourier transforms.

**Model / system.** The system involves open quantum dynamics governed by a Lindblad master equation, incorporating dissipation and dephasing. The light-matter interaction is modeled via an operator-valued coupling term $\mathbf{V}$ applied to the Liouville space evolution.

**Key observables.** Multidimensional coherent spectroscopy signals, specifically the third-order response $M^{(3)}(\omega_3, T, \omega_1)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pulse duration (e.g., 0.10 fs, 20 fs), energy splitting ($J$), and the comparison between the full finite-pulse response and the impulsive-RWA limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the separated-pulse regime, uses Gaussian pulse envelopes, and remains perturbative in the optical field strength.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare pointwise spectral discrepancies between the full finite-pulse response and the impulsive-RWA result across varying pulse durations, highlighting strong discrepancies at longer pulses.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from the general time-domain problem to the Liouville-space formulation, introduces the frequency-domain resolvent, and constructs a controlled hierarchy of approximations (finite-pulse, RWA, short-pulse, impulsive) to achieve direct spectral evaluation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce PULSUS, a direct frequency-domain framework for multidimensional spectroscopy of open quantum systems beyond the impulsive rotating-wave approximation. Coherence-frequency intervals are represented through Liouvillian resolvents, while finite Gaussian pulses are incorporated through operator-valued functions of the field-free Liouvillian. This enables direct evaluation on Cartesian grids or arbitrary spectral coordinates while retaining molecular evolution during the pulse envelopes and, when required, the complete light--matter interaction.   The implementation is validated against analytical and independent time-domain benchmarks and supports targeted spectral sampling that reduces the number of evaluated coordinates when the response is spectrally localized. A controlled hierarchy of finite-pulse, rotating-wave, short-pulse, and impulsive models separates the errors introduced by each approximation. For a coupled electronic dimer, the total impulsive-RWA error is nonmonotonic in pulse duration: an intermediate regime provides the highest accuracy, whereas ultrashort pulses expose coupling-enabled beyond-RWA pathways and longer pulses develop substantial finite-pulse corrections. These results show that the impulsive and rotating-wave approximations cannot be assumed to improve simultaneously and should be tested when quantitatively interpreting multidimensional spectra. PULSUS provides a direct frequency-domain framework for carrying out that comparison.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates quantum-enhanced mechanical sensing by utilizing a microgram-scale acoustic resonator coupled to a qubit. By preparing the mechanical mode in non-Gaussian Fock states, the authors achieved a 7.0 dB improvement in signal sensitivity over classical limits. The use of machine learning to estimate the signal further validates this quantum advantage by minimizing systematic model errors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes quantum metrology into the macroscopic mechanical domain, demonstrating how quantum resources (non-Gaussian states) can enhance sensing beyond classical limits in a solid-state platform.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of pushing quantum measurement sensitivities beyond the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL) for phase-insensitive signals using macroscopic mechanical oscillators.

**Main result.** They demonstrated a 7.0 dB sensitivity gain in signal-amplitude estimation using a microgram-scale acoustic resonator prepared in non-Gaussian Fock states. Furthermore, they showed that a model-free neural network estimator can reproduce this quantum advantage while significantly reducing systematic bias.

**Method.** The methodology combines quantum state preparation (Fock states via optimal control), quantum sensing protocols involving weak resonant drives, and advanced estimation techniques including Bayesian analysis and neural network training.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a high-overtone bulk acoustic wave resonator (HBAR) coupled to a transmon qubit (cQAD device). The sensing relies on preparing the mechanical mode in Fock states, which are then probed by a weak resonant signal.

**Key observables.** Fisher information, signal-amplitude sensitivity gain (7.0 dB), phonon-number distribution $P(\mu)$, and the Hellinger distance $D_H^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** HBAR effective mass ($\approx 16 \mu	ext{g}$), Qubit/Phonon frequencies ($\approx 5.07-5.08 	ext{ GHz}$), and the probe state Fock number ($|kangle$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial estimation relied on a parametrized model, which was overcome by using a model-free neural network estimator. The quantum enhancement peaks for the $|4angle$ state due to state preparation imperfections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device setup and sensing protocol, showing measured phonon-number populations for different Fock states before and after displacement, and comparing the squared Hellinger distance for various displacements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the quantum mechanical model (Hamiltonians), the sensing protocol involving Fock state preparation and weak signal interaction, the theoretical limits using Fisher information, and finally, the experimental demonstration using both model-based and model-free (NN) estimation techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Massive mechanical oscillators couple directly to forces, accelerations and gravity, making them attractive probes of new physics and of quantum mechanics at macroscopic scales. Pushing sensitivities beyond the standard quantum limit for signals of unknown phase requires non-Gaussian quantum states, whose metrological advantage has so far been confined to lighter systems such as trapped ions. Here we operate a microgram-scale acoustic mode of a high-overtone bulk acoustic wave resonator as a phase-insensitive quantum-enhanced sensor. A transmon qubit prepares the mode in Fock states of up to seven phonons by optimal control, and resolves its phonon-number distribution after interaction with a weak resonant signal. Bayesian analysis yields a Fisher information five times the vacuum value, a 7.0 dB gain in signal-amplitude sensitivity over any classical probe state, irrespective of its energy, currently limited by state preparation infidelities. We then train a neural network on the measured sensor response to perform Bayesian estimation without any analytical model of the system, showing the same sensitivity gain with half the systematic bias. Our results establish acoustic resonators prepared in non-Gaussian states as quantum-enhanced probes for high-frequency gravitational waves, dark matter and wave-function collapse.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an efficient protocol for implementing long-distance quantum fan-out gates ($C_n$) essential for quantum networks. By leveraging pre-shared GHZ states, the authors significantly reduce the required gate count compared to conventional methods. They further demonstrate the feasibility of these protocols using advanced linear optical architectures for photonic quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the resource overhead challenge in quantum networking by providing resource-efficient gate implementations, which is crucial for scaling up any quantum computation platform.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the critical need for scalable, long-distance quantum fan-out gates ($C_n$) necessary for distributed quantum computation and quantum networks.

**Main result.** The proposed scheme significantly reduces the required number of CNOT and Hadamard gates compared to standard decomposition methods, and the developed optical architectures achieve higher success probabilities.

**Method.** The authors propose a protocol utilizing a pre-shared $n$-qubit Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state combined with local CNOT gates and classical feed-forward operations.

**Model / system.** The work is modeled abstractly using qubits and then physically realized using linear optics, where qubits are encoded in the polarization states of single photons.

**Key observables.** Gate counts (CNOT, Hadamard), success probabilities, and quantum circuit diagrams for $C_n$ gates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate counts reduction factor of $(n-2)$, photon survival efficiency ($\eta_s$), and detection efficiencies ($\eta_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The feasibility relies on the ability to generate and utilize high-fidelity $n$-qubit GHZ states and the performance of linear optical components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuits for the standard decomposition-based $C_n$ gate and the proposed GHZ-state-based scheme, including schematics for the heralded optical implementation using PBSs.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem of scalable $C_n$ gates, detailing the abstract protocol using GHZ states, and finally presenting two specific, highly optimized optical architectures (heralded and post-selected) for photonic implementation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable long-distance quantum gates are essential for distributed quantum computation and scalable quantum networks. We propose an efficient scheme for implementing an $n$-qubit fan-out gate $C^n$ (also known as a controlled-NOT-$\cdots$-NOT gate) across spatially separated parties. Our protocol requires only a pre-shared $n$-qubit Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger state, $n$ local controlled-NOT (CNOT) gates, and feed-forward operations, thereby reducing both the CNOT and Hadamard gate counts by $(n-2)$ when compared with the standard decomposition-based approach. We further develop two optical architectures to implement the heralded and post-selected $n$-photon fan-out gates, respectively. These schemes require fewer single photons resources and achieve higher success probabilities than their decomposition-based counterparts, representing significant improvements. The results indicate that our approach is experimentally feasible and pave the way toward practical long-distance quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel, feedback-free hybrid quantum algorithm to calculate the ground state energy of the Fermi-Hubbard model. It addresses the mismatch between momentum-space quantum hardware and real-space correlation methods by performing variational optimization classically after quantum measurement. This approach demonstrates high accuracy on quantum hardware while requiring fewer quantum resources than competing methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it proposes a practical, resource-aware quantum algorithm for strongly correlated matter that circumvents the need for complex, iterative quantum feedback loops, making it more amenable to near-term quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is bridging the gap between explicitly correlated methods, which naturally use real-space correlators, and quantum processors, which operate naturally in momentum space, for strongly correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The authors propose a hybrid, feedback-free algorithm that achieves a significantly reduced relative ground-state energy error (down to 3.5% via calibration) on a 32-qubit system, outperforming other hybrid methods.

**Method.** The algorithm uses a quantum processor to prepare a low-entanglement momentum-space MPS, which is then rotated to real space. The crucial step is that the correlator parameters are optimized entirely in classical post-processing, avoiding a quantum-classical feedback loop.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the fully periodic 2D Fermi–Hubbard model, simulated at half-filling, using a Hamiltonian involving hopping ($t$) and on-site repulsion ($U$). The simulation is performed on a 32-qubit quantum processor.

**Key observables.** Relative ground-state energy error ($|E_{ec} - E_{gs}|/|E_{gs}|$), spin correlation observable ($S_{zz}(ho)$), and various expectation values required for energy calculation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi=4$), lattice size ($4 	imes 4$), and the ratio $U/t=4$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The transcorrelated energy used for precomputation is nonvariational, and the final energy minimization relies on classical post-processing of sampled distributions, which are calibrated using low-order moments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow (DMRG $ightarrow$ Quantum $ightarrow$ Classical), compare energy convergence between transcorrelated and explicitly correlated methods, and show the dramatic improvement in energy error when calibration is applied to quantum hardware samples.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the fundamental mismatch in bases. It then details the hybrid algorithm workflow, showing the quantum preparation and classical optimization. Results are presented by comparing the energy convergence of different methods and demonstrating the performance gains on actual quantum hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Explicitly correlated methods, which represent dominant short-range correlations by a diagonal Jastrow-type correlator, are designed to accurately describe systems of strongly correlated electrons, such as the fully periodic 2D Fermi--Hubbard model. However, as the reference state is compact in momentum space whereas the correlator acts naturally in real space, there is a fundamental mismatch of bases that classical algorithms resolve only by restricting the reference or the correlator. Here we propose a hybrid algorithm in which a quantum processor prepares a low-entanglement momentum-space matrix product state (MPS), precomputed classically by the transcorrelated method at the cost of a nonvariational energy, rotates it to real space, and measures in bases fixed by the Hamiltonian. Because the measurement bases are independent of the correlator, the correlator can be variationally optimized entirely in classical post-processing with no quantum-classical feedback loop. We demonstrate our algorithm on 32 qubits of Quantinuum's H2-2 with a bond-dimension-4 MPS for the half-filled $4 \times 4$ lattice and achieve a relative ground-state energy error of $16 \pm 4\%$ after postselection, reduced to $3.5 \pm 0.5\%$ by calibration using low-order moments from the MPS. Compared with other feedback-free hybrid algorithms, even under generous assumptions, our method requires fewer samples than quantum-selected configuration interaction and fewer two-qubit gates than quantum Krylov methods.

</details>

**Summary.** This work calculates the limit free energy for complex multi-species Ising spin glasses, overcoming the limitation of requiring convexity assumptions on the system's interactions. It achieves this by reformulating the problem using variational principles over Brownian martingales and solving associated Hamilton-Jacobi equations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the techniques involving variational representations over continuous paths (Brownian martingales) and the connection to PDEs (Hamilton-Jacobi) are highly relevant to modern theoretical physics, including quantum field theory and open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the limit free energy of mean-field multi-species Ising spin glasses, crucially without assuming the convexity of the Hamiltonian's covariance function.

**Main result.** The limit free energy is given by an 'uninverted' variational formula over Brownian martingales, derived through the solution of a Hamilton-Jacobi equation.

**Method.** The proof involves restricting the problem to fixed magnetization 'slices,' solving the resulting Hamilton-Jacobi equation, and then using convex duality to obtain the final variational representation.

**Model / system.** The system is a multi-species Ising spin glass defined by a Gaussian field $H_N(\sigma)$ with a covariance structure $\xi$ that is not assumed to be convex. External fields $h$ are also included.

**Key observables.** Limit free energy $F_N(t,0)$, enriched free energy $U(1, x; g)$, and the cost functional $A_q$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The limit is taken as $N 	o \infty$. The analysis involves paths $q$ and Brownian martingales $\alpha$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary strength is avoiding the assumption of convexity for the covariance function $\xi$. The derivation relies on showing the convergence of error terms related to the enriched free energy.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure suggests mathematical derivations involving equations for free energy (e.g., Eq. 1.3, 1.11, 1.5).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the result by first analyzing the free energy for fixed magnetization (slices), leading to a Hamilton-Jacobi equation solution, and finally extending this to the full system limit via variational principles over martingales.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify the limit free energy of mean-field multi-species spin glasses with +/-1 spins, allowing for the presence of external fields, and without assuming any convexity property of the covariance function of the Hamiltonian. The limit is given by an "uninverted" variational formula over Brownian martingales. The proof consists in first identifying the limit free energy of configurations with a fixed magnetization. Once the magnetization is fixed, we can show the convergence of the enriched free energy to the solution of a Hamilton-Jacobi equation. The magnetization constraint is also used to show the convexity of the initial condition in this equation, and this yields a variational representation of the solution, as a Hopf formula. Convex duality then turns this formula into an optimization over martingales whose initial values are the prescribed magnetizations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to generate quantum superpositions of ground states from the transverse-field Ising model by coupling it to a central auxiliary spin. By analyzing observables like magnetization, correlations, and the Quantum Fisher Information, the authors confirm that these superpositions span different magnetic phases. The key finding is the observation of Heisenberg scaling in the QFI, suggesting high sensitivity for parameter estimation near critical points.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects quantum metrology (QFI scaling) directly to the physics of quantum phase transitions in integrable models, providing a concrete physical realization for quantum sensing based on many-body ground states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the preparation and characterization of coherent superpositions of ground states of the transverse-field quantum Ising model belonging to different magnetic phases.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrate the preparation of such superpositions and show that the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) exhibits Heisenberg scaling ($L^2$) near critical points.

**Method.** The protocol involves adiabatic evolution of a joint system (Ising chain + central spin) followed by post-selection measurement on the central spin to project the chain into a superposition state.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional transverse-field Ising chain coupled to a central auxiliary spin (spin-1/2 or spin-1). The Hamiltonian involves nearest-neighbor coupling strength promoted to an operator acting on the central spin.

**Key observables.** Magnetization, nearest-neighbor spin correlations, entanglement (concurrence), and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction coupling ($J$), transverse field ($h$), and the superposition parameter ($\delta$). The analysis covers the paramagnetic, ferromagnetic, and anti-ferromagnetic phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The state preparation relies on adiabatic evolution, and certain properties are analyzed in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept of superpositions across three phases, show the dependence of magnetization and correlations on the $J-\delta$ plane, and display the QFI vs. $J$ plot, confirming $L^2$ scaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the model and the state preparation protocol via adiabatic evolution. It then analytically derives expressions for key observables like magnetization and correlations. Finally, it focuses on calculating the QFI, demonstrating its scaling behavior near critical points.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate superpositions of quantum Ising ground states generated through a quantum interaction, in which the nearest-neighbor coupling strength is promoted to an operator acting on a central auxiliary spin. We prepare the central spin in a superposition, which allows the chain to evolve coherently under different effective interaction strengths. The states are prepared by adiabatic evolution of the joint system comprising the central spin and the many-body spin chain, followed by a measurement on the central spin and post-selection of a specific outcome. It is possible to prepare superpositions of transverse-field quantum Ising ground states belonging to the three magnetic phases, namely, the paramagnetic, ferromagnetic, and anti-ferromagnetic phases. For a spin-1/2 central system, it is possible to prepare superpositions of ground states belonging to any two phases, while for the spin-1 case, the superpositions may contain three ground states, each belonging to one of the three phases. We analytically derive expressions for the magnetization, spin correlations, and entanglement of such states. The magnetization and spin correlations show distinct features in different superposition sectors. The nearest-neighbor entanglement is largest where the transverse field and Ising interaction compete, and its behavior differs from that of the magnetic observables. We also derive the quantum Fisher information relevant to estimating the Ising interaction strength. And it exhibits multiple peaks near the critical points of the constituent ground states, with the maximum value showing Heisenberg scaling, for the parameters and system sizes examined.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explores the complex phase transitions of skyrmion structures within a spin-1 Bose-Einstein Condensate. By analyzing the Gross-Pitaevskii equation, the authors identify three distinct soliton phases (BA-skin, AF-skin, normal-core) whose stability is governed by external parameters. The findings advance the understanding of topological defects in magnetic quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed theoretical predictions on complex topological textures (skyrmions) in ultracold atomic systems, linking macroscopic superfluid behavior to microscopic spin interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the skin phase transition of a skyrmion within a ferromagnetic superfluid system.

**Main result.** Three distinct soliton structures—BA-skin, AF-skin, and normal-core—are revealed, and continuous and discontinuous phase transitions between these states are predicted.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical analysis using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equation to estimate transition boundaries, supplemented by numerical solutions of the Gross-Pitaevskii equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1 Bose-Einstein Condensate (BEC) operating in a ferromagnetic regime, whose dynamics are governed by the Gross-Pitaevskii equation.

**Key observables.** The structure of the soliton core (BA-core, AF-core, or normal-core), the spin configuration, and the topological charge of the skyrmion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless parameters $q/\mu$ (quadratic Zeeman ratio) and $c_s/c_n$ (spin/density interaction ratio) control the phase transitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the linear Zeeman coefficient $p=0$ and focuses on the ferromagnetic phase ($c_s < 0$ and $q < 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the order parameter amplitude $f_m(r)$ for different phase structures (BA-skin vs. AF-skin) and show phase diagrams mapping transitions based on $q/\mu$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the three soliton phases, then using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equation to analyze the continuous BA-AF transition, and finally using Landau instability analysis to map out the full phase diagram including the normal-core phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Compared with a scalar Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC), a spinor BEC supports richer phenomena because of its internal spin degrees of freedom, including topological solitons such as domain walls or skyrmions. Here, we theoretically reveal three distinct axisymmetric soliton structures formed as a circular domain wall in a spin-1 BEC, connected within fixed winding numbers. We refer to these structures as the broken-axisymmetry (BA)-skin, antiferromagnetic (AF)-skin and normal-core phases. Their occurrence is controlled by the two dimensionless parameters in the absence of linear Zeeman coefficient $p=0$: $q/μ$, the ratio of the quadratic Zeeman coefficient to the chemical potential, and $c_s/c_n$, the ratio of spin interaction to the density interaction. The BA-skin and AF-skin phases exhibit local easy-plane magnetic order and local easy-plane spin-nematic order, respectively, while the normal-core phase is similar to a doubly quantized vortex analogous to that of a scalar BEC with a density depletion at the center. We obtain semi-analytical estimates of selected transition boundaries separating these phases by analyzing the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equation and also numerically confirm them by solving the Gross-Pitaevskii equation. We estimate the skyrmion radius using a hydrostatic approximation and compare the result with numerical solutions. We envision our results on skyrmions in a spin-1 BEC can trigger further investigation on novel soliton structures in a magnetic superfluid.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes quantum interferometers to show that the extractable work (ergotropy) is not fully characterized by interference visibility. By comparing two experimental setups, the authors demonstrate that the path predictability retained by the setup—a resource inaccessible to simple output measurements—contributes a measurable, non-zero amount to the coherent work. This establishes path information as a distinct, operational thermodynamic resource.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable example where standard quantum coherence measures (like visibility) fail to capture the full thermodynamic resource content, linking quantum foundations (which-path information) directly to thermodynamics (extractable work).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the coherent ergotropy (unitarily extractable work) in quantum interferometers is solely determined by output visibility or if it is sensitive to path predictability inaccessible to standard measurements.

**Main result.** The coherent ergotropy differs between two interferometer setups (QDCE vs. QCRE), showing that path predictability retained by the setup acts as an additional, measurable thermodynamic resource beyond what is revealed by interference visibility.

**Method.** The authors compare the coherent ergotropy extracted from the output battery for two specific Mach-Zehnder interferometer arrangements, analyzing the difference using path predictability and Landauer-bound related costs.

**Model / system.** The system is a Mach-Zehnder interferometer (MZI) using a two-level system (TLS) as both a quantum battery and a which-path probe. The analysis compares two protocols: the Quantum Delayed-Choice Experiment (QDCE) and the Quantum-Controlled Reality Experiment (QCRE).

**Key observables.** Coherent ergotropy ($R_{	ext{coh}}$), Interference Visibility ($V$), Path Predictability ($P$), and Which-Way Distinguishability ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The path predictability ($P$) and the geometric overlap parameter ($
u$) are crucial, with the analysis examining non-relativistic and relativistic limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes $\hbar=1$ and relates the work advantage to the Landauer bound on the cost of locally resetting the beam-splitter controller.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the two MZI setups being compared: (a) QDCE (fixed first BS, controlled second BS) and (b) QCRE (controlled first BS, fixed second BS).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by defining ergotropy and comparing the QDCE and QCRE protocols. It derives the observables for both setups, analyzes the difference in coherent ergotropy, and extends the findings to a relativistic realization involving spin-path correlation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The ergotropy, or unitarily extractable work, of a quantum system naturally splits into incoherent and coherent contributions, respectively set by population inversion and coherences in the energy eigenbasis. We show that this split makes the coherent ergotropy of a Mach--Zehnder qubit sensitive to path predictability that the output interference fringes do not reveal. We send the path qubit, prepared as a thermal quantum battery, through two quantum-controlled interferometers built from the same elements, a fixed beam splitter and a beam splitter coherently controlled to be present or absent, differing only in the order in which these two act. The two arrangements share identical visibility, stored energy, and incoherent ergotropy at every point in the parameter space. Yet, for work extracted from the output battery alone, placing the controlled beam splitter first never yields less coherent ergotropy, and at fixed visibility and interferometric phase the difference is governed by the path predictability that this ordering creates. The same expression reappears as a purity deficit on the beam-splitter controller, so the work advantage comes with a Landauer bound on the cost of locally resetting the controller that is never larger at the same temperature. We further examine the effects of decoherence on the battery and consider a relativistic realization in which a Wigner rotation correlates the particle's spin with its path. Tracing out the spin decoheres the battery and allows the two arrangements to exhibit different coherent ergotropies even in regimes where their non-relativistic counterparts coincide. These results show that interference visibility is not a complete measure of the coherence available as work.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces WannierSTM, a novel, efficient computational framework for simulating superconducting STM spectroscopy. It merges first-principles electronic structure methods using Wannier functions with the NEGF formalism in the Nambu basis. This allows for rapid, closed-form calculation of transport properties in complex superconducting nanostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, efficient, and unified computational tool for studying superconducting quantum transport, which is highly relevant for understanding molecular electronics and superconducting quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to bridge the gap between first-principles electronic structure calculations and efficient simulations of superconducting scanning tunneling microscopy (STM). It seeks a computationally tractable method to model complex quantum transport phenomena in nanoscale superconducting hybrid structures.

**Main result.** The authors developed WannierSTM, a Python-based framework that calculates transport quantities like $I-V$ and $dI/dV$ in a closed analytical form, enabling rapid spectroscopy over broad parameter spaces.

**Method.** The method combines maximally localized Wannier functions (MLWF) derived from DFT with the nonequilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism, incorporating superconductivity via the Nambu-spinor formalism.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a superconducting hybrid nanostructure, treated as a central region coupled to STM tip and substrate electrodes. The Hamiltonian is formulated in the Nambu basis to account for pairing potentials ($\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Current-voltage characteristics ($I-V$) and differential conductance ($dI/dV$) spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting pairing potential ($\Delta$), spin-orbit coupling, magnetic textures, and the bias voltage ($V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electrode coupling is treated using the wide-band limit approximation (WBLA), and superconductivity is modeled at a mean-field level without self-consistent feedback from nonequilibrium currents.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes imply figures demonstrate the schematic transport setup and results for various superconducting hybrid nanostructures.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds theoretically by establishing the Hamiltonian in the Nambu formalism, applying the NEGF framework with the WBLA, and deriving a computationally efficient Landauer-Büttiker-like formula for current calculation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a Python-based program that combines maximally localized Wannier functions-based first-principles electronic structure with the nonequilibrium Green's function formalism to enable efficient simulations of scanning tunneling microscopy (STM). The workflow builds upon standard density functional theory packages to construct compact transport Hamiltonians that preserve the properties of realistic materials. Superconductivity is incorporated via an effective pairing potential within the Nambu-spinor formalism, while coupling to the STM tip and substrate electrodes is treated using the wide-band limit approximation. Within this formulation, transport quantities such as current-voltage characteristics and differential conductance are evaluated in a closed analytical form. This avoids explicit numerical energy integrations and enables rapid spectroscopic calculations over broad parameter spaces. The framework provides a unified and computationally efficient approach for modeling superconducting hybrid nanostructures with spin-orbit coupling, magnetic textures, and spin polarization. Representative applications in spin chains, two-dimensional and molecular magnets on a superconductor demonstrate its robust capabilities for both simple model systems and realistic ab initio molecular structures relevant to modern STM experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how long-range dipolar interactions affect the dynamics of bosons in a 1D lattice, revealing a non-monotonic behavior. The dipolar tail first enhances delocalization at moderate interaction strengths by coupling different system states, but at stronger interactions, it causes localization by splitting energy degeneracies. This competition defines a critical crossover scale relevant for experiments with magnetic atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how long-range, anisotropic interactions can lead to complex, non-monotonic dynamics in quantum many-body systems, which is highly relevant for controlling quantum transport in engineered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the long-range dipolar tail of interactions modifies the dynamics of strongly interacting hard-core bosons in a 1D lattice, focusing on the competition between delocalization and localization.

**Main result.** The dipolar tail exhibits a non-monotonic effect, first enhancing delocalization at intermediate interaction strengths ($V/t$) by sustaining inter-manifold hybridization, but then promoting localization at larger $V/t$ by lifting degeneracies within manifolds.

**Method.** The study uses real-time dynamics simulations and analyzes various diagnostics like time-averaged density inhomogeneity ($ar{\eta}$) and the Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR) to quantify localization and delocalization.

**Model / system.** The system is hard-core bosons on an open 1D lattice governed by a Hamiltonian including nearest-neighbor and long-range dipolar interactions ($V_r \propto 1/r^3$). The analysis compares this to a simple nearest-neighbor model.

**Key observables.** Time-averaged density inhomogeneity ($ar{\eta}$), Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and the crossover strength $(V/t)^*$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction-to-tunnelling ratio ($V/t$) is the primary varying parameter, with intermediate and large $V/t$ defining distinct physical regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the dynamics onto manifolds defined by the number of nearest-neighbor bonds ($N_{\mathrm{NN}}$) and considering the effect of transverse confinement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crossover of $ar{\eta}$ vs. $V/t$ for different interaction models, showing the transition from delocalizing to localizing behavior, and also plot IPR and hybridization measures ($\Delta\sigma_{NNN}$) across the parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian, defines key diagnostics ($ar{\eta}$, IPR), and then explores the non-monotonic dependence of the dynamics on $V/t$, detailing the mechanisms of inter-manifold hybridization (delocalizing) versus intra-manifold degeneracy lifting (localizing).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how the long-range tail of dipole-dipole interactions modifies the dynamics of strongly interacting hard-core bosons in a one-dimensional lattice. In the nearest-neighbour model, large interactions generate an emergent Hilbert-space fragmentation into manifolds labelled by the number of nearest-neighbour bonds \(N_{\mathrm{NN}}\). The dipolar tail has a non-monotonic effect on this fragmented dynamics as the interaction-to-tunnelling ratio \(V/t\) is increased. At intermediate \(V/t\), it enhances delocalisation relative to the nearest-neighbour model by reducing the energetic detuning between neighbouring \(N_{\mathrm{NN}}\) manifolds and sustaining their hybridisation by hopping. At larger \(V/t\), the same tail lifts degeneracies within each manifold, beginning with the splitting associated with the next-nearest-neighbour bond number \(N_{\mathrm{NNN}}\), and promotes intra-manifold localisation and Hilbert-space shattering. The competition between these two effects produces a crossover from a delocalising to a localising role of the dipolar tail, visible in the late-time spatial inhomogeneity. This crossover occurs for nearly all initial states considered and persists at different fillings. Transverse confinement strengthens the short-range part of the dipolar tail and shifts the crossover to lower \(V/t\), bringing it into the regime relevant to current experiments with magnetic atoms and polar molecules.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses molecular dynamics simulations of a 2D fluid to test global thermodynamics in the presence of heat conduction and gravity. It shows that the phase transition point shifts from zero gravity to a finite effective gravity, confirming the predictive power of global thermodynamics for non-equilibrium structure formation. The methodology successfully maps non-equilibrium behavior onto an effective thermodynamic parameter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous demonstration of how global thermodynamic principles can predict non-equilibrium structural changes in classical many-body systems, which has parallels in understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tests the predictive power of global thermodynamics in determining the equilibrium configuration of a fluid undergoing phase separation when subjected to weak heat conduction and gravity.

**Main result.** The molecular dynamics simulations confirm that the configurational transition shifts from zero gravity ($g=0$) to a finite effective gravity ($	ext{g}_{	ext{eff}} > 0$) under heat conduction, validating the global thermodynamic prediction.

**Method.** The study uses classical molecular dynamics simulations of a 2D Lennard-Jones fluid, comparing results obtained under equilibrium conditions ($\Delta T=0$) versus non-equilibrium heat conduction ($\Delta T>0$).

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D Lennard-Jones fluid confined between two walls maintained at different temperatures ($T_1$ and $T_2$). The dynamics are governed by the Lennard-Jones potential and include external gravitational forces.

**Key observables.** Configurational order parameter ($\langle X angle/L$), effective gravity ($	ext{g}_{	ext{eff}}$), and the saturation-pressure difference ($\Delta p_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak heat conduction ($\Delta T$), weak gravity ($g$), and the ratio of pressure differences ($\Delta p_s / (ar{ho}L)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is limited to weak heat conduction and weak gravity, where higher-order corrections are negligible.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the shift of the liquid-gas transition point from $g=0$ to $	ext{g}_{	ext{eff}} > 0$ under heat conduction, and demonstrate that the hysteretic response also aligns when plotted against $	ext{g}_{	ext{eff}}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework linking global thermodynamics to effective gravity, simulates the 2D Lennard-Jones fluid under varying gravity and temperature gradients, and compares the resulting phase transition shifts and hysteresis loops against the theoretical predictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We test the effective-gravity prediction of global thermodynamics using standard molecular dynamics of a two-dimensional Lennard-Jones fluid. Under weak heat conduction with the lower boundary hotter than the upper boundary, the transition between liquid below gas and liquid above gas shifts from zero to finite downward gravity. Using independently measured equilibrium saturation pressures, we calculate the shift predicted by global thermodynamics and find agreement with the observed transition shift. The transition occurs at zero effective gravity, where the hydrostatic pressure difference balances the saturation-pressure difference between the two boundaries. The hysteresis response under heat conduction also agrees with the equilibrium response when plotted against effective gravity. These results establish the predictive power of global thermodynamics for nonequilibrium structure formation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory showing that quantum energy level crossings act as optimal 'hot spots' for multi-parameter quantum sensing. By analyzing the QFIM and using unitary quench dynamics combined with Bayesian estimation, the authors demonstrate a protocol that achieves the theoretical maximum precision limit (Holevo bound) without needing complex entanglement. This offers a scalable pathway for high-precision quantum measurements across various physical platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, generalizable methodology for optimizing quantum sensors by identifying specific Hamiltonian structures (level crossings) that maximize sensitivity, which is highly relevant for designing practical quantum measurement protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study establishes a theoretical framework connecting the geometric structure of energy level crossings in quantum Hamiltonians to the ultimate precision limits achievable in multi-parameter quantum metrology.

**Main result.** Level crossings are identified as universal operating points for high-precision quantum sensing, allowing estimation precision to saturate the Holevo Cramér–Rao bound using resource-efficient unitary quench dynamics.

**Method.** The authors employ the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) and the Holevo bound to quantify estimation limits, developing a resource-efficient sensing protocol based on unitary quench dynamics and Bayesian estimation.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses a minimal two-parameter Hamiltonian, exemplified by a two-level system and a two-spin Ising model, whose parameters are varied to induce level crossings or avoided crossings.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), Classical Fisher Information Matrix (CFIM), estimation error variance ($	ext{var}(s) + 	ext{var}(a)$), and the Holevo bound.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters defining the Hamiltonian (e.g., $s$ and $a$ in the two-level model; $B_z$ and $B_x$ in the Ising model) and the crossing point coordinates ($s_c, a_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations near the level crossing point, and the initial state is assumed to be pure for the Holevo bound calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy eigenvalues near crossings (Fig. 1), and heatmaps show the enhancement of QFI components ($Q_{ss}, Q_{aa}$) near the crossing point, confirming the failure of the ground state alone.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general problem, analyzing the two-level model using QFIM, demonstrating the failure of the ground state, and finally proposing and validating a resource-efficient protocol using unitary dynamics and Bayesian feedback, confirmed by matching the Holevo bound to the CFI.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Level crossings, where two quantum energy levels intersect, are ubiquitous in quantum systems and play a central role in controlling quantum dynamics. In this study, we establish a framework that connects these level crossing structures to the ultimate precision limits of quantum metrology. By analyzing a Hamiltonian with the level crossings structure, we identify the conditions under which multiple parameters can be estimated simultaneously with maximal sensitivity near the level crossing point. We then construct a resource efficient sensing protocol based on unitary quench dynamics and Bayesian estimation, enabling high precision, low cost parameter estimation without requiring entanglement or complex control. We present a protocol whose estimation precision saturates the Holevo Cramer Rao bound, the tightest achievable precision bound in multi parameter estimation. Using the two level model and the two spin Ising model as illustrative examples, we demonstrate how the protocol can be applied to a system with level crossing Hamiltonian structure. Our findings reveal that level crossings serve as universal operating points for precision quantum metrology, offering a simple and scalable path toward practical multi parameter sensing across diverse quantum platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper maps out the complex phase diagram of a rotating Bose-Einstein condensate confined in a hard-wall potential. By varying the rotational frequency and interaction strength, the authors predict transitions between multiple quantized vortex states, vortex lattices, and giant vortices. This work provides a unified theoretical framework for understanding superfluid behavior in rotating quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a comprehensive phase diagram for a fundamental quantum system (BEC) under rotation, connecting mean-field theory to observable vortex structures and phase transitions, which is highly relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the rotational properties and resulting quantum phases of a zero-temperature Bose-Einstein condensate confined within a hard-wall potential.

**Main result.** The system exhibits multiple distinct phases—including vortex lattices, central holes, and giant vortices—whose stability depends critically on the interplay between the rotational frequency ($\Omega$) and the interaction coupling constant ($g$).

**Method.** The analysis employs the mean-field approximation, reducing the problem to a quasi-two-dimensional effective Hamiltonian, and mapping out the phase diagram by minimizing the energy functional.

**Model / system.** The system is a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) confined in a hard-wall potential, analyzed in the zero-temperature limit. The dynamics are governed by an energy functional incorporating kinetic, interaction, and rotational energy terms.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the condensate density profile, the winding number (quantization), and the identification of distinct quantum phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the angular velocity ($\Omega$) and the dimensionless coupling constant ($g$), which define the phase diagram.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes zero temperature, utilizes the mean-field approximation, and employs a quasi-two-dimensional reduction due to tight axial confinement.

**Figures summary.** The paper presents a phase diagram ($\Omega$ vs. $g$) illustrating seven distinct phases, with solid and dotted lines marking discontinuous and continuous phase transitions, respectively.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the parameter space ($\Omega$ and $g$), detailing transitions from multiply-quantized vortex states to mixed states, and then to vortex lattices, central holes, or giant vortices depending on the coupling strength.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the rotational properties of a zero-temperature Bose-Einstein condensed gas, which is confined in a hard-wall potential. For weak interactions the gas carries its angular momentum via vortices of multiple quantization. As the coupling increases and for sufficiently slow rotational frequencies of the trap, the multiply-quantized vortex states become unstable against a mixed state. For stronger couplings, as the angular frequency of the trap increases, there is a transition from a vortex lattice, to a vortex lattice with a hole at the trap center, and finally to a giant vortex. For weak effective attractive interactions we still have vortices of multiple quantization. These states become unstable towards states which break the axial symmetry of the problem and resemble center-of-mass excitation, as the coupling becomes more negative. Finally, for even more attractive forces, the system becomes unstable against collapse.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes social dilemmas on hypergraphs to determine if complex, multi-player interactions are necessary for emergent critical behavior. By deriving a mesoscopic Langevin equation, the authors show that a simplified, strictly pairwise model can reproduce the critical exponents and scaling laws of the full higher-order game. This suggests that the critical physics is governed by pairwise dynamics, regardless of the complexity of the underlying group structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a powerful demonstration of universality in complex systems, showing that the critical behavior of high-order interactions can be captured by lower-order, pairwise descriptions, which is a key concept in statistical physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether emergent phenomena observed in complex, higher-order social dilemmas (on hypergraphs) can be accurately reproduced by simpler, strictly pairwise interaction dynamics.

**Main result.** The critical behavior (e.g., directed percolation class, critical exponents) of the higher-order games is shown to be equivalent to that of the pairwise theory, suggesting group interactions are not necessary for determining critical scaling.

**Method.** The authors derive a mesoscopic Langevin equation for the dynamics and compare its operator content and critical exponents derived from both the full hypergraph model and a constructed pairwise model.

**Model / system.** The model involves players adopting Cooperation or Defection in social dilemmas, ranging from pairwise (2-player) to three-player (3-player) games played on hypergraphs or lattices. The dynamics are governed by payoff differences leading to an effective Langevin equation.

**Key observables.** Order parameter (cooperator density $ho$), critical exponents ($\delta_s, 	heta, 2/z$), and the location of phase transition lines (Directed-Percolation line, Tricritical Point).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sucker payoff ($S$), fraction of 3-games ($\delta$), and the dimensionality of the lattice (e.g., 2D triangular lattice).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the higher-order dynamics can be mapped onto an effective pairwise dynamics with short-lived memory, and that lattice correlations are irrelevant at the DP fixed point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing the DP, TDP, and first-order regions, and later figures show the collapse of density decay curves and the matching of critical exponents between the two dynamics on a triangular lattice.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mean-field description for higher-order dilemmas, deriving the Langevin equation. It then constructs a strictly pairwise model that reproduces the necessary coefficients. Finally, it compares the critical scaling and dynamics (including 2D lattice simulations) between the two models to demonstrate equivalence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiplayer social dilemmas on hypergraphs show a variety of emergent phenomena, which has been attributed to group interactions. Here we test whether pairwise dynamics can reproduce them as well. We derive the mesoscopic Langevin equation of a testbed model and find the operator content of absorbing phase transitions: a directed-percolation line, a tricritical point, and a first-order region whose spinodal is the explosive transition. In our analysis, three-player games only set the values of the Landau coefficients. We construct a strictly pairwise model with short-lived memory, in which every event involves at most two entities, that reproduces these coefficients term by term and has exactly the same mean-field fixed points. Simulations confirm that the two dynamics share mean-field critical and tricritical scaling and the size of the critical mass, and that a purely three-player game on a two-dimensional triangular lattice lies in the directed-percolation class. For these games, the explosive transition and the critical mass therefore do not require group interactions, and the critical behavior is that of the pairwise theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the emergent geometry of paths generated by gauge-equivariant Graph Neural Networks operating on lattice gauge theories. It finds a crucial separation where the total path length can grow exponentially while the physical spatial extent grows much more slowly. This suggests that deep network layers encode complex, highly redundant information within a relatively compact physical region.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of path complexity diverging from simple spatial metrics is relevant to understanding entanglement structure or correlation functions in many-body systems where local interactions can lead to highly complex, non-local effective descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To understand the geometric structure of paths and loops generated by gauge-equivariant Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) through local message passing, which is not fully captured by the simple receptive field.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals a separation between the rapid, exponential growth of the total path length (contour length) and the much slower growth of the actual spatial extent of the generated loops.

**Method.** The authors analyze the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks generated by recursive node- and edge-feature operations within the GNN framework, independent of network training.

**Model / system.** The model is based on lattice gauge configurations, relevant to non-Abelian gauge theories like QCD and condensed-matter systems such as quantum spin liquids. The GNN operates on a lattice structure using gauge-equivariant message passing.

**Key observables.** Contour length ($\ell$), Radius of Gyration ($	ext{Rg}$), Number of Distinct Visited Sites ($N_{	ext{distinct sites}}$), and Effective Dimension ($D_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network depth ($n$), spatial dimension ($d=2, 3, 4$), and the gauge link variables $U_{ij} \in G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the path generation process retains all ordered links, including immediately retraced ones, for accurate statistical counting.

**Figures summary.** Figure 8 plots geometric observables ($	ext{Rg}, N_{	ext{distinct sites}}, D_{	ext{eff}}$) against the total loop length ($\ell$) for various dimensions and recursion depths, visually confirming that length increases through repeated traversals within a slowly expanding spatial region.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the GNN mechanism for generating gauge-invariant loops, then analyzes the statistical properties of these paths by comparing contour length to spatial metrics, culminating in a physical interpretation via fermionic observable loop expansions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge-equivariant graph neural networks generate ordered products of gauge links through local message passing, but the geometry of these internal structures is not captured by the receptive field alone. We investigate the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks generated by recursive node- and edge-feature operations, independently of network training. Analytical and numerical results reveal a separation between rapid growth of contour length and much slower spatial expansion: deeper compositions generate increasingly long paths through repeated traversal of relatively compact regions. Loop expansions of fermionic observables provide a physical interpretation of this hierarchy, showing how spatial locality remains compatible with high-order dependence on nearby gauge fields. These findings identify complementary roles of network depth in extending spatial reach and enriching the representations constructed within that reach, providing a geometrical perspective on learning lattice gauge observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives a rigorous lower bound on the sample complexity required for coherent classical shadows protocols to estimate quantum state properties. The bound depends on the state rank, the number of observables, and the desired accuracy. By matching known upper bounds, the result establishes the fundamental limits on resource scaling for quantum state estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental, quantitative bounds on the resources (number of copies) needed for state estimation protocols, which is crucial for designing practical, resource-efficient quantum measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the optimal lower bound on the sample complexity ($T$) required for coherent classical shadows protocols to estimate expectation values of observables for an unknown quantum state.

**Main result.** A lower bound for the sample complexity is established as $T = \Omega \left( \min \left( rac{\sqrt{rF\ell}}{\varepsilon}, rac{(F\ell)^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}} ight) + rac{\ell}{\varepsilon^2} ight)$, which nearly matches known upper bounds.

**Method.** The proof uses techniques inspired by the Boolean Hidden Matching problem, involving a Gaussian-lift noise kernel and controlling the distinguishing advantage in an auxiliary game.

**Model / system.** The system involves an unknown quantum state $ho$ on a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space with a maximum rank $r$. The estimation is performed using $T$ copies of this state and an arbitrary joint measurement (POVM).

**Key observables.** The observables $O_i$ are constrained by the operator norm ($\le 1$) and a centered squared Frobenius norm ($\le F$). The goal is to estimate expectation values of these observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the state rank ($r$), the number of observables ($M$), the accuracy ($\varepsilon$), the failure probability ($\delta$), and the derived parameter $\ell = \max\{\log(2M), \log(1/\delta)\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The lower bound holds under the mild assumption $\log(2M) \lesssim d \min\{r, F\}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the problem setup, reviews related work on tomography and estimation, and provides a technical overview of the proof strategy, including parameter reduction and source/query-side bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study coherent classical shadows protocols for an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state of rank at most $r$, allowing an arbitrary joint measurement on all available copies. For observables with operator norm at most one and centered squared Frobenius norm at most $F$, we study the minumum number of copies $T$ needed for the protocol to successed with propbability $1 - δ$. We prove a lower bound on $T$, that depends both on $δ$ and the number $M$ of post-measurement observables. Denoting $\ell=\max\{\log(2M),\log(1/δ)\}$, we show that $T=Ω\left(\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{rF\ell}}{\varepsilon},\frac{(F\ell)^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}\right\}+\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon^2}\right)$ under the mild assumption $\log(2M)\lesssim d\min\{r,F\}$. The lower bound nearly matches the known upper bound and matches the conjectural strengthening of the known upper bound.   The proof is inspired by the framework of the proof of the lower bound for Boolean Hidden Matching. We develop a Gaussian-lift noise kernel that transfers forward and reverse Gaussian hypercontractivity to the Grassmannian, allowing us to bound the distinguishing advantage in an auxiliary game with a revealed query observable. We use Schur--Weyl structure and posterior moment bounds to control arbitrary coherent measurements on the source side.

</details>
