**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how Orbital Angular Momentum is transferred and limited when electron beams diffract through a fork-grating structure. By incorporating the effects of an electromagnetic self-field, the authors show that while the mean OAM is topologically protected, the beam's transverse purity is limited by the initial transverse momentum spread. The methodology combines relativistic quantum mechanics with advanced diffraction theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed theoretical framework for quantifying fundamental quantum beam properties (OAM) in structured matter-wave optics, connecting topological invariants to measurable beam quality metrics like purity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the transfer of Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) and the resulting beam-quality limits when electron vortex beams diffract through a fork-grating structure.

**Main result.** The mean OAM ($\langle L_z angle_m$) is robustly fixed by the topological rule ($m\hbar$), while the beam's transverse purity is determined by the residual transverse momentum spread ($\Delta L_{z,m}$).

**Method.** The analysis uses a paraxial approximation, incorporating an electromagnetic Hartree self-field phase screen into the Fresnel propagation kernel to model the diffraction process.

**Model / system.** The system involves an electron ensemble propagating through a region where it accumulates a Hartree self-field phase before diffracting through a holographic fork-grating. The description uses a modified Schrödinger-like equation derived from relativistic considerations.

**Key observables.** Mean OAM ($\langle L_z angle_m$), Transverse Purity ($P_{\perp,m}$), and OAM Variance ($\Delta L_{z,m}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The residual transverse momentum spread ($\Delta L_{z,m}$), the grating period ($D$), and the accumulated Hartree phase ($	ilde{\chi}_{em}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the quasistatic electromagnetic Hartree approximation and assumes a phase-screen model for propagation, neglecting higher-order self-field corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of the fork-grating setup, including the compensation of deterministic transverse momentum, and show the displacement of the single-component beam.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general description of the electron ensemble and the Hartree potential, derives the order-resolved field using Bessel functions, and finally calculates the OAM variance using Hankel transforms and closure relations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze fork-grating generation of electron vortex beams beyond the ideal single-mode approximation. The incident beam is described as a paraxial Gaussian ensemble with finite transverse momentum spread and a first electromagnetic Hartree phase accumulated before the grating. Within Fresnel propagation we derive an analytical expression for each diffraction order and determine the corresponding orbital-angular-momentum statistics. The mean OAM of a selected order remains fixed by the topological rule, $\langle L_z\rangle_m=mp\hbar$, while its variance is set by the residual transverse momentum spread of the beam prepared at the grating. For the Gaussian mixture, the transverse purity is $\mathcal P_{\perp,m}=[1+2(ΔL_{z,m}/\hbar)^2]^{-1}$. The observed annular profile is governed by a separate propagation-dependent blurring scale, so a beam can retain well-defined mean OAM while having low transverse purity or a partially filled vortex minimum. Within the radial phase-screen approximation, the Hartree self-field modifies the propagated profile while preserving the full-plane OAM moments and transverse purity.

</details>

</figure>

**Summary.** This work studies the extractable work (ergotropy) of entangled quantum states (GHZ and W) near a black hole horizon. It finds that the way this work is lost or recovered depends critically on the initial entanglement structure and the local gravitational environment. The results establish a thermodynamic signature distinguishing different types of quantum correlations in curved spacetime.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete example of how fundamental quantum resources (entanglement) interact with gravitational thermodynamics, offering a measurable, non-equilibrium signature in curved spacetime.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the extractable work (ergotropy) of multipartite entangled states is redistributed in the quantum atmosphere of a Schwarzschild black hole due to Hawking-induced thermalization.

**Main result.** The ergotropy structures for GHZ and W states are qualitatively different, and the work recovery is governed by a competition between the Hartle-Hawking constant and the radial distance.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum thermodynamics applied to curved spacetime, calculating ergotropy based on the energy spectrum of reduced density matrices derived from the Dirac field quantization.

**Model / system.** The system models tripartite GHZ and W entangled states evolving in the quantum atmosphere near a Schwarzschild black hole, incorporating gravitational thermal corrections.

**Key observables.** Ergotropy ($E(ho)$), which quantifies the maximum extractable work.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hartle-Hawking constant ($D_{HH}$), normalized radial distance ($r/r_h$), and the structure of the initial entanglement (GHZ vs. W).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the Hartle-Hawking vacuum, and the reduced states are constructed by tracing out complementary modes near the horizon.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of ergotropy for both GHZ and W states as a function of radial position, showing characteristic peaks, dips, and recovery trends.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the problem and defining ergotropy, reviewing the curved spacetime quantization, and then analyzing the specific mathematical forms and radial dependencies of the ergotropy for GHZ and W states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the ergotropy of tripartite Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) and W states in the quantum atmosphere of a Schwarzschild black hole, focusing on how the extractable work is redistributed under Hawking-induced thermalization. We find that the two classes of multipartite entanglement exhibit qualitatively different ergotropy structures. For GHZ states, the ergotropies associated with different accessible subsystems organize into two degenerate families, whereas for W states they obey a linear averaging relation with no counterpart in the GHZ case. Under symmetric gravitational thermal corrections, the two GHZ families remain separated by a constant energy gap that is independent of the radial position. We further show that the Hartle-Hawking constant $D_{HH}$ and the normalized radial distance $r/r_h$ play competing roles in the evolution of extractable work: increasing $D_{HH}$ suppresses its recovery toward the asymptotic Hawking limit, while increasing $r/r_h$ enhances the ergotropy accessible to the observers. These results show that the redistribution of extractable work in a black-hole environment is governed not only by gravitationally induced thermalization but also by the structure of the underlying multipartite entanglement, providing a thermodynamic signature that distinguishes GHZ- and W-type quantum correlations in curved spacetime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses half-polarized neutron scattering on a hexaferrite to investigate spin-lattice coupling in an incommensurate magnet. The researchers demonstrate the existence of a field-induced, phase-coherent longitudinal lattice modulation locked to the magnetic modulation at the fundamental wavevector. This observation challenges conventional symmetry rules and reveals a fundamental magnetoelastic coupling mechanism in the material.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental, the detailed analysis of symmetry-forbidden coupling mechanisms and the use of polarization-sensitive scattering techniques provide rigorous physical constraints on the coupling terms ($\mathbf{M}_0 \mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{q}}$) relevant to emergent order parameters in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin-lattice coupling in incommensurate magnets, specifically addressing the observation of lattice distortions at the fundamental magnetic wavevector where symmetry rules typically forbid them.

**Main result.** The Y-type hexaferrite hosts a phase-coherent magnetic and longitudinal lattice modulation at the fundamental wavevector in its high-field state, evidenced by a large, persistent nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry.

**Method.** Half-polarized neutron scattering was employed to measure the nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry ($r$) at conjugate satellite positions, providing a sensitive probe of coupled magnetic and structural order.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Y-type hexaferrite $	ext{Ba}_{0.4}	ext{Sr}_{1.6}	ext{Mg}_2	ext{Fe}_{12}	ext{O}_{22}$. The analysis focuses on the magnetoelastic coupling mechanism enabled by a uniform magnetic moment ($\mathbf{M}_0$) in the presence of an incommensurate magnetic modulation ($\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{q}}$).

**Key observables.** The normalized asymmetry $r$, the field dependence of satellite intensities ($	ext{k}_0, 	ext{k}_2, 	ext{k}_3$), and the temperature dependence of the modulation amplitude and phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurements are performed in the saturated high-field state, with key observations persisting up to 275 K and requiring fields above $\approx 2 	ext{ T}$ to resolve the $	ext{k}_3$ satellite.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the interpretation of the asymmetry parameter $r$ to isolate the nuclear-magnetic interference term, which confirms the coupling between the magnetic modulation and an internal lattice distortion.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the field evolution of scattering intensities across different wavevectors ($	ext{k}_0, 	ext{k}_2, 	ext{k}_3$), and show the temperature and field dependence of the $\pm	ext{k}_3$ pair asymmetry parameters, confirming the sign consistency of the interference signal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical context regarding symmetry constraints on structural response in incommensurate magnets. It then presents neutron scattering data showing the appearance and persistence of the $	ext{k}_3$ satellite. The core of the work uses polarization analysis to confirm that the observed signal is due to a field-enabled, phase-coherent coupling between the magnetic and lattice modulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-driven multiferroics owe their electric polarization to lattice distortions that follow the magnetic order, yet in incommensurate magnets symmetry normally forbids such a distortion at the fundamental magnetic wavevector, and the distortions themselves are rarely observed. Here we use half-polarized neutron scattering to show that the Y-type hexaferrite Ba$_{0.4}$Sr$_{1.6}$Mg$_2$Fe$_{12}$O$_{22}$ hosts, in its saturated high-field state, a magnetic modulation and a longitudinal lattice modulation at one and the same wavevector, locked in phase. The signature is a large nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry, $r$ ~ 0.8, of the same sign at the two conjugate satellites and persisting to 275 K. The zone contrast and the field dependence of the satellite indicate an internal, field-induced longitudinal distortion. The modulated state coexists with the transverse-conical magnetoelectric phase and outlives it, revealing a fundamental-wavevector spin-lattice coupling in a magnetic field regime previously regarded as a simple ferrimagnet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper determines the exact altermagnetic ground state of a Heisenberg model on the Ammann-Beenker quasicrystal. The authors find that the magnetic order locks into a coplanar, canted state with specific spin-group symmetry. This magnetic structure is then experimentally verified by calculating spin-resolved electronic transport, which exhibits characteristic g-wave symmetry.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, geometric realization of altermagnetism in a non-periodic system, linking the exact magnetic ground state structure to measurable quantum transport signatures (g-wave symmetry).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to identify and characterize the exact altermagnetic ground states of a classical Heisenberg model defined on the geometrically complex Ammann-Beenker quasicrystal tiling.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a coplanar altermagnetic order characterized by an orthogonal locking of the two primary ordering axes ($\phi_{AB} = \pi/2$), which is confirmed by spin-resolved transport measurements showing g-wave symmetry.

**Method.** The authors combine analytical energy minimization techniques with detailed electronic structure calculations using the Green's function formalism to probe spin-resolved transport.

**Model / system.** The system is a classical Heisenberg spin model on the Ammann-Beenker tiling, which is derived from projecting a 4D hypercubic lattice. The model is analyzed by coupling the spins to itinerant electrons via an onsite exchange interaction.

**Key observables.** Spin-resolved transmission ($\Delta T^{\hat{a}}$), spin-group symmetry ($[C_{2x} \parallel C_{8z}]$), and the angle ($\phi_{AB}$) between the magnetic ordering axes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling ratio parameterized by $	heta$ ($J_1 = \sin 	heta, J_4 = \cos 	heta$), and the contact rotation angle $\phi$ for transport calculations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electronic structure analysis relies on coupling the system to semi-infinite leads (Source S and Drain D) and assuming the ground state spin configuration is determined by energy minimization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AM/N phase structure, the orthogonal locking of ordering axes via second-order canting, and the dependence of spin-resolved transmission ($\Delta T^{\hat{z}}$ and $\Delta T^{\hat{x}}$) on the contact angle $\phi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the magnetic ground state by analyzing the Hamiltonian's parity structure and finding the minimum energy configuration ($\phi_{AB} = \pi/2$). It then transitions to electronic structure calculations, using the determined spin configuration to calculate spin-resolved transport properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify exact altermagnetic ground states of a classical Heisenberg model on a quasicrystal. Considering the Ammann--Beenker tiling as an example, we determine the ground states by exploiting the parity structure of its parent four-dimensional hypercubic lattice. The ground state hosts coplanar altermagnetic order characterized by the preserved spin-group symmetry $[C_{2x}\parallel C_{8z}]$. Without translational symmetry, the momentum-space spin splitting that usually identifies altermagnets is not directly accessible. We instead couple the spins to itinerant electrons and show spin-resolved transport with $g$-wave symmetry, confirming the altermagnetic nature in a nonperiodic system. Our results establish a geometric route to altermagnetic order in quasicrystals, connecting exact magnetic ground states to their spin-resolved transport signatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies the Curie-Weiss cell fluid model to analyze the critical and triple-point behavior of various real fluids. By fitting the model's microscopic parameters to experimental critical data, the authors demonstrate that the model provides a good description, particularly for large molecules, and can be successfully extended to describe complex phase coexistence regions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the methodology of parameterizing complex many-body interactions (like the Curie-Weiss term) and mapping them onto measurable macroscopic phase transitions is relevant for developing effective Hamiltonians in quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a quantitative link between the microscopic parameters of the Curie-Weiss cell fluid model and the macroscopic critical and triple-point data of real fluids.

**Main result.** The model shows the best agreement with experimental data for polyol ester lubricants, suggesting its suitability for soft-matter systems, and can be generalized to describe the triple point region.

**Method.** The authors fit the model's microscopic parameters ($J_1, v$) by matching the model's predictions for the critical temperature and density to experimental values.

**Model / system.** The study uses the Curie-Weiss cell fluid model, characterized by global attraction ($J_1$) and local repulsion ($J_2$), applied to real fluids like water, $	ext{CO}_2$, and polyol ester lubricants.

**Key observables.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), critical density ($ho_c$), critical pressure ($P_c$), critical compressibility factor ($Z_c$), and the ratio $T_c/T_{tr}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of local repulsion to global attraction ($f=J_2/J_1$), and the temperature- and phase-dependent attraction parameters ($\alpha_\pi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on a mean-field approximation, and initial parameter fitting is done using only the critical point data, requiring generalization to address the triple point.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention figures illustrating the phase diagram with coexistence lines and the comparison of model predictions versus experimental data for critical and triple points.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model parameters using critical point data, noting deviations in $P_c$ and $Z_c$. It then generalizes the model by introducing temperature-dependent attraction to successfully describe the triple point and reproduce the range of $T_c/T_{tr}$ observed in real fluids.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We examine how the Curie--Weiss cell fluid model can be quantitatively related to real fluids by determining its microscopic parameters from experimental critical-point data for water, carbon dioxide, and three polyol ester lubricants (POE5, POE7, and POE9). The microscopic parameters of the model are determined from the experimental values of the critical temperature and critical density: the global attraction coupling constant is fixed by the critical temperature, and the cell volume by the critical density. The critical pressure then follows as an independent model prediction, deviating from experimental values by 10--41$\%$ across the studied substances. Varying the ratio of local repulsion to global attraction $f=J_2/J_1$ over a wide range has only a weak effect on this deviation. For $f\geq1$, the model's critical compressibility factor lays within a narrow range $Z_c\approx0.382-0.386$, remaining close to the mean-field van der Waals value $Z_c=3/8=0.375$ and consistently overestimating experimental values. The best agreement with experiment is obtained for the polyol ester lubricants and improves along the POE5--POE7--POE9 series with increasing molar mass, suggesting that multiple-occupancy cell fluid models are well suited to systems of large molecules, particularly soft-matter systems with bounded interaction potentials. We further show that generalizing the model by introducing a temperature- and phase-dependent effective attraction allows it to describe a triple point and to reproduce the broad range of critical-to-triple temperature ratios $T_c/T_{\mathrm{tr}}$ characteristic of real fluids.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel method to locally determine the sign of the Dirac mass in magnetic topological materials using spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy. By exploiting crystalline rotation symmetry, the technique allows researchers to map the sign of the Berry curvature in real space, moving beyond merely identifying where a spectral gap exists. This capability is crucial for understanding the full topological character of materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable spectroscopic signature ($	ext{sgn}(\Omega_{	ext{occ}})$) that goes beyond simple gap measurements, offering a powerful tool for mapping topological phase transitions in real space, which is highly relevant for understanding topological materials in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a local spectroscopic technique capable of determining the topological sign of a Dirac mass ($M$), which is crucial because current methods only measure the gap magnitude $|M|$.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that spin-resolved tunneling, particularly when exploiting crystalline rotation symmetry on a $C_3$ axis, can directly and locally measure the sign of the occupied-band Berry curvature, $	ext{sgn}(\Omega_{	ext{occ}})$.

**Method.** The method combines theoretical modeling of magnetic topological surfaces with proposed experimental measurements using spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The study models magnetic topological surfaces exhibiting a Dirac cone gapped by an exchange mass $M$. The analysis uses tight-binding models, continuum Hamiltonians, and incorporates effects from spin-polarized tunneling tips and domain wall structures.

**Key observables.** Spin asymmetry $A(E)$, the sign of the Berry curvature ($	ext{sgn}(\Omega_{	ext{occ}})$), and the energy/spin characteristics of chiral domain-wall modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mass $M$, the tip polarization $P$, the crystalline symmetry axis ($C_3$), and the disorder strength ($\delta M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique relies on the existence of crystalline rotation symmetry ($C_n$ axis) to simplify the spin-orbital entanglement and ensure the band-edge state is fully spin-polarized.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the readout mechanism using spin-polarized tips on $C_3$ axes, showing spin reversal with $M$, and confirming the relationship between measured spin asymmetry and the Berry curvature sign. Other figures detail domain wall physics and the robustness of the sign measurement against disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical necessity of measuring the mass sign to detailing the symmetry-protected measurement protocol using spin-polarized STM, and finally validating the technique by analyzing domain walls and disordered systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A Dirac mass is characterized not only by its magnitude but also by its sign, which influences the Berry curvature, Hall response, and the propagation direction of chiral boundary modes. However, local spectroscopy of magnetic topological surfaces has so far accessed only the gap magnitude $|M|$. Here we show how the topological sign of $M$ can be determined locally. We first establish the sign information that spin-resolved tunneling can access and the reference required to obtain an absolute Hall sign. Although spin--orbital entanglement generally obscures the relation between measured spin and Dirac pseudospin, crystalline rotation symmetry alleviates this ambiguity at the band edge. On a $C_3$ axis, an axial tip couples to a fully spin-polarized state whose tunneling asymmetry directly reveals the sign of the occupied-band Berry curvature, independent of orbital composition. We further show that the zeroth Landau level provides an independent mass-sign measurement with an unpolarized tip, while opposite domains and their chiral domain-wall modes provide self-consistency checks. These results pave the way for imaging the locations where the topological character changes within a material, rather than merely identifying where a spectral gap appears.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes superconductivity in twisted WSe2, showing that pairing arises from inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations acting as the pairing glue. Using FRG and RPA, the authors demonstrate that the superconducting state is a mixed d-wave/p-wave pairing, and their continuum model successfully predicts this superconductivity persists even at zero displacement field, aligning with experimental findings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed theoretical mapping of pairing mechanisms in 2D van der Waals heterostructures using FRG is highly relevant for understanding emergent superconductivity in correlated quantum materials, bridging theory to experimental measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the superconducting instability and pairing mechanism in twisted bilayer WSe2, particularly how superconductivity emerges from repulsive interactions.

**Main result.** The pairing instability is driven by inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations, leading to a mixed d-wave singlet and p-wave triplet superconducting state. The continuum model successfully predicts superconductivity down to zero displacement field, matching experiments.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced many-body techniques, including Functional Renormalization Group (FRG), Random Phase Approximation (RPA), and Hartree-Fock calculations, comparing results across different theoretical models.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted bilayer WSe2 (tWSe2) at hole filling $\nu = -1$. Calculations utilize continuum models derived from first principles, comparing them to realistic three-orbital Wannier models with Hubbard and Coulomb interactions.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), pairing symmetry (mixed d-wave/p-wave), and the presence/strength of inter-valley-coherent spin-density-wave (IVC-SDW) order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hole filling $\nu = -1$, twist angle $\theta = 3.65^\circ$, displacement field ($D$), and interaction strength ($10/\epsilon$ or $\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include the Hubbard approximation (neglecting momentum/orbital dependence) and the assumption that RPA fluctuations capture the main pairing glue contribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate FRG phase diagrams showing the competition between IVC-SDW and superconducting regimes, and display the structure of the leading pairing eigenfunction, confirming its d-wave/p-wave mixed character.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results from multiple theoretical frameworks (HF, RPA, FRG) applied to the tWSe2 system. It first establishes the existence of competing ordered states (SDW) and then pinpoints the pairing glue by comparing FRG results with RPA projections, culminating in the continuum model's agreement with experimental observations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by the observation of superconductivity in twisted bilayer WSe$_2$ over an extended range of displacement fields, reaching from the correlated insulator down to zero field, we analyze the correlated electron phases of this system at hole filling $ν= -1$ and twist angle $3.65^\circ$ using Hartree--Fock, random phase approximation, and functional renormalization group calculations. We derive a continuum model with gate-screened Coulomb interaction from first principles and compare its instabilities to those of a realistic three-orbital Wannier model with a Hubbard interaction and an artificial single-orbital Hubbard model. In both cases, we find a superconducting instability of mixed $d$-wave singlet and $p$-wave triplet character, arising adjacent to inter-valley-coherent spin-density-wave order in the phase diagram spanned by displacement field and interaction strength. Our additional analysis of the pairing mechanism points to inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations as the pairing glue. The extent of the pairing regime and the associated critical temperatures depend on the model input, and we trace differences to the interaction range entering the projected interaction. Within the continuum model, the gate-screened Coulomb interaction yields pairing down to zero displacement field, bringing theory into agreement with experiment.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous mathematical framework for Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs) by treating the reference frame itself as a quantum object defined over the space of tetrads. It uses fibre bundle theory to distinguish between scenarios where the QRF superposes perspectives on one geometry versus superposing over multiple possible geometries. The work culminates in deriving a unique, invariant volume element on the general frame bundle.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly mathematical, the concept of a QRF is foundational for quantum gravity and quantum field theory in curved spacetime, making it relevant to theoretical physics concerned with spacetime structure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper adapts the relativistic concept of a reference frame as a tetrad to the quantum domain, formalizing the state of a Quantum Reference Frame (QRF). It aims to distinguish between superpositions of perspectives on a single geometry versus superpositions of geometries themselves.

**Main result.** The formalism is constructed using the language of fibre bundles, distinguishing between the orthonormal frame bundle (for fixed geometry) and the general frame bundle (for variable geometry). The construction yields a unique, invariant volume element on the general frame bundle.

**Method.** The authors use differential geometry, specifically analyzing the structure and invariance of volume elements on the frame bundle $F(M)$ by stipulating that the basis field acts as a tetrad field.

**Model / system.** The system is the Quantum Reference Frame (QRF), whose state is proposed to be a complex amplitude assignment over the space of tetrads. The mathematical framework is built upon fibre bundles over spacetime, specifically the frame bundle $F(M)$.

**Key observables.** The state of the QRF, which is a complex function on the relevant set of tetrads, and the expectation values of observables derived from coupling the QRF to measured systems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice between the orthonormal frame bundle (restricted QRF) and the general frame bundle (general QRF) dictates the physical interpretation of the superposition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumptions involve treating the reference frame via tetrads, and mathematically, the determination of the invariant volume element relies on stipulating orthonormality (a tetrad condition) to fix the constant in the Haar measure.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured conceptually, outlining the proposal's scope by contrasting the restricted (single geometry) and general (superposition of geometries) QRFs. It then delves into the mathematical details of constructing the invariant volume element on the general frame bundle $F(M)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We adapt to the context of quantum theory the treatment, familiar in relativity theory, of a reference frame as a tetrad. Since a tetrad is a set of four orthonormal vectors at a spacetime point, it encodes a timelike direction, three orthogonal spatial directions, and units; and so it gives a configuration in spacetime (a spatial orientation and state of motion) of an idealized infinitesimal apparatus (``rulers and a clock"). So a natural ``first guess" is that the state of a quantum reference frame should be an assignment of complex amplitudes to a set of tetrads. We develop this idea, in terms of bundles over spacetime of bases of the tangent spaces.   This paper is a companion to two other papers. It emphasises the main conceptual aspects of our proposal, while the other two papers give physical details. It also explains the main contrast between those two papers. Namely: (i) the first considers a single spacetime geometry, so that a quantum reference frame, on our proposal, gives a superposition of perspectives on that geometry; while (ii) the second considers the spacetime manifold with no geometry given a priori, which will imply that a quantum reference frame, on our proposal, can describe a superposition of perspectives on a superposition of geometries---not ``just" a superposition of perspectives on a single geometry. The main mathematical idea for representing this contrast will be choosing between (i) the orthonormal frame bundle, for a given spacetime geometry; (ii) the frame bundle in the usual sense of containing all bases of all spacetime points' tangent spaces.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the quantum sample complexity required to learn Boolean functions whose Fourier spectrum is sparse. It achieves a near-optimal quantum sample bound by combining techniques like multilinear majority polynomials for support detection and established sparse recovery theorems for coefficient estimation. This advances the understanding of quantum data requirements for learning structured functions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed for robust quantum learning and sparse recovery are fundamental tools applicable to quantum machine learning models or characterizing quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimum number of uniform quantum examples required to exactly learn a Boolean function that is sparse in its Fourier representation.

**Main result.** It establishes a near-optimal quantum sample complexity of O(k * Lambda(k)) for learning k-Fourier-sparse functions, significantly improving previous bounds.

**Method.** The approach involves constructing a candidate set containing the true Fourier support using multilinear majority polynomials and then applying sparse recovery theorems.

**Model / system.** The model involves Boolean functions f: {0, 1}^n -> {-1, 1} sampled via quantum states, analyzed using Fourier analysis techniques.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the sample complexity (number of quantum examples) needed for exact learning.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the sparsity bound k, the input dimension n, and the desired failure probability delta.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to perform separate measurements on individual examples and relies on established results like the Candès–Plan recovery theorem.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around two main parts: first, constructing a candidate set containing the true Fourier support using random approximations; second, using this set to exactly recover the unknown sparse Fourier coefficients.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the number of uniform quantum examples needed to exactly learn a Boolean function with at most $k$ nonzero Fourier coefficients. We give a learner with near-optimal $\widetilde{O}(k)$ sample complexity independent of the input length $n$. The learner uses only separate measurements of individual examples. This improves the previous $O(k^{3/2}\log^2 k)$ upper bound (Arunachalam, Chakraborty, Lee, Paraashar, and de Wolf, 2021). The main ingredient is a candidate set formed by symmetric differences of subsets from independent groups of Fourier samples. A multilinear majority polynomial combines unbiased random approximations into an accurate witness whose Fourier support lies in this set. Granularity then forces the set to contain the entire Fourier support, including subsets never directly sampled. For comparison, we derive the tight classical sample complexity $Θ(nk)$ by combining known sparse-recovery and lower-bound results.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the second law of thermodynamics for closed quantum systems by defining and analyzing the quantum Boltzmann entropy. It develops formalisms—including weak and strong second laws—and tests them on various model Hamiltonians. The results confirm that entropy tends to increase, suggesting that macroscopic irreversibility is a robust feature of quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the quantum foundations of thermodynamics, providing rigorous theoretical tools to understand how macroscopic irreversibility emerges from unitary quantum evolution, which is central to open quantum systems theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the formulation and confirmation of precise versions of the second law of thermodynamics for closed macroscopic quantum systems.

**Main result.** The analysis suggests that the weak and strong second laws hold for several model systems, with probability currents showing a tendency to flow from smaller to larger dimension macro spaces as time progresses.

**Method.** The methodology involves analyzing the time evolution of the probability distribution across macrostate subspaces using concepts like probability currents and deriving approximate master equations.

**Model / system.** The system is a closed macroscopic quantum system evolving unitarily in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, decomposed into orthogonal macrostate subspaces. Models include random block-band Hamiltonians and those based on Gaussian Unitary Ensembles (GUE).

**Key observables.** Quantum Boltzmann Entropy ($S_{qB}$), probability distribution weights ($p_
u(t)$), and probability currents ($\delta_\ell(t)$ and $\delta_r(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the Hilbert space ($D$), the ratio of dimensions of macrospaces ($d_
u/D$), and the time scale ($t 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption of a macrostate decomposition, the use of approximations (like the Markovian approximation), and taking limits ($D 	o \infty$ or $t 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of probability flow functions, such as $\varphi_2^1(t) = (J_1(2t)/t)^2$, showing a predominantly decreasing trend, and comparing positive versus negative contributions to probability flow.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the quantum Boltzmann entropy and the two proposed second laws. It then analyzes the evolution of probability distributions using continuity equations and current definitions. Finally, it applies these concepts to specific model Hamiltonians to confirm the laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a closed macroscopic quantum system in a pure state $ψ$ evolving unitarily with Hamiltonian $H$ in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space $\mathscr{H}$. Following von Neumann, we assume a decomposition of $\mathscr{H}$ into mutually orthogonal subspaces $\mathscr{H}_ν$, where each $ν$ represents a macro state, and $\mathscr{H}_ν$ contains the pure states compatible with $ν$. We define the quantum Boltzmann entropy for a macro state $ν$ as $S_\mathrm{qB}(ν) = k_{\mathrm{B}} \log \dim \mathscr{H}_ν$, where $k_\mathrm{B}$ is the Boltzmann constant. Goldstein et al. [http://arxiv.org/abs/1903.11870] have suggested, as a version of the second law of thermodynamics, that $S_\mathrm{qB}$ predominantly increases with time for realistic macroscopic systems. Here, we discuss precise versions of this statement and confirm them for several simple models of $H$ and $\mathscr{H}_ν$.

</details>

### [QEDIA: Quantum Program Verification in Lean](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17708v1)

**Authors:** Yuanjie, Ren, Zhicheng Zhang, Junyi Liu, Jinzheng Li, Yidi Qi, Mingsheng Ying  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17708v1>

Icons show whether Lean, Python scripts, or LLM review performs each acceptance check of Section 5.1. Dotted arrows show where the agents use the guidelines and the quantum extension.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QEDIA, a novel framework in Lean 4 designed to formally verify quantum programs that mix classical control flow with quantum operations. It achieves this by formalizing an extended Quantum Hoare Logic (QHL+) that consistently handles the classical-quantum interplay. This advancement provides a powerful, automated tool for ensuring the correctness of complex quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, machine-checkable methodology for verifying complex quantum algorithms, which is crucial for building reliable quantum hardware and software stacks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is the formal verification of quantum programs that involve complex interleaving between classical control flow and quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors present QEDIA, a novel framework built in Lean 4 that formalizes and proves the soundness of an extended Quantum Hoare Logic (QHL+) capable of handling this classical-quantum interplay.

**Method.** The method involves developing a formal proof assistant library based on QHL+ and utilizing an LLM-based prover agent to automate the construction of the formal proofs for various quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The system models general quantum programs, including circuits for the Quantum Fourier Transform, Phase Estimation, and Hamiltonian simulation, where classical variables dictate gate execution and measurement outcomes influence subsequent classical steps.

**Key observables.** Correctness proofs for quantum algorithms, including fidelity guarantees for Hamiltonian simulation and state mappings for QFT.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The framework handles parameters related to circuit depth, error bounds ($\epsilon$), and the structure of classical control flow (e.g., loop invariants).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the classical-quantum interplay can be consistently mechanized across the mathematical, syntactic, and semantic levels of the program logic.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the classical-quantum interplay in examples like Repeat-Until-Success circuits, QFT structures, and Trotter simulation circuits.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QEDIA, formalizes QHL+ for classical-quantum interaction, and then demonstrates its application by verifying fundamental circuits such as QFT, Phase Estimation, and Hamiltonian simulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Practical quantum programs interleave quantum and classical computation: classical data shape the quantum operations that are performed, and measurement outcomes shape the classical computation that follows. Formal verification of such programs in a proof assistant requires mechanizing a program logic that handles this interplay consistently at every level, from the mathematics of quantum mechanics, through program syntax and semantics, to specifications and proof rules. Such mechanizations, along with the program proofs built on them, typically require substantial manual effort.   We present QEDIA, a framework for verifying quantum programs with classical variables in Lean 4. At its foundation is an open library that formalizes the quantum Hoare logic QHL+ across all these levels and proves the logic sound for both partial and total correctness. We construct the library and the program proofs, under human supervision, using an LLM-based prover agent, whose general-purpose core we extend for quantum program verification. We illustrate QEDIA with verified programs including the quantum Fourier transform, phase estimation, repeat-until-success circuits, and Hamiltonian simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work examines quantum machine learning on real-world images to determine if theoretical quantum advantages persist. By controlling the model structure, the authors find that quantum classifiers perform best when the input features are geometrically compatible with the quantum feature space. This suggests that successful quantum ML requires careful alignment between classical data representations and quantum structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on ML, the analysis of how unitary constraints act as implicit regularizers and how feature compatibility dictates performance has parallels with understanding effective Hamiltonians or constraints in physical models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether theoretical quantum advantages over classical algorithms translate to meaningful improvements when applied to realistic classical data like natural images.

**Main result.** The effectiveness of quantum classifiers depends strongly on the geometry of the input representations, suggesting they benefit from classical features aligned with quantum feature spaces.

**Method.** The authors focus on the classifier level, using classical feature extractors and analyzing how the unitary structure of quantum classifiers affects generalization by controlling data scale and supervision strength.

**Model / system.** The model uses quantum classifiers operating on classical visual benchmarks. The process involves encoding classical data into quantum states via a quantum feature mapping, followed by classification using a learnable quantum circuit composed of unitary matrices.

**Key observables.** Generalization performance, Kernel Target Alignment (KTA), and the generalization gap (TestAcc - TrainAcc).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Data scale, supervision strength, and the number of unitary operations (circuit depth).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study isolates the classifier's inductive bias by using classical feature extractors, and it analyzes the impact of the unitary constraint acting as an implicit regularizer.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum classification pipeline using classical features, and later figures compare generalization gaps and analyze the effect of varying 'bag size' (m) on test accuracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of quantum advantage on real data. It then details the quantum classification pipeline, analyzes generalization bounds, compares quantum vs. classical heads, and finally investigates the sensitivity of performance to the 'bag size' used in weak supervision.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Machine Learning (QML) has a theoretical advantage over classical algorithms for specific synthetic data that is computationally hard. However, it remains unclear whether such theoretical advantages play a meaningful role for realistic classical data, such as natural images. Quantum structural properties induced by unitary operations or entanglement introduce inductive biases that differ from those of classical models, potentially leading to distinct learning behavior on real-world data. In a fully quantum learning pipeline, classical real-world data are first encoded into quantum states via a quantum feature mapping, analogous to a classical feature extractor, and a quantum classifier then classifies the resulting states. Quantum feature mappings generate representations that are difficult to realize classically, while also inducing representation-level biases that do not necessarily match the structure of realistic data. To enable a fair comparison at the classifier level, we focus on the classifier and use classical feature extractors that already provide powerful representations for natural images. By controlling the data scale and supervision strength, we analyze how the unitary structure of quantum classifiers affects generalization, and show that their effectiveness depends strongly on the geometry of the input representations. These results indicate that quantum classifiers benefit from classical features that are aligned with quantum feature spaces. Our analysis shows that kernel alignment identifies representations compatible with quantum feature spaces, providing insights into translating classical models into quantum algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses time-resolved ARPES to probe the dynamics of excitons and hot carriers in twisted bilayer graphene. The authors successfully identify a 'ghost-Fano' exciton signature and reveal that an inherent interlayer electric field causes the two graphene layers to reach different peak electronic temperatures. This demonstrates how electrostatic asymmetry can control non-equilibrium energy flow in correlated 2D systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides direct experimental evidence of non-equilibrium energy coupling and correlated excitonic physics in a tunable 2D material system, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electronic states in novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the dynamics of excitons and charge carriers in twisted bilayer graphene (tBLG) to understand interlayer energy transfer and correlated electronic states.

**Main result.** The research identified a 'ghost-Fano' exciton signature and demonstrated that an interlayer electric field drives an ultrafast, asymmetric redistribution of electronic energy between the two graphene layers.

**Method.** Time-resolved Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (TR-ARPES) was employed to track spectral features and measure time-dependent electronic temperatures.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted bilayer graphene (tBLG) on SiC at a twist angle of $9 \pm 1^\circ$. The analysis involves modeling carrier cooling using a three-temperature model and interpreting spectral features via exciton theory.

**Key observables.** Spectral features in the hybridization-induced band gap (ghost-Fano exciton), layer-resolved electronic temperatures ($T_e$), and time-dependent photoemission intensity differences.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($9 \pm 1^\circ$), doping offset (30 meV), interlayer electric field ($\approx 0.1 	ext{ V/nm}$), and characteristic decay times ($	au_X = 300 	ext{ fs}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The binding energy estimate from energy separation is approximate, and the temperature asymmetry analysis assumes energy transfer occurs during the initial excitation/thermalization stage.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate ARPES intensity maps, time-dependent photoemission intensity differences for exciton/conduction band features, and plots showing the distinct, asymmetric evolution of electronic temperature in the top versus bottom graphene layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing the tBLG structure and observing the exciton signature via ARPES, to quantifying the layer-dependent carrier cooling dynamics, and finally attributing the temperature asymmetry to interlayer energy transfer driven by doping-induced fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted bilayer graphene (tBLG) on H-intercalated SiC (twist angle of $9 \pm 1^{\circ}$) is studied by position\mbox{-}, time\mbox{-}, and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES). Both graphene layers are hole doped, with a Dirac-point offset of 30~meV. Time-resolved ARPES near the van Hove singularity reveals a spectral feature within the hybridization-induced band gap, together with excited electrons in the conduction band. The in-gap feature exhibits distinct dynamics, and its binding energy and $k$-space location are consistent with the proposed non-hydrogenic ``ghost-Fano'' exciton arising from destructive interference between two nearly degenerate excitonic channels. Near the Dirac points, the twist separates the largely layer-polarized Dirac cones in momentum space, providing direct layer selectivity. We find markedly different peak electronic temperatures in the two graphene layers, even though their subsequent cooling dynamics are very similar. We attribute this to an ultrafast interlayer redistribution of electronic energy, driven by an interlayer electric field that arises from the different doping in the layers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the Mean First-Passage Time (MFPT) formalism to accurately map high free-energy barriers. It introduces a 'top-start' simulation technique that bypasses the need to observe rare spontaneous barrier crossings. This allows for reliable reconstruction of free-energy profiles in systems exhibiting extremely slow kinetics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the mathematical techniques for handling rare events and reconstructing underlying potentials from kinetic measurements are highly relevant to open quantum systems where tunneling or barrier crossing rates are critical.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of reconstructing one-dimensional free-energy profiles, particularly when the barriers separating metastable states are very high.

**Main result.** A novel "top-start" extension to the Mean First-Passage Time (MFPT) formalism is introduced, which accurately reconstructs free-energy profiles even when spontaneous barrier crossings are prohibitively rare.

**Method.** The method utilizes Markov-chain transition-matrix formulations, establishing the equivalence between the top-start approach and traditional two-region constructions.

**Model / system.** The model involves a particle undergoing Brownian motion along a one-dimensional potential landscape, which is treated using a discrete Markov process.

**Key observables.** Mean First-Passage Time (MFPT, $	au(x)$), steady-state distribution ($P_{st}(x)$), and the free-energy profile ($eta U(x)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis covers low, medium, and high free-energy barrier regimes, with $eta = 1/(k_B T)$ being a key parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are assumed to be Markovian, and the reconstruction relies on the relationship between MFPT, steady-state flux, and the free-energy profile.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 illustrates the decomposition of a top-start trajectory into multiple region-restricted trajectories, demonstrating the core mechanism of the method.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem and the limitations of conventional MFPT sampling. It then develops the theoretical framework using Markov chains, proposes the top-start scheme, and validates the method using random-walk simulations against exact transition-matrix benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rare events, such as crystal nucleation in mildly supercooled liquids, are characterized by waiting times that can be much longer than the timescale of the underlying microscopic dynamics. The mean first-passage time (MFPT) formalism [J. Wedekind and D. Reguera, J. Phys. Chem. B 112, 11060 (2008)] provides a route to reconstruct one-dimensional free-energy profiles from the MFPT and steady-state distribution measured along a reaction coordinate, but conventional implementations become inefficient for sufficiently high barriers because spontaneous crossings are rarely observed. We introduce a "top-start" extension that initiates trajectories near the barrier top and reconstructs the free-energy profile from their subsequent evolution away from the barrier toward lower-free-energy regions. We establish the equivalence between the top-start and two-region constructions using a Markov-chain transition-matrix formulation. We validate the method using one-dimensional random-walk simulations across low, medium, and high free-energy barriers, with transition-matrix calculations providing an exact benchmark for the corresponding discretized models. The top-start reconstruction remains accurate in the high-barrier regime where conventional MFPT sampling becomes prohibitively inefficient.

</details>

**Authors:** Y. -J. Shen, Y. -C. Shen, C. -H. Chu, H. H. Jen

**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates that incorporating nonlinear couplings between a charger and a quantum battery significantly accelerates the energy charging process. The authors show that this nonlinear interaction acts as a powerful resource, allowing for fast charging even when no external driving field is applied. This broadens the understanding of interaction-enabled quantum advantages in energy storage.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems and energy transfer, utilizing concepts like coupling-induced dynamics and non-equilibrium thermodynamics, which are central to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how nonlinear couplings can accelerate the charging process of quantum batteries, potentially surpassing the performance limits of classical or linearly coupled quantum systems.

**Main result.** Nonlinear charger-battery interactions are shown to be an effective resource for accelerating charging, enabling faster charging even in the absence of external driving.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical modeling based on the master equation to derive the time evolution of energy and ergotropy in the quantum battery system.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a charger coupled to a quantum battery, modeled with a Hamiltonian including linear and nonlinear coupling terms, and subject to external driving and dephasing.

**Key observables.** Battery Energy (E_B), Ergotropy, and the characteristic Charging Time ($\tau$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinear coupling strength ($g_2$), linear coupling strength ($g_1$), external driving strength ($F$), and dephasing rate ($\gamma_C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the interaction picture under the resonance condition, and the system is modeled using two-level approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of energy and ergotropy under varying coupling strengths and driving conditions, and plot the charging time ($\tau$) dependence on the dephasing rate ($\gamma_C$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the charging dynamics by first establishing the model Hamiltonian, then deriving the equations of motion, and finally comparing the charging time and energy storage capabilities across weak, strong, and undriven regimes, highlighting the role of $g_2$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Charger-mediated quantum batteries offer promising opportunities for efficient energy transfer and storage at the quantum level, with potential advantages beyond their classical counterparts. Here, we theoretically demonstrate that nonlinear charger-battery interactions can substantially accelerate the charging of quantum batteries. In the weak-driving regime, sufficiently strong nonlinear coupling can dominate over the effect of dephasing and significantly reduce the charging time. In the strong- and optimal-driving regimes, where the optimal driving strength maximizes the steady-state ergotropy in the linear-coupling regime, nonlinear coupling gives rise to a new optimum in the charging time beyond that of the linear-coupling regime, accompanied by nearly critically damped dynamics. Remarkably, even in the absence of external driving, nonlinear coupling alone can substantially reduce the charging time, providing an alternative route toward fast battery charging. Our results establish nonlinear charger-battery coupling as an effective resource for accelerating quantum-battery charging and broaden the scope of interaction-enabled quantum advantages in energy transfer and storage.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the mathematical nature of critical fluctuations in crystals approaching elastic phase transitions. It shows that the associated zero modes satisfy equations related to the conformal Killing equation. The findings provide a systematic classification of these critical modes, which have implications for understanding mechanical metamaterials and critical elastic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state elasticity, the mathematical framework—relating critical fluctuations to conformal symmetries—is highly relevant to theoretical physics dealing with scale invariance, critical phenomena, and emergent symmetries in many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the critical fluctuations and zero modes of the displacement field in crystalline solids near continuous elastic phase transitions (elastic instabilities).

**Main result.** The critical modes obey a condition reminiscent of the conformal Killing equation, yielding specific sets of zero modes (e.g., dilation and three special conformal transformations for isostructural instability).

**Method.** The analysis uses linear elasticity theory, deriving zero mode conditions from the vanishing internal stress ($\partial_i u_j + \partial_j u_i = \sum \lambda_\alpha \varepsilon^A_{ij}$) and comparing these solutions to known mathematical structures.

**Model / system.** The system is crystalline solids undergoing elastic instabilities, modeled using the displacement vector $\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})$ and the strain tensor $\varepsilon_{ij}$. The analysis is generalized to extremal mechanical metamaterials.

**Key observables.** Critical modes of the displacement field, principal strains ($\epsilon_\alpha$), and the vanishing of the determinant of principal strains.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Elastic eigenmoduli ($C_\alpha$), the dimensionality of the critical subspace ($\Gamma$), and the wavevector $\mathbf{q}$ in Fourier space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to linear elasticity theory and assumes the absence of external stresses; later notes suggest the initial assumption of analyticity for $\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{q})$ is too restrictive.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the macroscopic zero modes for both volumetric singlet (dilation and SCT) and symmetry-breaking instabilities, showing the functional form of the displacement field.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the zero mode condition by first establishing the connection to the conformal Killing equation for isostructural instabilities, then extending the analysis to symmetry-breaking instabilities, and finally using group theory and determinant analysis to constrain the principal strains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In case a crystal is close to a continuous elastic phase transition, pronounced critical fluctuations emerge. We show that on the level of linear elasticity theory the displacement vector for these critical modes obeys a condition reminiscent of the conformal Killing equation. For an isostructural instability, four critical modes arise corresponding to one dilation and three special conformal transformations. At crystal symmetry-breaking instabilities, the critical fluctuations include a continuum of modes even for crystals with finite size. For certain cases, these comprise the conformal transformations associated with two-dimensional Minkowski space. These results have implications for critical elastic phenomena as well as for extremal mechanical metamaterials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a resource-based framework to assess the reliability of quantum devices whose failure modes are complex and do not map to simple failure subspaces. By quantifying the remaining task-relevant resource (like coherence) relative to an ideal capacity, the authors provide a robust, task-specific metric. This is demonstrated using spin-boson models applied to quantum random number generators and interferometers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, task-specific methodology for characterizing quantum device performance degradation that goes beyond simple measures like fidelity or survival probability, which is crucial for practical quantum technology assessment.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of assessing quantum device reliability when failure is not characterized by entering a fixed failure subspace, necessitating a resource-based framework.

**Main result.** A resource-based reliability framework is proposed, quantifying remaining task-relevant resources (like coherence) relative to an ideal capacity, which is distinct from classical survival probability.

**Method.** The authors develop the framework by defining a resource measure $Q$ operationally linked to the task, and applying it to model systems like QRNGs and interferometers.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses the spin-boson model to simulate open quantum systems, specifically examining how environmental noise affects coherence in multi-spin systems. The framework is applied to model quantum random number generators and two-port interferometers.

**Key observables.** Relative-entropy coherence, logarithmic $l_1$ coherence (fringe resource), and the resource reliability $R_Q(t) = Q[ho(t)] / Q_{	ext{max}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coherence decay rate $\Gamma(t)$, the number of spins $M$, and the correlation parameter $c$ used to model noise correlations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resource reliability is a capacity fraction, not a survival probability; the analysis often assumes the bath is unobserved during operation (trusted-device model).

**Figures summary.** Figure 7 compares resource inference for QRNGs under different noise models (collective vs. partially correlated) and shows the one-spin interferometer reliability and inferred all-correct probability over simulated records.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the resource-based reliability concept, contrasting it with fixed-subspace failure models. It then applies this to specific systems (QRNG, interferometer) using the spin-boson model to analyze coherence decay under various noise correlations, culminating in finite-record simulations to validate the resource assessment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum devices can lose the resource needed for a specified function without entering a fixed failure subspace. We propose a resource-based reliability framework for this situation, quantifying the remaining task-relevant resource relative to a stated ideal capacity. The resource measure is selected through its operational connection to the task and the allowed operations. We develop this approach for a trusted quantum random-number generator and a calibrated two-port interferometer. Relative-entropy coherence determines the optimal asymptotic yield of standard coherent qubits under incoherent operations for the former, while logarithmic $l_1$ coherence quantifies the fringe resource of the latter and determines its calibrated correct-port probability. An exactly solvable multi-spin spin-boson model illustrates how environmental records affect these resources at the same isolated-spin dephasing strength. Collective noise preserves coherence within magnetization sectors for product inputs, but enhances the decay of Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger branch coherence. Finite-record simulations connect the resource definitions to measurement counts and show that increasing the sample size reduces statistical fluctuations without eliminating bias from an incorrect many-spin noise model. The resulting framework provides a task-specific route to reliability assessment when a fixed failure projector is insufficient, while keeping retained resource capacity distinct from a classical survival probability.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17923v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous mathematical proof that trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field will dynamically form a Bose-Einstein condensate, regardless of the initial mixedness of the state. By tracking the one-particle density matrix, the authors show that the system's entropy vanishes asymptotically, purifying the state to the ground state. This establishes a fundamental mechanism for quantum state purification driven by radiative relaxation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical proof for the dynamical purification of quantum states in a realistic, interacting many-body system, bridging the gap between equilibrium theory and time evolution for BEC formation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper rigorously establishes the dynamical formation of a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) and the asymptotic purification of trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field.

**Main result.** It proves that any initially mixed, positive-entropy state purifies to the ground state, and the von Neumann entropy of the macroscopic one-particle density matrix vanishes asymptotically.

**Method.** The analysis tracks the one-particle density matrix through a nonlinear resonance cascade, employing successive limits: the mean-field limit ($N	o\infty$), the weak-coupling limit ($\eta	o 0$), and the long-time limit ($T	o\infty$).

**Model / system.** The system consists of trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field, modeled by a closed, unitary Hamiltonian. The dynamics are analyzed via the Hartree hierarchy derived from the BBGKY hierarchy.

**Key observables.** Macroscopic one-particle density matrix ($\kappa_T$) and its von Neumann entropy ($S[\kappa_T]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time ($T$), particle number ($N$), and coupling strength ($\eta$); the process is analyzed under the assumption of strictly positive downward Fermi-Golden-Rule rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a non-vanishing initial ground state component and relies on taking successive limits ($N	o\infty$, weak-coupling, $T	o\infty$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the argument by first deriving the Hartree hierarchy, then showing the convergence of the density matrix to the pure ground state projector in the trace norm, and finally demonstrating the vanishing of the von Neumann entropy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The dynamical formation of a complete Bose-Einstein condensate and the asymptotic purification of trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field are rigorously established. By tracking the macroscopic one-particle density matrix $κ_T$ through a nonlinear resonance cascade, it is demonstrated that radiative relaxation irreversibly depletes all excited trap modes, even when the initial macroscopic state $κ_0$ is genuinely mixed. Here $κ_T$ is an average, with respect to a de Finetti measure $μ_0$, over mean-field particle-field orbits, so that the mixedness of $κ_0$ reflects a statistical ensemble of orbits. Under the assumptions of a non-vanishing initial ground state component and strictly positive downward Fermi-Golden-Rule rates, strong trace-norm convergence to a pure condensate is proven, $$   \lim_{T\to\infty} \| κ_T-|χ_0\rangle\langleχ_0| \|_{\mathfrak{S}_1(\mathfrak{h})} = 0 \, . $$ Furthermore, the von Neumann entropy of the macroscopic one-particle density matrix is shown to vanish asymptotically, $$   \lim_{T\to\infty}S[κ_T]=0 \, , $$ without any claim of monotonicity in $T$. Thus, any initially mixed, positive-entropy state ($0<S[κ_0]<\infty$) is purified to the ground state $|χ_0\rangle\langleχ_0|$. For the proof, we derive the Hartree hierarchy of the coupled system of bosons and photons from the finite-$N$ BBGKY hierarchy of the microscopic quantum many-body system, establishing trace-norm convergence in the mean-field limit as $N\to\infty$. The limits are taken successively; first $N\to\infty$, then the weak-coupling limit, and finally $T\to\infty$. Although the global particle-field evolution remains unitary, the macroscopic one-particle state converges to a pure state as energy and correlations are continuously radiated into the dispersive field.

</details>

**Authors:** Xuanran Zhu, Keyi Huang, Chao Zhang, Xinfang Nie, Qi Zhao, Dawei Lu

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified framework to construct geometrically optimal entanglement witnesses (GEWs) that overcome the complexity of high-dimensional quantum state spaces. By defining witnesses based on the nearest separable state in Hilbert-Schmidt space, the method achieves universal detection of various entanglement types. It demonstrates this powerful tool on a four-qubit NMR system, showing enhanced noise robustness and efficiency over prior methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general mathematical tool for quantifying entanglement that is directly applicable to experimental quantum platforms like NMR, bridging abstract theory with concrete physical measurement protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is constructing optimal entanglement witnesses (EWs) for arbitrary quantum systems, especially in high-dimensional spaces, due to structural complexity.

**Main result.** The proposed geometrically optimal witnesses (GEWs) provide a universal, efficient, and robust method for detecting bipartite, multipartite, and high-dimensional entanglement structures.

**Method.** The method constructs GEWs by leveraging the geometric properties of the target state relative to the set of separable states in Hilbert-Schmidt space, finding the nearest separable state via optimization.

**Model / system.** The framework is demonstrated on a four-qubit nuclear spin quantum system using NMR, and the theory applies generally to arbitrary quantum systems and entanglement structures.

**Key observables.** Entanglement witness expectation values ($\langle W angle$), fidelity ($F$), and the Hilbert-Schmidt (HS) distance measure are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The visibility threshold ($p_{	ext{GEW}}$) under depolarizing noise, and the number of qubits/dimensions ($N=4$ qubits in the example).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The numerical optimization used to find the nearest separable state is a convex hull approximation (CHA) and is not guaranteed to find the exact global minimum.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric separation between the target state and the set of separable states, show the detection of bound entanglement, and compare noise robustness across different witness types.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the GEW framework based on geometric optimization, details its application to finding the nearest separable state, and validates its utility by applying it to a four-qubit NMR system, comparing results against existing witnesses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Constructing optimal entanglement witnesses for arbitrary quantum systems remains a formidable challenge, hindered by the structural complexity of high-dimensional quantum state spaces. In this work, we overcome this barrier by introducing a unified framework for constructing geometrically optimal witnesses capable of identifying arbitrary entanglement structures. By leveraging the geometric properties of a target quantum state relative to a specific entanglement structure in Hilbert-Schmidt space, these witnesses are constructed in an efficient and effective manner. Among all nontrivial witnesses, they provide maximal distinguishability between the target state and the set of states without the desired entanglement structure. As a result, our approach enables the universal detection of bipartite, multipartite, and high-dimensional entanglement. To demonstrate its experimental feasibility, we apply the framework to a four-qubit nuclear spin quantum system. Compared to existing methods, our approach offers enhanced noise robustness, broader detection universality, and improved construction efficiency, providing a powerful and versatile tool for advancing the characterization and understanding of entanglement in quantum systems.

</details>
