**Summary.** This work develops advanced criteria to quantify entanglement in quantum states that deviate from the simple Gaussian form, using polynomial excitations. By reducing complex infinite-dimensional problems to finite matrix checks, the authors show that entanglement resources are robustly maintained or generated during non-linear quantum operations. This provides a vital theoretical tool for understanding non-Gaussian quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous framework to quantify and track entanglement resources (NPT) in complex, non-Gaussian quantum systems relevant to quantum optics and quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize quantum entanglement in continuous-variable multipartite systems, specifically extending the analysis beyond the limitations of purely Gaussian entanglement.

**Main result.** It proves that polynomial excitations can create NPT entanglement between initially separable subsystems, and it derives analytical expressions for the covariance matrix of general quadratic operators.

**Method.** The methodology involves reducing the infinite-dimensional positivity problem to a finite-dimensional one by analyzing the partial transposition of a bilinear representation matrix, utilizing symplectic diagonalization, and spectral decomposition.

**Model / system.** The focus is on continuous-variable quantum systems (e.g., optical modes, phonons) whose states are subjected to polynomial excitations acting on an underlying Gaussian state.

**Key observables.** NPT entanglement (Negative Partial Transpose), quantum entanglement negativity, and the covariance matrix of quadratic operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-locality of the excitation, tuned by a parameter $\kappa$, and the number of degrees of freedom.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is most tractable for states that can be expressed as polynomial excitations of an initially Gaussian state.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the experimental implementation of polynomial excitations involving linear unitary Bogoliubov transformations and non-Gaussian processes like photon addition/subtraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general problem of non-Gaussian entanglement, developing the finite-dimensional mathematical criteria for detection, applying these tools to specific quadratic excitations, and concluding with the derivation of closed-form analytical expressions for correlation functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Polynomial excitations consisting of creation and annihilation operators are studied. Subsystem entanglement is proven produced and preserved by such manipulations. NPT entanglement already present between any two subsystems of a general pure or mixed multipartite Gaussian state is preserved under all polynomial excitations. Nevertheless, these excitations can create NPT entanglement between initially separable subsystems, implying that the number of NPT entangled pairs cannot decrease. Polynomial excitations of a wide class of initially separable pure states are considered. For these states, the method reduces the infinite-dimensional positivity problem to a finite-dimensional one: after partial transposition, NPT entanglement is equivalent to a corresponding bilinear representation matrix not being positive semi-definite. Furthermore, using symplectic diagonalization and spectral decomposition, an explicit transformation is constructed between the Fock-space and position representations. It is applied to quadratic excitations of a three-mode mixed Gaussian state. The behavior of the NPT entanglement is tracked between subsystems as a function of a parameter $κ$, which tunes the non-locality of the excitation, and quantum negativity is evaluated. Under a chosen quadratic Hamiltonian, the resulting unitary time evolution maintains NPT entanglement for long intervals, interrupted only by short periods of PPT states. Finally, a closed analytical expression is derived for the covariance matrix of a general quadratic operator for an arbitrary number of degrees of freedom in phase space. Our framework directly connects mathematical foundations, theoretical modeling, and experimentally feasible implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'ghost-unitary' methods to enhance the generation of entangled photonic resource states by engineering how the system responds to unavoidable interferometric loss. By applying the required unitary transformation to the heralding modes rather than the resource modes, the method suppresses false-positive detection events. This offers a crucial pathway for building higher-fidelity quantum computation and communication protocols using photonics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, mathematically derived technique (ghost-unitary implementation) to mitigate the primary experimental hurdle in photonic quantum information—photon loss—by fundamentally changing how the loss affects the state preparation process.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of generating high-fidelity entangled photonic resource states (RSG) in the presence of significant interferometric loss.

**Main result.** The proposed 'ghost-unitary' implementation, which applies the required unitary transformation to the heralding modes instead of the resource modes, significantly improves the fidelity of generated states compared to direct methods, especially under lossy conditions.

**Method.** The core method involves exploiting the transpose identity of multi-mode squeezed vacuum states to redefine the resource generation process, effectively reshaping the detrimental effects of amplitude damping loss.

**Model / system.** The system is discrete-variable photonic quantum information processing, utilizing entangled biphoton sources (Multi-Mode Squeezed Vacuum states) to generate states like Bell, NOON, and GHZ states.

**Key observables.** Output fidelity ($F$), Success Probability ($p_{succ}$), and the overall Yield ($Y = p 	imes F$) for various resource states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing strength ($\lambda$), various loss efficiencies ($\eta_{BS}, \eta_d, \eta_e$), and the photon number ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the dominant imperfection is interferometric loss and that the ghost-unitary implementation remains well-defined despite general residual non-commuting loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the fidelity and yield of direct versus ghost implementations for NOON states and teleportation, showing the parameter regimes where the ghost method outperforms the direct one.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from background theory (MMSV states and the transpose trick) to specific analyses, comparing direct vs. ghost methods for two-photon NOON states, then extending this comparison to Bell state generation and $n$-GHZ state fidelity, and finally simulating Type-I fusion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entangled few-photon states, such as Bell and Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states, are central resources for discrete-variable photonic quantum information processing. In many architectures, these states are generated probabilistically by first heralding single photons from biphoton sources and then interfering them in linear-optical circuits. In this work, we show that the entanglement structure of said biphoton sources can be directly exploited for noise-engineering to deliver higher quality resource states. Using the transpose identity of multi-mode squeezed vacuum states, we introduce "ghost-unitary" implementations in which the target resource-generation interferometer is applied to the heralding modes rather than to the delivered resource modes. When the dominant imperfection is interferometric loss, this relocation converts direct amplitude damping of the output into false-positive heralding events that require both higher-pair emission and photon loss. In the low-squeezing regime, these events are parametrically suppressed, yielding higher-fidelity conditional resource states. We analyze this mechanism for single-qubit states, two-photon NOON states, Bell states, and a family of $n$-qubit GHZ states, as well as for single-qubit teleportation and type-I-fusion-based resource growth. Across these examples, we delineate finite parameter regimes in which ghost-unitary implementations outperform direct implementations, with particular relevance to frequency-bin photonics where active linear-optical transformations can be relatively lossy. We also prove that, in the setting considered here, the direct implementation of the $n$-GHZ family delivers identically damped outputs: equivalent to the ideal state followed by uniform amplitude damping. This extends the known $n=2,3$ cases and provides a structural result of independent interest for photonic quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the theory of large deviations and gradient flows to systems exhibiting fractional diffusion, modeling particles undergoing Lévy flights. The authors derive the LDP for both independent and interacting particles, leading to a solvable stochastic PDE describing the system's fluctuations. This work provides a powerful mathematical toolset for analyzing anomalous transport in statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework connecting LDPs, gradient flows, and fractional dynamics provides powerful tools for analyzing non-Markovian or anomalous transport phenomena in complex many-body systems, which is relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a general understanding of large-scale fluctuations for particle systems governed by fractional diffusion, extending concepts from standard Brownian motion.

**Main result.** The authors derived the Large Deviation Principle (LDP) for both independent and mean-field interacting Lévy particles, leading to a generalized gradient flow structure and a solvable linear Stochastic PDE for fluctuations.

**Method.** The work combines formal derivations of LDPs, the identification of associated Hamiltonians, and the numerical solution of the resulting stochastic Partial Differential Equations (SPDEs).

**Model / system.** The system consists of $N$ particles undergoing Lévy flights (stable symmetric Lévy processes) with fractional order $\alpha \in (0, 2]$, which can be treated either independently or with mean-field interactions.

**Key observables.** The empirical measure $\mu_N(t)$, the rate function $I[\mu]$, and the fluctuation field $\eta(x, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fractional diffusion order $\alpha$, the particle density $\mu(x)$, and the system size $N$ (which dictates the speed of the LDP).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of the mean-field Hamiltonian $H_{mf}$ is noted as formal, and the analysis relies on the system exhibiting large-scale universality described by fractional diffusion.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the analysis involves deriving and comparing the SPDE solution with direct numerical integration of particle dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the LDP for independent particles, then extending this framework to mean-field interactions to derive $H_{mf}$, and finally deriving and solving the corresponding linear SPDE for the fluctuations $\eta$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work is a step towards a general understanding of fluctuations for systems of particles described at large scale by fractional diffusion. First, we derive formally a Large Deviation Principle for the empirical measure of mean-field interacting particles with a L{é}vy-type noise. In the case of independent particles, this unveils a generalized gradient flow structure at large scales. Second, we derive and solve numerically a stochastic Partial Differential Equation describing the fluctuating hydrodynamics of this system. The results compare well with direct numerical integration of the particles' dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports record-high mechanical quality factors in atomically thin $	ext{MoS}_2$ resonators, attributing the enhancement to a mechanism called 'dissipation dilution.' By combining advanced measurements with theoretical modeling, the authors establish that this dilution effect allows sub-nanometer vdW materials to function as platforms for ultrahigh-Q quantum sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses energy dissipation in nanoscale mechanical systems, which is crucial for coupling mechanical motion to quantum states (quantum transduction) and understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify the role of 'dissipation dilution' in achieving record-high mechanical quality factors (Q) in atomically thin van der Waals (vdW) resonators.

**Main result.** Record Q factors up to $372,000$ were achieved in monolayer $	ext{MoS}_2$ drums, establishing dissipation dilution as the governing mechanism for ultrahigh-Q mechanical response in vdW materials.

**Method.** The researchers combined experimental measurements of mechanical resonance (ringdown) with theoretical modeling based on elastic theory to analyze energy dissipation mechanisms.

**Model / system.** The physical system is suspended, circular drums made of 2D vdW materials like $	ext{MoS}_2$. The model analyzes the quality factor ($Q$) by decomposing it into intrinsic and dilution components, using specialized elastic theory for axisymmetric mode shapes.

**Key observables.** Quality factor ($Q$), fundamental resonance frequency ($f_0$), and the dilution factor ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (4 K), material thickness (monolayer vs. multilayer), and the degree of tensile stress/tension ($\sigma_{2D}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The intrinsic quality factor of monolayers is assumed to be limited by surface losses, while the dilution factor is modeled based on stored elastic energy contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares Q vs. frequency for various vdW materials; Figure 2 details the experimental setup and ringdown measurements for a monolayer drum, yielding a high Q value.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, develops a theoretical model for dissipation dilution in circular drums, presents experimental measurements on $	ext{MoS}_2$ monolayers and multilayers, analyzes the thickness dependence of $Q$, and concludes by establishing the mechanism's importance for quantum transduction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dissipation dilution has driven a revolution in the quality factors ($Q$) of top-down nanomechanical resonators. Yet it has not been leveraged for van der Waals (vdW) materials. Here we report quality factors up to $372{,}000\pm37{,}000$ in monolayer MoS$_2$ drums at 4 K, nearly an order of magnitude beyond the previous best for any vdW material at this temperature, enabled by a clean, polymer-free exfoliation technique. We develop a dissipation-dilution model for circular drums and analyze the contributing intrinsic and extrinsic dissipation mechanisms. The MoS$_2$ monolayer exhibits a dilution factor of order $1,000$, establishing sub-nanometer-thin resonators as ultimate dilution materials. The intrinsic quality factor, however, is limited by surface losses at atomic thickness. Conversely, multilayer drums show a lower dilution factor but a high intrinsic quality factor, on par with established top-down materials of similar dimensions, both resulting from the increased thickness. A two-level-system analysis of the temperature-dependent damping confirms this bulk-versus-surface picture and provides first insight into its microscopic origin. Our results identify dissipation dilution as the governing mechanism for high-$Q$ mechanical vdW resonators. Combined with strategies such as soft clamping or stress engineering, they establish atomically thin resonators as a platform for ultrahigh-$Q$ quantum transduction and sensing.

</details>

**Authors:** Michael G. Scheer, Eslam Khalaf  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18854v1>

**Summary.** This paper presents a sophisticated mathematical framework to derive rigorous lower bounds on the spectral gap for models describing strongly correlated electrons, specifically those related to the Fractional Quantum Hall Effect. It employs a bootstrap approach based on semidefinite programming applied to the Hamiltonian squared. The work provides a general, powerful technique for converting computationally derived bounds into mathematically proven results.

**Why it may be interesting.** The general methodology—turning numerical SDP bounds into rigorous proofs—is highly valuable for any theoretical physicist working on strongly correlated systems where exact spectral gap calculations are notoriously difficult.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to derive exact, rigorous lower bounds on the spectral gap of the Laughlin spectral gap Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The authors develop a general method using semidefinite programming (SDP) and advanced mathematical techniques to establish these bounds, achieving results numerically exact up to solver precision.

**Method.** The approach utilizes Knabe's observation ($H^2 \succeq \epsilon H$) and decomposes the Hamiltonian squared ($H^2$) into parts that are bounded analytically or via finite SDPs. Novel algorithms are introduced to convert numerical SDP bounds into rigorous mathematical proofs.

**Model / system.** The study concerns the spectral gap of the first Haldane pseudopotential Hamiltonian, which models interacting electrons in the lowest Landau level on a sphere with $N_\Phi$ flux quanta, relevant to the Fractional Quantum Hall Effect.

**Key observables.** The spectral gap ($\epsilon$), the lower bound on the gap, and the Hamiltonian itself.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Flux quanta ($N_\Phi$), filling fraction ($
u$), and cutoff parameters ($r', r$) that determine the size of the SDP.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on mapping the problem onto a structure suitable for $H^2 = K_1 + K_2$ decomposition, and later steps involve assuming the existence of limits and using symmetry averaging over SU(2).

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general principle of using SDPs on the spectral gap, applies this to the specific pseudopotential Hamiltonian via symmetry averaging, and culminates in developing rigorous algorithms to prove the derived numerical bounds in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a bootstrap approach to deriving lower bounds on the spectral gap of the first Haldane pseudopotential Hamiltonian on the sphere with $N_Φ$ flux quanta for arbitrary $N_Φ$, including in the thermodynamic limit $N_Φ\to \infty$. Our approach can in principle be generalized to pseudopotentials describing other model wavefunctions such as the Moore-Read state or Read-Rezayi sequence. Our approach is based on Knabe's observation that if $H$ is a Hamiltonian with ground state energy $0$ then the spectral gap is at least $ε> 0$ if and only if $H^2 \succeq εH$. To show this inequality, we split $H^2$ into two parts, one that can be analytically bounded and another that can be bounded using a finite semidefinite program (SDP). Crucially, the size of this program is determined only by cutoff parameters, and the program depends on $N_Φ$ only parametrically, allowing us to obtain numerically exact (up to solver precision) lower bounds on the gap. In order to turn these numerical bounds into mathematically rigorous bounds, we introduce an algorithm that can produce nearly-optimal exact feasible points for large rational SDPs. We additionally provide an algorithm that establishes the rank stability of the semidefinite program in the limit $N_Φ\to \infty$ which is needed to certify the bound in the thermodynamic limit. Beyond establishing the Laughlin gap and revealing a potential method for deriving spectral gap lower bounds for other pseudopotentials, our work provides a general method for turning numerical semidefinite programming bounds into rigorous mathematical proofs, which may have wide applicability.

</details>

### [Exact Microscopic One-Body Reduction in the Symmetric Exclusion Process](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18626v1)

**Authors:** Olivier Bénichou, Aurélien Grabsch  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18626v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

At random times, picked with unit rate, two neighboring integers are exchanged (red arrows). A configuration of the SEP is obtained by filling the sites hosting a label that was initially occupied by a particle (blue). Note that exchanging the labels of two occupied sites yield the same SEP configuration.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work solves a major problem in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics by showing that the complex, many-body correlation dynamics of the Symmetric Exclusion Process (SEP) simplify exactly. The infinite hierarchy of equations collapses onto a single, linear equation equivalent to a single random walker's persistence problem. This establishes a fundamental, microscopic one-body description for the system's behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that a complex, interacting many-body system reduces exactly to a one-body problem before any scaling limit is taken provides a powerful, general organizing principle for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in interacting particle systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of determining the correlation profile in the Symmetric Exclusion Process (SEP), which is governed by an intractable, infinite many-body hierarchy of equations.

**Main result.** They prove that the complex many-body problem reduces exactly to a simple, genuine one-body problem equivalent to a persistence problem for a single auxiliary random walker, even at finite time.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques involving the stirring representation, Bethe-ansatz calculations, and the use of Poisson transforms to derive a closed, linear equation for the correlation profile.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Symmetric Exclusion Process (SEP), a fundamental model for interacting particle transport on a lattice where particles undergo symmetric random walks subject to hard-core exclusion.

**Key observables.** The correlation profile $\omega_r(t; \lambda)$ and the $n$-particle propagators $\langle \eta_{r_1}(t) \cdots \eta_{r_n}(t) angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time ($t$), spatial lattice positions ($r$), and the coupling parameter $\lambda$. The analysis distinguishes between finite-time microscopic regimes and large-scale diffusive limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on extending the microscopic Bethe-ansatz calculation and involves complex mathematical manipulations like contour deformations and expansions in the large-scale limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the SEP as a stirring process, showing how the particle configuration is derived from the initial labeling of particles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the many-body hierarchy, then developing the microscopic tools (Bethe ansatz, determinantal representations) to derive a closed, linear equation, and finally demonstrating that this equation is equivalent to a single random walker's persistence problem.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The symmetric exclusion process (SEP) is a paradigmatic model of interacting particle transport. Its current--density correlations are governed by an infinite many-body hierarchy of equations. Only recently, this hierarchy was found to collapse at large scales onto a single closed equation. Is this remarkable simplification created by coarse graining, or does it reflect a deeper property already present in the microscopic dynamics? Here we show that the second scenario is realized. At arbitrary time on the lattice, the entire hierarchy collapses onto a single linear equation which is exactly equivalent to a persistence problem for a single auxiliary random walker. Thus an interacting many-body problem reduces exactly to a genuine one-body problem before any scaling limit is taken. This reduction determines the complete finite-time correlation profile, including during nonequilibrium relaxation. More broadly, the microscopic operator uncovered here opens a route to more general correlation functions in the SEP, and may point to a general organizing principle for nonequilibrium interacting particle systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies statistical mechanics, specifically using free-energy formalisms derived from Hopfield models, to analyze the Transformer attention mechanism. It treats attention as an emergent physical process governed by energy minimization, allowing researchers to map out phase diagrams. This provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding when and why attention systems fail or become unreliable.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep connection between attention mechanisms (a core component of modern AI) and established concepts from spin-glass theory and associative memory provides a powerful, physics-based lens for understanding AI reliability.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously characterize the statistical mechanics of the Transformer attention mechanism to understand its operational regimes and critical thresholds for reliable inference.

**Main result.** It establishes that attention emerges naturally from the free-energy minimization principle of a modern Hopfield model, allowing for the identification of first-order phase transitions between glassy and robust-inference states.

**Method.** The authors employ a free-energy formalism, utilizing concepts from statistical mechanics like Tsallis entropy and Fenchel-Young duality, to analyze the energy landscape of the system.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Transformer attention mechanism, which is mapped onto a Modern Hopfield Model (MHM). This model's dynamics are governed by a joint energy function involving query states and memory patterns.

**Key observables.** Free-energy landscape ($\Gamma_\alpha$), phase structure, coexistence points ($eta_{coexist}$), and spinodal points ($eta_{sp}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attention temperature ($eta$), memory load, and the entropy parameter ($\alpha$) used in the Tsallis entropy generalization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often restricts attention to the deterministic regime (zero variance) and relies on assumptions of uniform distribution and well-separation for quantitative validation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the derivation from microscopic fluctuations to macroscopic energy landscapes, and later show numerical validation of theoretical formulas for coexistence and spinodal points in two-key and general k-support systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from introducing the MHM and free-energy formalism, deriving key relationships via duality, and then validating the resulting theoretical predictions (coexistence/spinodal points) through numerical calculations on simplified memory systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the statistical mechanics of Transformer attention via modern Hopfield models. We present a free-energy formalism to characterize parameter regimes and critical thresholds for robust attention inference. The resulting free-energy landscape incorporates randomness in query states and attention weights. Attention then emerges as the equation of motion of the corresponding statistical-mechanical system (i.e., modern Hopfield model) through the standard free-energy minimization principle. This allows us to identify first-order phase transitions in attention between glassy and robust-inference phases. We further extend to attentions with stochastic queries and keys. Together, our results provide a rigorous statistical-mechanics framework for characterizing phase structure and inference reliability across a family of Transformer attention mechanisms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel, machine-verified framework in the Lean proof assistant to rigorously prove the security of Quantum Key Distribution protocols like BB84. By formalizing the entire process, including finite-size effects and LOCC constraints, the authors eliminate ambiguity in security assumptions. This establishes a reproducible, automated standard for security proofs in quantum cryptography.

**Why it may be interesting.** It demonstrates a powerful synergy between theoretical quantum information security and advanced formal verification tools (Lean), setting a new standard for rigorously proving quantum protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of establishing end-to-end, machine-verified security theorems for Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) protocols, specifically closing gaps in existing proofs due to mismatched assumptions.

**Main result.** The authors provide the first end-to-end, machine-verified composable finite-key security proof for the entanglement-based qubit BB84 protocol against coherent attacks.

**Method.** The core method involves formalizing the entire protocol and its security analysis within the Lean proof assistant, utilizing LLM-based autoformalization and a specialized Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC) language.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the theoretical security analysis of the entanglement-based qubit BB84 protocol, modeling quantum operations using Kraus maps and defining security via the diamond norm distance between real and ideal quantum channels.

**Key observables.** Security bound ($\epsilon_{	ext{QKD}}$), key rate (finite-size penalty), and the distinguishability between the real and ideal quantum channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite block size, coherence of attacks, and the robustness bound ($\delta_{	ext{rob}}$) related to the abort probability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security proof is fundamentally built upon the assumption of Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the AI-assisted proving workflow (AI agents $	o$ Lean kernel checking $	o$ manual review). Figure 2 details the machine-checked BB84 development using the LOCC construction.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a formal framework in Lean to model quantum protocols, applies this framework to the BB84 protocol to prove finite-key security, and discusses the integration of autoformalization pipelines to accelerate discovery.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum key distribution provides secure communication by relying on physics principles and mathematical reasoning. Security proofs across the field can contain gaps, often between individually valid theorems integrated under mismatched assumptions. We produce an end-to-end security theorem formalized in the Lean proof assistant, eliminating hidden hypotheses by building only on Lean's standard axioms. Our proof covers composable security of the entanglement-based qubit BB84 protocol against coherent attacks by the postselection technique, accounting for finite block size, and is written in a language that enforces local operations and classical communication to help ensure a faithful representation of the protocol. The proof itself was enabled by LLM-based autoformalization, and with the proof complete, we used the same pipeline as a discovery machine, reducing the finite-size penalty on the key rate. Our work sets a reproducible standard for security arguments in quantum cryptography and shows how formal proof engines can accelerate discovery across quantum information science.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical proof demonstrating that simulated annealing efficiently samples the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick spin glass model up to the critical temperature $eta < 1$. The authors achieve this by establishing key spectral properties, such as the weak Poincaré inequality, using advanced techniques involving stochastic localization and covariance bounding.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical spin glasses, the techniques developed—such as using spectral gaps, Poincaré inequalities, and localization schemes to prove mixing times—are foundational tools applicable to analyzing quantum dynamics in complex, disordered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper focuses on developing rigorous mathematical tools to prove efficient sampling from the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model, particularly near the critical inverse temperature $eta < 1$.

**Main result.** They prove that the simulated annealing algorithm can sample the SK model with an error bounded by $N^{-a}$ in total variation distance for all $eta < 1$.

**Method.** The proof relies on establishing the weak Poincaré inequality and bounding the covariance matrix of the model's measure using stochastic localization techniques.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model, an Ising spin glass model defined by a Hamiltonian involving Gaussian random couplings $W \sim 	ext{GOE}(N)$.

**Key observables.** Total Variation Distance (dTV), Weak Poincaré Inequality constant, Covariance matrix ($	ext{Cov}_{\mu_{eta W}}$), and various derivatives of order parameters ($\lambda(B), r(B)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature $eta$ (studied for $0 \le eta < 1$), System size $N$, and the accuracy parameter $a$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often requires shifting to a more tractable 'planted model' and relies on bounding expectations of norms of tensors using Gaussian moment estimates.

**Paper structure.** The development builds from establishing covariance bounds (Theorem 1.3) to proving the weak Poincaré inequality (Theorem 1.2), which finally enables the main result regarding efficient sampling via simulated annealing (Theorem 1.1).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the simulated annealing algorithm efficiently samples from the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model at inverse temperature $β< 1$ to an arbitrary inverse polynomial accuracy in total variation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'scheduled trotterization,' a variable-weight modification to standard quantum simulation algorithms. It rigorously proves that by adaptively scheduling the local pulse weights, the global error in approximating time evolution can be improved from $O(N^{-1})$ to $O(N^{-2})$. This offers a powerful theoretical tool for designing more accurate quantum simulation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, mathematically advanced framework for optimizing quantum simulation algorithms, directly impacting the achievable accuracy in simulating complex quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately simulating quantum time evolution using product formulas (Trotterization) when the available local generators are limited.

**Main result.** By introducing a variable-weight, scheduled trotterization scheme, the global approximation error can be significantly reduced from the standard $O(N^{-1})$ scaling to $O(N^{-2})$ for exact synthesis.

**Method.** The analysis uses the geometry of the Lie group, expressing the global error as a product of transported local defects analyzed via the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.

**Model / system.** The model involves approximating a target unitary evolution $U = \exp(itH)$ using a sequence of local pulses generated by a finite set of available Hamiltonians $\{H_j\}$ in $SU(d)$.

**Key observables.** Global endpoint error ($\|U - W\|$); local defects; internal and external remainder terms ($R_{	ext{int}}, R_{	ext{ext}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of steps ($N$); error scaling ($\delta, \epsilon$); local covering parameter (for imperfect synthesis).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the validity of BCH expansions under small norm conditions and assumes the target Hamiltonian lies in the real span of the available generators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares fixed-weight ($O(N^{-1})$) vs. variable-weight ($O(N^{-2})$) scaling. Figure 2 illustrates the geometric path difference between ideal and simulated evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the error analysis inductively, first establishing the general error bound using BCH expansions, then deriving the improved $O(N^{-2})$ bound for exact synthesis by defining an adaptive schedule, and finally extending the framework to imperfect synthesis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Trotterization is a widely used algorithm for simulating quantum evolutions and consists of approximating a target unitary through a local linear decomposition of its generator that is repeated over $N$ steps. We introduce {scheduled trotterization}, a variable-weight modification in which the coefficients of the local linear decomposition are allowed to change from step to step. Using the geometry of the Lie group of unitary operators, we express the global endpoint error as a product of transported local defects, whose leading terms are obtained by two applications of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, one inside each local step and one between different transported defects, separating the internal Trotter error from the external accumulation error. While we recover recent tighter bounds on the global approximation error when the weights are held fixed, the new framework allows us to propose a weight-varying schedule that significantly decreases the global error. For exact infinitesimal synthesis, we prove that this improves the usual fixed-weight $O(N^{-1})$ behavior to $O(N^{-2})$, without changing the basic pulse skeleton. We also extend the framework to imperfect infinitesimal synthesis, as arises in quantum gate synthesis problems, where admissible coefficients may only approximate the desired local corrections. In this case, the error is controlled by a local covering parameter measuring the quality of the available coefficient set. Numerical experiments for random $\SU(2)$ targets further support the predicted theoretical improvements. The results suggest a novel perspective for the analysis of Trotter errors, which can be treated as schedulable quantities to be coherently canceled at the endpoint.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes search processes in a 1D harmonic trap subjected to stochastic resetting with a finite-time return phase. By deriving the MFPT and using the CV criterion, the authors map out the parameter space where this stochastic return mechanism significantly enhances search efficiency over simpler instantaneous return models. The results offer concrete, experimentally testable guidelines for optimizing search protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The analysis of non-Markovian or finite-time return dynamics (stochastic return) provides a crucial refinement to standard resetting models, directly bridging theoretical predictions to measurable optical trap experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates first-passage processes under stochastic resetting with stochastic return (SR) protocols to understand how this mechanism controls search efficiency compared to instantaneous return (IR).

**Main result.** The most beneficial regime for SR occurs when the potential strength in the search phase ($k_1$) is large and the potential strength in the return phase ($k_2$) is small. The work provides experimentally testable predictions.

**Method.** The authors analytically derive the Mean First-Passage Time (MFPT) and utilize the coefficient-of-variation (CV) criterion to construct phase diagrams comparing SR efficiency against the underlying dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model considers a one-dimensional diffusing particle confined in a harmonic trap, undergoing a search phase (stiffness $k_1$) followed by a return phase (stiffness $k_2$) after stochastic resetting.

**Key observables.** Mean First-Passage Time (MFPT), Search efficiency enhancement/suppression (via CV criterion), Comparison between SR and IR efficiency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential stiffnesses ($k_1, k_2$), resetting rate ($r$), and the target location ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are modeled using an overdamped Langevin process (Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process) in both phases, and the return phase allows for target detection.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates three search scenarios: direct success, target detection during return, or return to origin followed by a new search. Figure 2 shows a phase-space diagram mapping parameter regimes where SR is beneficial.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for SR dynamics, derives the MFPT, applies the CV criterion to map the parameter space, and finally tests these theoretical predictions against parameters reported in existing experimental studies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stochastic resetting with stochastic return (SR) has recently emerged as an effective and practically relevant strategy for controlling first-passage phenomena and optimizing search processes by significantly reducing completion times in a wide class of stochastic systems. While most theoretical studies assume instantaneous return (IR), in realistic systems the return to the resetting position typically occurs over a finite time. We consider a 1-d diffusing particle in a harmonic trap with stiffnesses $k_1$ and $k_2$ during search and return, respectively. The particle searches for the target and, upon resetting at rate $r$, enters the return phase, continuing until it reaches either the origin or the absorbing target at $L>0$. Notably, the model permits target detection throughout the return phase, distinguishing it from IR scenarios. We analytically derive the MFPT for our model and determine parameter regions where SR enhances or suppresses search efficiency over the underlying dynamics, constructing the corresponding phase diagram using the coefficient of variation criterion. We further identify regimes in which SR is more efficient than IR using the SR criterion. This behavior is tested across different parameter sets taken from experimental studies by Goerlich et al., Biroli et al., and Besga et al. We map the parameter values reported in existing experimental studies onto our theoretical phase diagram, thereby identifying the regimes in which SR outperforms IR. In particular, we find that the most relevant region of the phase space, where SR is most beneficial corresponds to larger values of the potential strength in the search phase and lower values of potential in the return phase. Our results provide experimentally testable predictions and bridge the gap between theoretical resetting models and optical trap mediated finite-time return dynamics.

</details>

**Authors:** Rishabh Batra  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18799v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>

**Summary.** This paper proves a fundamental equivalence in quantum cryptography, showing that EFI pairs are mathematically equivalent to expanding single-copy pseudorandom state generators (1PRS). By removing non-uniformity from previous work, the authors provide the first uniform characterization of this relationship. This result establishes a deep quantum analogue to classical cryptographic theorems linking one-way functions and pseudorandomness.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the rigorous mathematical tools developed for bounding quantum entropy and proving computational indistinguishability are foundational to understanding the limits of quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To prove the uniform equivalence between Efficiently Formed Information (EFI) pairs and expanding single-copy pseudorandom state generators (1PRS).

**Main result.** The authors establish the first uniform meta-complexity characterization showing that EFI pairs exist if and only if expanding 1PRS exist, providing a quantum analogue to classical cryptographic equivalences.

**Method.** The proof adapts the random-start technique and uses entropy chain rules to remove non-uniformity from prior constructions, relating the existence of quantum states to computational hardness assumptions.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted using quantum states (pure states) within the framework of quantum cryptography primitives like EFI pairs and 1PRS.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entropy, algorithmic entropy, trace distance, and the entropy surplus.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Security parameter $\lambda$, output/key lengths ($n, r$), and the stretch factor.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof builds upon prior results regarding non-uniform equivalence and relies on the random-start idea holding for quantum side information.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the equivalence relationships between EFI, statistically verifiable one-way state generators (svOWSG), quantum commitments, and 1PRS.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the equivalence ($	ext{EFI} \Leftrightarrow 	ext{1PRS}$) using advanced entropy analysis, culminating in a theorem that provides the uniform meta-complexity characterization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A series of recent works has established EFI pairs as the "minimal" assumption for quantum cryptography. Recently, Cavalar et al. [CCC+26] showed that EFI pairs are equivalent to non-uniform expanding single-copy pseudorandom state generators (1PRS), and obtained a non-uniform meta-complexity characterization of EFI pairs. We remove the non-uniformity in their construction and prove that EFI pairs are equivalent to expanding 1PRS. As a corollary, we obtain the first uniform meta-complexity characterization of EFI pairs. Together with the equivalence of statistically verifiable one-way state generators and commitments proved by Batra and Jain [BJ24], this gives a quantum analogue of the classical equivalence between one-way functions, commitments, and pseudorandom generators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel construction for synthesizing arbitrary $n$-qubit unitaries using Clifford+T circuits, achieving a T-gate complexity of $\widetilde{O}(N^{8/7}L^{3/7}+NL)$. The authors improve upon previous bounds and rigorously prove that the $N^{8/7}$ scaling is optimal within the sequential block-SELECT model, advancing the theoretical understanding of quantum circuit resource requirements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the fundamental resource cost of quantum computation, providing state-of-the-art bounds on gate synthesis complexity, which is crucial for designing practical quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Determining the minimum number of T gates required to implement an arbitrary n-qubit unitary circuit efficiently.

**Main result.** The authors achieve a T-gate count of $\widetilde{O}(N^{8/7}L^{3/7}+NL)$ and prove that the exponent $8/7$ is optimal in the worst case for the sequential block-SELECT model.

**Method.** The approach simulates the target unitary via its Hermitian logarithm, flattening the generator using random similarity transformations, and combining the resulting block-sparse evolutions using the qDRIFT technique.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation using Clifford+T circuits to realize arbitrary $n$-qubit unitaries $U \in U(2n)$. The resource measure is the T-gate count, as T-gates are non-Clifford and costly.

**Key observables.** T-gate count, clean ancilla count, and the approximation error $\epsilon$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** N (total qubits, $2^n$), L ($\log_2(N/\epsilon)$), and the exponent $8/7$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of clean ancillas, and the lower bound proof is restricted to the sequential block-SELECT model.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates matrix partitioning for $N=8$. Figure 2 compares schematic table access for dense vs. separate piece encodings. Figure 3 and 4 show the assignment and structure of blocks for Hamiltonian simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the construction by first establishing the simulation framework (using qDRIFT and block encoding) and then deriving the optimal complexity bounds by analyzing the required resources in the sequential block-SELECT model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct Clifford+$T$ circuits implementing arbitrary $n$-qubit unitaries with worst-case $T$-count $\widetilde{O}(N^{8/7})$ at constant operator-norm error, where $N=2^n$, improving the previous exponent $5/4$. The construction uses $\widetilde{O}(N^{6/7})$ clean ancillas. The main idea is to reduce the data addressed by each query to the block-encoding circuit for a sparse piece while controlling the additional simulation work needed to combine the pieces. We simulate a Hermitian logarithm of the target by flattening its traceless part with a two-layer random similarity transformation, randomly partitioning the transformed generator into block-sparse Hermitian pieces, and combining their evolutions using qDRIFT. For a target operator-norm error $ε\in(0,1/2)$, let $L=\log_2(N/ε)$, the qubit count plus the requested bits of accuracy. By successively correcting the approximation error, we obtain a circuit using $\widetilde{O}(N^{8/7}L^{3/7}+NL)$ $T$ gates and $\widetilde{O}(N)$ clean ancillas whenever $L\le N$. We also prove that, at constant precision, the exponent $8/7$ is optimal within a sequential block-SELECT model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates whether using Riemannian geometry to optimize individual gates in VQE circuits improves performance over standard optimization methods. Through rigorous testing on a hydrogen-chain benchmark, the authors report a controlled null result, finding no practical advantage. The main contribution is establishing a detailed validation checklist to guide future, more reliable comparisons of quantum optimizers.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial, highly technical benchmark and validation checklist for the entire field of quantum machine learning optimization, helping researchers avoid misleading positive results.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To rigorously test whether optimizing variational quantum circuits using per-gate Riemannian optimization offers a practical advantage over standard classical optimizers like Adam.

**Main result.** The study concludes a controlled null result, finding no measurable practical advantage for Riemannian optimization over a matched Euler-angle Adam baseline on a validated hydrogen-chain benchmark.

**Method.** The authors compare Riemannian optimization techniques (using various retractions and metrics) against a classical Adam baseline applied to the same physical gates, using a rigorous validation protocol.

**Model / system.** The model is the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) applied to neutral hydrogen chains. The ansatz involves trainable single-qubit U(2) gates followed by a fixed CNOT entangler.

**Key observables.** Geometric mean best energy error (|E - \lambda_min|) and success rate at chemical accuracy (1.6 x 10^-3 Ha). Diagnostics include Schmidt rank and half-chain entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is restricted to the clean per-gate U(2) setting, excluding noisy hardware or large-block compilation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is the scope: the result is only valid for the specific per-gate U(2) regime tested, and the paper provides a checklist for future comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the performance of Euler-coordinate Adam versus direct per-gate manifold Adam, while tables summarize performance metrics and list four identified failure modes that can create false positives.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem, details the Riemannian optimization methods and baselines, presents results on the hydrogen-chain benchmark showing no advantage, and concludes by detailing four failure modes and proposing a validation checklist for future work.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parametrized gates in a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) live on a product of unitary groups, which makes Riemannian optimization a natural candidate for improving training. We test this candidate in the clean per-gate setting: each single-qubit gate is optimized directly on U (2) and compared with Adam on an unconstrained Euler-angle chart representing the same physical gate, initialized from the same unitaries and tuned with a separate learning-rate sweep. On a validated hydrogen-chain benchmark, we find no measurable practical advantage from the Riemannian machinery. The direct manifold optimizer and the Euler-coordinate baseline are interleaved in final error and success rate; the classical coordinate baseline is not slower in iterations on the successful cells; and the choices of Riemannian metric, retraction, and momentum vector transport do not change the conclusion. We also identify four failure modes that can manufacture an apparent positive result: a trivial product-state benchmark, a projected-matrix Adam baseline that discards most of its own step, a non-unitary inherited Cayley retraction that lets the reported energy pass below the variational minimum, and a learning-rate grid whose optimum sits at its boundary. Each failure mode produced a plausible multi-seed result in our own workflow before being caught. The practical output of this paper is therefore a controlled null result and a validation checklist for VQE optimizer comparisons.

</details>

**Authors:** Samuel Bevins

**Summary.** This paper derives a new, improved upper bound ($3/8$) for the stabilizer-rank exponent associated with magic states. The authors achieve this by developing a sophisticated construction that combines existing stabilizer identities recursively. This tighter bound provides a more precise measure of the resource cost for simulating quantum circuits involving these specific quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts the understanding of resource quantification for quantum simulation, which is crucial for designing efficient quantum algorithms and understanding the limits of classical simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine a tighter upper bound on the stabilizer-rank exponent ($\gamma_T$) for magic states, which governs the resource cost for simulating quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The authors establish a new upper bound of $\gamma_T \le 3/8$ for the exponent, improving upon the previous bound of $\log_2(3)/4$.

**Method.** The improvement is achieved through an explicit construction involving symmetry gluing and a recursive substitution method applied to the stabilizer identities.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the tensor product of qubit face-center magic states, $|	ext{T}angle^{\otimes m}$, and their associated stabilizer rank.

**Key observables.** The exact complex stabilizer rank $\chi(|\psiangle)$ and the stabilizer-rank exponent $\gamma_T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponent $\gamma_T$, which is compared against the previously known value $\log_2(3)/4$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is an upper bound and does not determine the optimal exponent or establish a lower bound.

**Figures summary.** Table I shows the exact twenty-four-word identity for $v^{\otimes 6}$, and Table II provides a coordinate check for the seven orbits.

**Paper structure.** The argument progresses by using a three-word identity for $v^{\otimes 2}$ and iteratively applying a linear map $E$ to generate the bound for $v^{\otimes (2+4j)}$, building upon symmetry gluing techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an explicit construction proving that the exact stabilizer rank of $m$ copies of the qubit face-center magic state is $O(2^{3m/8})$. Two symmetry-related fifteen-term identities combine into a twenty-four-term decomposition at twelve copies. The balanced structure of this decomposition yields a substitution that adds eight copies at a cost of eight terms per existing term. The resulting bound $γ_T \leq 3/8$ improves the previously published exponent $\log_2(3)/4$.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17836v1>

</figure>

**Summary.** This tutorial analyzes how 'data re-uploading' enriches amplitude-embedded quantum models by repeatedly encoding classical input features. The work demonstrates that each upload increases the model's polynomial expressivity, allowing for better fitting of complex functions. Crucially, it advises practitioners on matching the number of uploads to the inherent polynomial order of the task to maximize performance while managing hardware error budgets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum machine learning, the rigorous analysis of model expressivity and the quantification of resource trade-offs (capacity vs. gate error) provides general insights into designing complex quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to explain the functional gain provided by data re-uploading techniques within amplitude-embedded variational quantum models and to quantify this performance enhancement.

**Main result.** Re-uploading increases the reachable polynomial order by two for each upload, leading to significant performance gains on high-order targets, although one upload is optimal for low-order tasks like quadratic fitting.

**Method.** The authors analyze sequential and parallel re-uploading schemes by comparing the resulting polynomial expressivity and measuring performance using the test coefficient of determination on synthetic and image datasets.

**Model / system.** The model is a variational quantum circuit utilizing amplitude embedding to process classical features. Re-uploading involves repeatedly encoding the input state between trainable blocks to increase model capacity.

**Key observables.** Test coefficient of determination ($R^2$), reachable polynomial order, and the gate error rate the classifier can tolerate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of uploads (L or k), the polynomial order of the target function, and the two-qubit gate error rate ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a fixed budget of trainable parameters and that the gain from re-uploading must be known beforehand; the training itself is assumed noiseless for error analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit structure for re-uploading (sequential vs. parallel) and show performance gains ($R^2$) on targets of order 4 and 6, alongside how gate error tolerance decreases with more uploads.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces amplitude embedding and the concept of re-uploading. It then theoretically analyzes how sequential and parallel re-uploading raise the polynomial order. Finally, it presents simulation results comparing performance gains and error tolerance across different upload counts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Amplitude embedding lets a variational quantum model read many classical features with few qubits, but the model output is then only a quadratic form in the normalized input. Data re-uploading, which encodes the input repeatedly between trainable blocks, can be used to enrich such a model. Because every additional upload of an amplitude embedding repeats a costly state preparation, the gain that an upload buys has to be known before the gates are spent. This tutorial thus addresses the problem of explaining what data re-uploading adds to an amplitude-embedded model and how large the gain is. We express sequential re-uploading and parallel re-uploading with two copies in one notation and show that each upload raises the reachable polynomial order by two. Under one trainable circuit family and one budget of trainable parameters, simulation results show that re-uploading raises the test coefficient of determination from 0.689 to between 0.883 and 0.975 on targets of order four and from 0.337 to between 0.642 and 0.783 on targets of order six, on all ten seeds. On a quadratic target and on handwritten digits, one upload remains the best choice, and every additional upload lowers the gate error that the classifier tolerates. These results help designers match the number of uploads to the polynomial order of the task at hand.

</details>

**Authors:** Mert Gökduman, Cyril Guyot, Henry D. Pfister  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17520v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

The p-bit decoder acts on the DEM obtained by retaining the X-error component, while the correlated p-bit decoder acts on the full undecomposed DEM. Reference curves: Cascade [38], Tesseract [39], Relay-BP [21], impulse decoding [24], and BP+OSD [8].</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel decoding algorithm for QLDPC codes called constraint-annealed p-bit decoding. It uses stochastic sampling and parallel updates to efficiently search for error corrections. The method proves to be a competitive alternative to message-passing decoders for achieving fault tolerance in quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of stochastic dynamics (p-bits) and annealing techniques to solve a hard constraint satisfaction problem (syndrome decoding) provides an interesting analogy to non-equilibrium statistical physics methods applied to quantum error correction.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient and robust decoder for Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (QLDPC) codes, which are essential for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The proposed constraint-annealed p-bit decoder achieves logical error rates competitive with leading decoders under both code-capacity and circuit-level noise models.

**Method.** The decoder employs a stochastic search strategy using probabilistic bits (p-bits) and combines this with constraint annealing and parallel updates via graph coloring.

**Model / system.** The system involves QLDPC codes defined by a parity-check matrix H, and the decoding process is modeled using tunable stochastic binary units (p-bits) to find error corrections.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates, block failure rates, and the minimum Hamming weight of the found candidate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The annealing strength parameter (beta), the number of replicas (R), and the update budget (K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison is made at explicitly stated update budgets, and the method relies on the assumption that stochastic sampling can efficiently explore the solution space.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare block failure rates vs. bit-flip rate (code-capacity noise) and logical error rates vs. physical error rate (circuit noise) against established decoders.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the p-bit framework, details the constraint-annealed Gibbs sampling process, and evaluates its performance against state-of-the-art decoders under various noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a parallel decoder for quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes based on probabilistic bits (p-bits), tunable stochastic binary units controlled by local fields. The idea is that predicted error configurations, interpreted as explanations of the observed syndrome, are updated stochastically using local Gibbs conditionals, while a progressively strengthened parity penalty drives the search toward syndrome-valid configurations. A coloring of the decoding graph enables parallel block updates, and independent replicas and restarts provide trajectory diversity. The best candidate encountered during the search is returned, mirroring the candidate-selection strategy of recent ensemble decoders. At the stated update budgets, the resulting logical error rates are on par with leading decoders under both code-capacity and circuit-level noise. While the total number of variable resamplings can be substantially larger than the message-update count of BP-based alternatives, the numerical results establish constraint-annealed p-bit dynamics as a competitive stochastic alternative for QLDPC decoding and motivate further study of hardware-native implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the first successful growth of single crystals of $	ext{Cr}_{1/4}	ext{NbS}_2$, a material proposed to be an altermagnet. By measuring its structural, thermal, and transport properties, the authors confirm its magnetic ordering temperature and observe transport signatures consistent with quantum diffusive behavior, establishing it as a key platform for altermagnetism research.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation of altermagnetism in layered TMDs connects concepts of magnetic ordering, electronic structure, and transport phenomena, which are central themes in strongly correlated electron systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally investigate altermagnetism, a magnetic order challenging the simple ferromagnetic/antiferromagnetic classification, in the candidate material $	ext{Cr}_{1/4}	ext{NbS}_2$.

**Main result.** The authors successfully grew high-quality single crystals of $	ext{Cr}_{1/4}	ext{NbS}_2$, confirming its hexagonal structure and observing signatures of quantum diffusive transport in magnetotransport measurements.

**Method.** The study employed a combination of techniques, including single-crystal X-ray diffraction, heat capacity measurements, and magnetotransport measurements on single crystals.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Cr}_{1/4}	ext{NbS}_2$, a transition metal-intercalated transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) proposed to exhibit altermagnetism. Its structure is confirmed to be hexagonal ($P6_3/mmc$), which is crucial for its magnetic properties.

**Key observables.** Antiferromagnetic Néel temperature ($T_N \sim 141 	ext{ K}$), non-trivial magnetoresistance and Hall response, and hexagonal crystal symmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperatures ranging from 1.8 K to 300 K; magnetic fields up to 9 T.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that the observed magnetic and transport features are indicative of the predicted altermagnetic behavior, and that single-crystal measurements are necessary to probe anisotropic properties.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) shows the grown single crystals, and Figure 1(b) confirms the six-fold symmetry using Laue back-scattering diffraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard experimental structure: introduction establishing the need to study altermagnets, detailed characterization of the single crystals (structural, thermal, magnetic), and finally, transport measurements to confirm the predicted quantum phenomena.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism is a rapidly emerging paradigm in condensed matter magnetism, yet the number of experimentally confirmed altermagnetic candidates remains remarkably limited. Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$ has long been theoretically proposed as a promising altermagnet, but there are no reports on experimental investigation of single crystals. Here, we report the successful growth of high-quality single crystals of Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$ and present its structural, thermal, magnetic, and transport properties. Single-crystal X-ray diffraction provides detailed crystallographic information and confirms the hexagonal crystal symmetry ($P6_3/mmc$), one of the criteria for altermagnetic behavior. Resistivity and heat capacity measurements unambiguously identify an antiferromagnetic Néel temperature of $T_N \sim 141$ K. Magnetotransport measurements reveal non-trivial features in the magnetoresistance and Hall response, indicative of quantum diffusive transport phenomena. Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$, synthesized here for the first time in single-crystalline form, therefore serves as an experimental platform for probing the rich spectrum of phenomena predicted for altermagnetic materials.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18130v1>

A BB code is processed by two encoders; The polynomial encoder applies self-attention to monomial tokens and is conditioned on the code parameters through FiLM layers. In parallel, the Tanner spectral encoder processes Fourier-character tokens using attention as well. Each branch produces a code latent representation, which is combined through a learned gated residual connection. The fused representation then passed to a classification head.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.18130_figures/2610.18130_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Figure 3: BB-Net tokenization and embedding. A BB code specified by sparse polynomials A and B is mapped to two complementary representations: (i) polynomial tokens encode monomial supports on Zℓ× Zm with the type (A,B) indicator as a one-hot vector and the first eight Fourier harmonics, represented by sine and cosine components along each lattice dimension, yielding a 32-dimensional positional representation. (ii) Tanner spectral tokens encode the responses of the full polynomials at each group character χ using 18-dimensional character features. Learned CLS tokens are used as latent vector aggregators for the downstream classification task.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.18130_figures/2610.18130_fig4.jpg" alt="Figure 4">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> Figure 4: The x-axis denotes the minimum distance threshold t, and the y-axis reports the accuracy computed over codes whose distance satisfies d ≥t. Thus, moving to the right restricts the evalua- tion to increasingly high-distance codes. This was examined on three subsets: the whole benchmark, certified exact distances, and upper bound, unresolved distances.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.18130_figures/2610.18130_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 5: Bold curves denote the best configuration, including augmentation, shift canonicalisation, and FiLM conditioning; lighter curves show the corresponding model with the component named in each panel removed.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the computational bottleneck in finding the minimum distance of quantum error-correcting codes, particularly qLDPC codes. The authors introduce BB-Net, an attention-based neural network that learns to predict this critical distance metric. By successfully automating this calculation, BB-Net accelerates the search for novel, high-performance quantum codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum codes, the application of advanced ML architectures (attention mechanisms, spectral analysis) to solve a hard combinatorial problem is relevant to developing predictive tools for complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is the computationally hard problem of determining the minimum distance ($d$) of quantum error-correcting codes, which dictates their fault-tolerance capability.

**Main result.** The proposed neural network, BB-Net, achieves high accuracy in predicting the code distance, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art heuristics while drastically reducing computational time.

**Method.** The authors developed BB-Net, an attention-based neural network that processes the algebraic structure of Bivariate Bicycle (BB) codes using two complementary token sets: polynomial and Tanner spectral representations.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (qLDPC) codes, specifically the Bivariate Bicycle (BB) family, which are used for quantum error correction (QEC). The distance $d$ is the minimum weight of a nontrivial logical operator.

**Key observables.** The minimum distance ($d$) of the quantum code, and the prediction accuracy (e.g., 96.39% on the test set).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters ($\ell, m, n, k$), the computational budget (comparing $10^4$ times runtime differences), and the structure of the stabilizer checks ($H_X, H_Z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The distance calculation is NP-hard, necessitating the use of ML prediction as a practical alternative to exhaustive search.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare distance estimation times (heuristic vs. BB-Net) and detail the BB-Net architecture, showing the polynomial and spectral encoding branches. Tables present performance metrics and ablation study results.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the computational bottleneck of distance finding, introduces the BB-Net architecture utilizing polynomial and spectral tokenization, trains the network on heuristic-derived data, and validates its superior performance in predicting code distances.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The distance of a quantum code is a fundamental metric that dictates its error-correction capability. While quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) families such as Bivariate Bicycle (BB) codes offer a promising pathway to low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computing, computing their distance is challenging. Current exact solvers and even search heuristics that provide a distance upper bound suffer from severe computational bottlenecks, restricting the discovery of optimal codes. In this paper, we construct a dataset of BB codes with corresponding distance upper bounds obtained through high-budget heuristic searches. We introduce BB-Net, an attention-based neural network architecture for predicting the distance of BB codes. It achieves 96.39% accuracy on the reference distances in the test set, compared with 94.86% for state-of-the-art heuristics with a budget of up to $10^{4}$ times more computational runtime. On a subset with certified exact distances, BB-Net achieves 98.94%. By relieving the distance-calculation bottleneck, BB-Net enables faster screening of large code spaces, particularly in ML-driven search pipelines, and thereby can accelerate the discovery of novel quantum error-correcting codes, an important capability for advancing scalable fault-tolerant quantum computing. Our code is publicly available: https://github.com/NadavSc/BBNet

</details>
