**Summary.** This paper rigorously justifies the use of spin-wave theory for describing low-energy quantum dynamics in the 1D Heisenberg ferromagnet. By analyzing the difference between the exact and free time evolution for two-magnon wave packets, the authors establish a quantitative bound on the approximation error. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding magnetic excitations in quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical foundation for a standard approximation (spin-wave theory) used extensively in condensed matter physics to study magnetic excitations, which is crucial for understanding many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to provide a rigorous mathematical justification for using spin-wave theory to describe the low-energy quantum dynamics of the one-dimensional Heisenberg ferromagnet.

**Main result.** The authors establish a quantitative bound showing that the difference between the exact time evolution and the free evolution of two-magnon wave packets decays according to a specific functional form involving time and system size.

**Method.** The analysis combines techniques from integrable systems, functional analysis, and harmonic analysis, focusing on bounding the norm difference between the exact and approximate time evolution operators.

**Model / system.** The system is the spin-1/2 quantum Heisenberg ferromagnet on a 1D lattice. The dynamics are governed by the Hamiltonian $H_{\Lambda} = \sum_{\langle x,yangle \subset \Lambda} (S^2 - \mathbf{S}_x \cdot \mathbf{S}_y)$, and the focus is on low-energy excitations (magnons) in the two-magnon sector.

**Key observables.** The norm distance $\lVert \Psi_t^K - \Psi_{t, 	ext{free}}^K Vert^2_{\mathcal{H}_\Lambda}$ between the exact and free time evolutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed in the thermodynamic limit ($|\Lambda| 	o \infty$), for small momentum spread ($\epsilon$), and restricted to the two-magnon sector.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial state must satisfy specific constraints related to the momentum spread $\epsilon$, and the result is restricted to the two-magnon subspace.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the rigorous bound by analyzing the difference between the exact and free evolution, utilizing the integrable structure of the two-magnon sector and employing advanced mathematical tools like the Periodic Discrete Hilbert Transform.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin wave theory plays a central role in the analysis of low-energy properties of the quantum ferromagnetic Heisenberg model. In this work, we present a first step towards a rigorous justification of this approximation in the context of quantum dynamics in one dimension. According to spin wave theory, the evolution of a spin wave state should be well approximated by its free evolution with the appropriate energy of free quasiparticles called magnons. Our main result establishes this idea rigorously for a class of spin wave packets. We focus on spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ systems, and consider wave packets composed of two magnons. We argue our result is optimal with respect to the timescales.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives exact formulas for the fundamental capacities (quantum, entanglement, private, and key) achievable in quantum communication over lossy, multimode channels. By imposing finite-energy constraints, the authors show that these different capacities are equal and provide explicit rules for optimally allocating limited photon resources across different spectral modes. This work is vital for advancing the theory and engineering of energy-efficient quantum optical communication systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, energy-constrained benchmarks for quantum communication protocols, which is crucial for designing practical quantum networks using optical fiber or free-space links where loss and fading are inevitable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the exact two-way quantum and secret-key capacities for quantum communication tasks over multimode bosonic loss and fading channels.

**Main result.** The authors establish that under finite-energy constraints, the quantum, entanglement, private, and secret-key capacities all coincide, yielding closed-form expressions dependent on the channel loss and energy budget.

**Method.** The analysis employs techniques like sector teleportation, finite-energy converse arguments, and optimization calculus (Lagrange multipliers) to find the optimal photon allocation across multiple modes.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum communication through multimode bosonic loss and fading channels, which can be extended to continuous frequency-dependent loss under a power constraint.

**Key observables.** Two-way quantum capacity ($Q_2$), entanglement distribution capacity ($D_2$), private capacity ($P_2$), and secret-key capacity ($K_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean photon-number constraint ($N$) or mean input power ($P$); channel transmissivities ($\eta_j$ or $\eta(\omega)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the availability of local quantum operations and two-way classical communication, and it distinguishes between observed and unobserved fading realizations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a three-mode example comparing the exact capacity against two feasible allocations and the derived optimal allocation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from discrete multimode loss channels to continuous frequency-dependent loss, establishing capacity identities ($Q_2=D_2=P_2=K_2$) and deriving optimal energy allocation rules for both finite and continuous settings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study quantum communication, entanglement distribution, and secret-key generation through multimode bosonic loss and fading channels, assisted by local quantum operations and two-way classical communication. Using sector teleportation, we determine the exact two-way quantum and secret-key capacities of multimode pure-loss channels under a mean photon-number constraint, allowing arbitrary adaptive quantum protocols. We extend these results to continuous frequency-dependent loss under a power constraint and identify the optimal allocation of photons among optical modes. For constant loss over an unlimited bandwidth, we obtain a closed-form capacity proportional to the square root of power. For randomly varying loss, we determine exact finite-energy capacities when the channel realization is publicly revealed, distinguishing knowledge before and after transmission. When fading remains unobserved, we derive capacity bounds and explicit formulas for an on/off channel. We also establish strong-converse bounds, with exact thresholds for the deterministic loss models considered here, observed fading, and unobserved fading with finitely many possible transmissivities. Under mean energy constraints, these thresholds generally exceed the corresponding finite-energy capacities. These results guide energy allocation and provide benchmarks for energy-constrained optical quantum communication with multimode signals and variable loss.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general, syndrome-based framework for designing fault-tolerant logical gates in quantum error-correcting codes. By relating logical operations directly to the stabilizer syndromes of physical Pauli rotations, the authors provide systematic methods to construct low-weight gates on various qLDPC codes. This advances the understanding of how code structure dictates computational capability.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract coding theory, the systematic construction of logical gates is crucial for realizing any quantum computation, including those involving complex interactions relevant to AMO physics or quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a general framework to systematically design fault-tolerant logical quantum gates (both Clifford and non-Clifford) within Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs). It aims to unify the construction process beyond previous limitations.

**Main result.** The framework establishes stabilizer syndromes as a constructive link, allowing the explicit, low-weight construction of various logical gates (like addressed T gates and C^rZ gates) on specific codes such as BB and Mirror qLDPC codes.

**Method.** The core method involves using the stabilizer syndromes of commuting Pauli rotations to derive an exact codespace-preservation condition. This is supplemented by analytic constructions using Clifford algebra bases (CABs) to solve syndrome constraints.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on stabilizer codes, particularly quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes like the Bivariate-Bicycle (BB) and Mirror codes, and the recursive hypercube construction. These codes are used to encode quantum information.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer syndromes, gate weight, code distance (d), and the resulting logical gate type (e.g., CNOT, T-gate).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$, the general generator weight bound $\lceil d/2 ceil$, and the rotation angles for non-Clifford operations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that logical operations can be systematically organized by analyzing the commutation relations (syndromes) of the physical Pauli rotations.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention tables summarizing bounded physical searches for logical gates on the $[[16, 4, 4]]$ and $[[64, 8, 8]]$ hypercube codes, grouping actions by family.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general syndrome-partition condition, applies it to existing qLDPC codes, utilizes analytic constructions via Clifford algebra bases, and finally examines the resulting fault-tolerance properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computation requires error-correcting codes that not only protect quantum information but also support useful logical operations with practical physical realizations. Here we develop a general framework in which the stabilizer syndromes of commuting Pauli rotations directly organize logical-gate design. We derive an exact codespace-preservation condition valid at arbitrary rotation angles, providing a common stabilizer-level description for CSS and non-CSS codes and for both Clifford and non-Clifford operations. A particularly useful equal-syndrome specialization reduces Clifford gate design to stabilizer factorization, yields a general generator weight bound of $\lceil d/2\rceil$ for distance-$d$ codes, and systematically extends to logical rotations higher in the Clifford hierarchy. Applied to bivariate-bicycle (BB) and non-CSS Mirror qLDPC codes, the framework gives explicit low-weight Clifford and controlled-phase gates. Our implementations yield new low-weight gates, including an addressed $T$ gate on the $[[16,4,4]]$ BB code, $T$-type rotations on the $[[64,8,8]]$ BB code, and CCZ-, CS-, and $T$-type operations on the $[[36,6,6]]$ Mirror code. We also develop analytic constructions using Clifford algebra bases, which turn the syndrome constraints into explicit algebraic relations and provide a systematic route to logical addressability. We use this approach to analytically construct addressed CNOT, SWAP, controlled, and CCZ-type gates on recursive hypercube codes. An independent construction on middle quantum Reed--Muller codes yields C${}^r$Z gates that saturate the equal-syndrome weight bound. These results establish stabilizer syndromes as a constructive link between the stabilizer structure of a quantum code, the logical computations it can directly support, and the fault-tolerance properties of their physical realizations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of quantifying entanglement and communication capacity for noisy quantum channels using Gaussian states. It develops sophisticated convex optimization frameworks to compute Gaussian-restricted entanglement measures like Rains entanglement. The results provide rigorous, computationally bounded estimates for extractable entanglement and secret key rates under realistic physical constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides concrete, computationally tractable bounds for quantifying entanglement and communication capacity when noise and loss are present.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops rigorous methods to compute Gaussian-restricted versions of entanglement measures, such as relative entropy of entanglement and Rains entanglement, which are crucial for quantifying quantum resources in noisy communication channels.

**Main result.** It establishes key identities, such as the equality between Gaussian Rains entanglement and Gaussian PPT relative entropy, and provides polynomial-time complexity bounds for calculating extractable entanglement and secret key rates.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced convex optimization techniques, including integral representations derived from the relative entropy, and utilize specialized convex-majorization algorithms for numerical computation.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on bosonic Gaussian states, which are compactly described by their means and covariance matrices, modeling quantum information transmission using light.

**Key observables.** Relative entropy of entanglement (REE), Rains entanglement (RG), extractable entanglement, and secret key rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Input-energy constraints, covariance matrices (V, W), and the required accuracy bits for numerical computation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the restriction to Gaussian states, which provides a tractable, though potentially suboptimal, upper bound compared to general quantum states.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention comparisons of derived bounds with achievable rates for two- and three-mode examples.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the mathematical foundations of Gaussian entanglement measures via path integrals, to developing convex optimization frameworks, deriving operational bounds for communication tasks, and finally applying these bounds to specific channel simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Loss and noise limit the use of light to distribute entanglement, transmit quantum information, and establish secret keys. Gaussian states have a compact description in terms of means and covariance matrices, but their entanglement still requires global optimization. We develop a framework for computing Gaussian-restricted versions of the relative entropy of entanglement and Rains entanglement. An integral representation reveals a convex geometry under matrix geometric means and reduces Gaussian Rains entanglement to relative entropy minimized over Gaussian states with positive partial transpose. We obtain unique optimal reference states and additivity for independent Gaussian input states. Our convex-majorization algorithm has an explicit geometric convergence rate, uniform in the faithful regularization parameter and valid for pure input states. Controlled numerical integration and approximate convex subproblem solutions give a bit-complexity bound that is polynomial in accuracy bits for each fixed rational input covariance matrix, with explicit dependence on covariance size. These calculations bound extractable entanglement and, through the relative entropy of entanglement, extractable secret key. Teleportation simulation, energy allocation, and a convex optimization of passive-basis loss cores give bounds on quantum and private communication under an input-energy constraint, allowing arbitrary two-way classically assisted protocols. Two- and three-mode examples compare the bounds with achievable rates, including matching pure-loss benchmarks. Finally, we separate the Gaussian-restricted quantities from their unrestricted counterparts and give a photon-number truncation method with controlled error for computing the latter.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the problem of 'two-Hamiltonian universality,' determining the minimum number of times one must switch between two fixed Hamiltonian controls to generate any desired unitary transformation in a quantum system. It establishes that this required number scales linearly with the system dimension $d$ for generic cases. The results provide crucial theoretical bounds for quantum control protocols, which are vital for quantum computing and simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, quantitative bounds on the complexity of quantum control, which is fundamental to designing quantum gates and understanding the limitations of physical implementations of quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimal number of alternating Hamiltonian switches required to synthesize any arbitrary unitary evolution in a $d$-dimensional quantum system.

**Main result.** For generic Hamiltonians, the required number of switches scales linearly with the dimension $d$ in expectation, and specific bounds (like $9d$) are derived for certain structured cases.

**Method.** The authors relate the problem to finding the minimal length representation of unitaries as products of elements diagonal in two different eigenbases, utilizing techniques from group theory and graph theory.

**Model / system.** The model involves a $d$-dimensional quantum system whose evolution is governed by alternating unitary operators derived from two fixed Hamiltonians, $H_1$ and $H_2$.

**Key observables.** The minimal number of switches ($n$ or $\ell$) required to achieve a target unitary $W \in U(d)$, and the approximation error $\|W - V\|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$, the structure of the change-of-basis matrix between the two eigenbases, and the spectral properties (linear independence over $\mathbb{Q}$) of the eigenvalues.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The main results often assume the spectra of the Hamiltonians are sufficiently generic (eigenvalues linearly independent over $\mathbb{Q}$), although bounds are extended to non-generic cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general problem of two-Hamiltonian universality to deriving constructive upper bounds using representations of unitaries via Householder reflections, culminating in explicit bounds on the required number of switches and approximation errors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Any pair of sufficiently generic Hamiltonians on $\mathbb{C}^d$ generates the full unitary group, i.e., any $W\in U(d)$ can be synthesized to arbitrary precision by alternating between the two evolutions for suitably chosen durations. For any given pair of Hamiltonians, the minimal number of switches required to cover the entire group is at least $d$ and unbounded in general. Despite this, we show that it scales linearly in $d$ in expectation. Assuming that the two spectra are sufficiently generic, we derive constructive upper bounds on the minimal switching number as a function of the change-of-basis matrix between the two eigenbases. In particular, if this matrix has a column whose entries all have equal moduli, as for a complex Hadamard matrix, then $9d$ switches suffice to reach every unitary. To obtain these results, we construct a short exact representation of any unitary as a product of unitaries that are alternately diagonal in two given bases. All bounds are extended to non-generic spectra and bounded evolution times.

</details>

**Authors:** Orson Ye, James Bonfield, Joshua Cudby, Richard Durbin, Sergii Strelchuk  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18029v1>

**Summary.** This paper enhances phylogenetic reconstruction by tackling the difficult problem of enumerating all maximum compatible character sets. It combines graph reduction techniques with quantum sampling methods and benchmarks these against advanced classical solvers. The goal is to efficiently solve the underlying maximum weight clique problem derived from sequence compatibility data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core problem is combinatorial biology, the explicit formulation of the MWC problem as a QUBO/MWIS problem and the application of QAOA instances make it relevant to quantum algorithm development for hard optimization problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computationally hard problem of enumerating all maximum weight cliques (MWC) derived from the Maximum Compatibility Criterion used in phylogenetic reconstruction.

**Main result.** The authors propose a hybrid approach combining graph reduction techniques with quantum sampling and advanced classical solvers to tackle the enumeration bottleneck, showing improvements over existing methods.

**Method.** The methodology involves formulating the problem as a maximum weight independent set enumeration, applying lossless graph reductions, and testing bespoke Quantum Approximate Optimisation Algorithm (QAOA) instances alongside classical solvers like AClique and SCCWalk4L.

**Model / system.** The model is phylogenetic reconstruction based on Multiple Sequence Alignments (MSAs), where compatibility data forms a weighted graph whose MWC corresponds to the optimal set of characters retained.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the size and weight of the maximum compatible character set (MWC), and the computational time required to enumerate the entire set of optimal solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size (number of vertices/characters), weights assigned to characters, and the number of samples/iterations used in quantum algorithms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes that graph reduction and quantum sampling can be effectively combined to maintain exact reconstruction, although the quantum results are not claimed as a definitive quantum advantage.

**Figures summary.** Figures benchmark various classical solvers (FastWCLQ2, SCCWalk4L, AClique) showing the trade-off between computational time and the quality (size) of the found maximum clique for graphs of increasing size.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon existing work (Cherry's algorithm), first detailing the formulation as an MWC enumeration problem, then introducing graph reduction techniques, and finally exploring hybrid solvers combining these reductions with quantum sampling and benchmarking against state-of-the-art classical solvers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximum compatibility provides a criterion for phylogenetic reconstruction in which the objective is to retain a maximum set of mutually compatible characters. For binary characters, pairwise compatibility can be represented by a weighted graph whose vertices correspond to characters and whose edges connect compatible pairs. A leading algorithm by Cherry [BMC Bioinformatics, 18(1):127, 2017] uses this representation to identify maximum compatible character sets as maximum weight cliques.   We formulate the problem as maximum weight independent set enumeration on the complement graph and use deterministic postprocessing to map sampled bit strings to maximal feasible solutions. To explore the prospects of quantum computational advantage, we further construct bespoke correction-in-the-loop Quantum Approximate Optimisation Algorithm instances and find that traditional Lagrange multiplier choices may not be optimal for the enumeration problem on noisy devices.   We also introduce lossless graph reductions with reconstruction rules that preserve the complete set of optimal solutions. Our results indicate that graph reduction and sampling can be effectively combined to address the enumeration bottleneck in maximum compatibility phylogeny, while retaining exact reconstruction when the residual optimum set is completely resolved.   We enhance Cherry's approach to maximum compatibility by introducing graph reduction techniques and quantum sampling. Our classical benchmarks show a clear difference between finding one optimum and recovering the optimum set in practice. We demonstrate the utility of our methods on graphs constructed from SARS-CoV-2 sequences.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of automatically checking if two quantum error-correcting codes are equivalent, accounting for multiple sources of ambiguity. The authors develop and benchmark a hybrid computational framework that combines several advanced techniques to solve this problem efficiently. The work provides a crucial tool for the field by offering scalable, systematic methods for code comparison.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QEC, the rigorous development of automated algebraic and computational tools for determining structural equivalence is highly relevant to characterizing complex quantum states and codes used in quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops automated methods to determine if two descriptions of Quantum Error-Correction (QEC) codes are equivalent under various transformations.

**Main result.** No single algorithmic paradigm works universally, necessitating a hybrid checker combining group-action search, isomorphism methods, and constraint solving for optimal performance.

**Method.** The authors formalize equivalence notions (like permutation and local-Clifford) and combine three distinct algorithmic strategies into a staged Refute-Refine-Decide architecture.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Quantum Error-Correction Codes (QECCs), specifically stabilizer codes and CSS codes, which are defined by their generator matrices and associated Pauli group structures.

**Key observables.** Mean runtimes for equivalence checking, the fraction of distinguished qubit pairs ($ar{q}$), and the number of SAT solver decisions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical qubit number ($n$), number of stabilizers ($r$), and the specific equivalence notion (permutation vs. local-Clifford).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes that the core challenge lies in automating the comparison across different representations and physical freedoms inherent in code descriptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ambiguity of code representations (e.g., Steane code realizations) and compare the runtime and memory scaling of various encoding techniques (Full-Group vs. Matroid-Circuit) for equivalence checking.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically defines the problem by separating physical freedom from representation freedom, develops multiple algorithmic tools (group action, isomorphism, constraint solving), and empirically evaluates their combined performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error-correction code and circuit construction is an active field of research, motivating automated methods for identifying equivalent designs. We develop and evaluate automated strategies for deciding whether two descriptions of quantum error-correction codes are equivalent under specified transformations. We formalize permutation equivalence for stabilizer and CSS codes and local-Clifford equivalence for stabilizer codes within a unified framework that separates physical from representation freedom. We develop three decision strategies (group-action search, isomorphism-based methods, and constraint solving), together with invariants and signatures for preprocessing. The empirical evaluation shows that no paradigm dominates across equivalence notions, code families, and parameter ranges. We therefore combine their complementary strengths in input-dependent hybrid checkers following a staged Refute-Refine-Decide architecture. Mean hybrid runtimes remain below 10 s for most evaluated structured-code cases with $n \leq 37$; for inequivalent code instances with $n=144$, they are about 310 s for permutation and 16 s for local-Clifford equivalence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the conditions under which quantum uniformity measures act as true norms. It proves that these measures are only norms for $k=2, 3,$ and $4$, while demonstrating that the triangle inequality fails for all $k \ge 5$. This result provides a complete mathematical answer to an open question concerning the structure of quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The failure of fundamental mathematical properties like the triangle inequality for higher-order quantum measures suggests deep structural limitations in how these measures capture quantum correlations, which is crucial for understanding resource theories in quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical properties, specifically the triangle inequality, of quantum uniformity measures ($\lVert \cdot Vert_{Qk}$).

**Main result.** The quantum uniformity measure $\lVert \cdot Vert_{Qk}$ is proven to be a norm only for $k=2, 3, 4$; for all $k \ge 5$, the triangle inequality fails.

**Method.** The proof for $k=4$ uses the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality on a defined quantum pairing, while the failure for $k \ge 5$ is shown by constructing contradictions using linear systems over finite fields.

**Model / system.** The context involves linear operators $T$ acting on the tensor product Hilbert space $H^{\otimes n}$, where $H = \mathbb{C}^p$ ($p$ prime). The measures are related to the Clifford hierarchy, relevant for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Key observables.** Quantum uniformity measures ($\lVert \cdot Vert_{Qk}$), quantum uniformity pairing ($\langle \cdot, \cdot angle_{Q4}$), and Weyl moments ($m_r(\psi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The integer $k$, which dictates the specific uniformity measure being analyzed; the local dimension $p$ (a prime).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on relating the quantum norm to norms derived from associated linear systems, particularly the failure of $L(k)$ to be a norming system for $k \ge 5$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the necessary mathematical tools, proving $\lVert \cdot Vert_{Q4}$ is a norm using Cauchy-Schwarz. It then proves the failure of the triangle inequality for $k \ge 5$ by constructing counterexamples using finite field linear algebra, providing a complete answer to an open problem.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that for $k=4$ the quantum uniformity measures are norms, whereas for every $k\geq5$ the triangle inequality fails. This gives a complete answer to an open question of Bu, Gu, and Jaffe. The $k=4$ case is proved using the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the quantum uniformity pairing $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{Q^4}$ introduced here, together with the triangle inequality in a suitable finite-dimensional Bochner $L^8$ space. For $k\geq5$, we construct systems of linear equations over a finite field and derive a contradiction from the structure theory of norming linear systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that the discrete and continuous definitions of the Entanglement of Formation coincide for infinite-dimensional bipartite quantum systems. The authors achieve this by developing a powerful discretization theorem that allows any continuous ensemble to be approximated arbitrarily closely by a countable ensemble. This result unifies the mathematical treatment of entanglement measures in complex quantum settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical grounding for entanglement measures in infinite dimensions, which is crucial for developing robust quantum information protocols that rely on these quantities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove the mathematical coincidence between two definitions of the Entanglement of Formation (EoF) for infinite-dimensional bipartite quantum systems: the discrete version (based on countable decompositions) and the continuous version (based on Borel probability measures).

**Main result.** The core result is the equality $E_d^F(ho) = E_c^F(ho)$ for all bipartite states $ho$, establishing that the continuous definition does not yield a lower bound below that achievable by countable ensembles.

**Method.** The proof employs an 'exact discretization theorem' by mapping the continuous ensemble to a Borel measure on Hilbert-Schmidt operators, which is then recursively partitioned and approximated by a countable ensemble.

**Model / system.** The system involves bipartite quantum states $ho$ on separable Hilbert spaces $H_A \otimes H_B$. The analysis is formalized using the representation of pure states via Hilbert-Schmidt operators $A \in \mathcal{S}_2(H_B, H_A)$.

**Key observables.** Entanglement of Formation ($E^F$), Entanglement Entropy ($S(\cdot)$), and the associated cost functional $J(A)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The proof involves taking a limit $\eta 	o 0$ and relies on contraction factors $\alpha = \eta/4$ and $\eta/2$ during the recursive decomposition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof assumes the existence of a Borel section for lifting the measure $\mu$ to a probability measure $\mu_0$, and it requires the cost functional $J$ to be convex and lower semicontinuous.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the proof by first establishing the necessary discretization theorem (Theorem 1), then formalizing the continuous ensemble as a Borel measure on amplitudes, and finally applying a recursive decomposition process (Lemma 5) to show that the total cost converges to the discrete definition's value.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is proved that every Borel ensemble of pure states on a separable bipartite Hilbert space with finite average entanglement admits a countable ensemble with the same barycenter and arbitrarily small additional average entanglement. Consequently, the discrete and continuous versions of the Entanglement of Formation coincide on the whole space of states of infinite-dimensional bipartite quantum system (with infinite values allowed).   The main idea of the proof (realized with the AI-assistance) is the identification of a Borel ensemble of pure bipartite states with a Borel probability measure on the unit sphere of the space of Hilbert--Schmidt operators followed by dividing the support of this measure into appropriate cells and replacing each cell by its conditional barycenter. The resulting countable ensemble is obtained by a recursive construction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses advanced molecular simulations (OPES and infinityRETIS) to study how calcium carbonate crystallizes in water. The research distinguishes between simple ionic aggregation and the formation of local structural order (the first coordination sphere). It finds that the crystallization pathway is complex, depending on the solution's supersaturation, and involves two distinct mechanisms for forming the initial ordered units.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical crystallization, the use of advanced sampling techniques like RETIS and the detailed mapping of free-energy barriers to distinguish between local order and bulk aggregation provides a sophisticated framework applicable to understanding phase transitions in complex, multi-component systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex relationship between the initial formation of the first coordination sphere of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) and the overall process of ionic aggregation during crystallization in aqueous solutions.

**Main result.** The kinetics of forming the first coordination sphere are highly dependent on the supersaturation level, revealing two distinct formation pathways (near minimum size vs. within large amorphous aggregates).

**Method.** The research employs advanced molecular simulation techniques, specifically On-the-fly probability-enhanced sampling (OPES) for free-energy landscapes and asynchronous/infinite-swap replica exchange transition interface sampling (infinityRETIS) for reaction kinetics.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is aqueous solutions of calcium carbonate ions (CaCO3), using a full-atomistic interaction potential combined with the SPC/Fw water model.

**Key observables.** Free-energy landscapes, reaction rate constants, average radius of gyration (Rg), and structural shape parameters (K1, K2) characterizing ionic clusters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Two levels of supersaturation (0.01 and 0.02) and varying ion concentrations are key parameters used to probe different aggregation regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that crystallisation can be analyzed by differentiating between the local structural ordering (coordination sphere formation) and the bulk aggregation process.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize CFE/FES surfaces mapping structural parameters and coordination numbers for various ion pairs, and probability density plots showing cluster size changes along reactive paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first establishing the simulation methods (OPES, infinityRETIS) to study the formation of the first coordination sphere. It then compares kinetic behavior across different supersaturation levels, linking kinetic differences to concentration-dependent aggregation, and finally details the two coexisting formation pathways.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Crystallisation of calcium carbonate (CaCO$_3$) in aqueous solutions is essential for carbon dioxide sequestration and the sustainable production of calcium carbonate materials, which are widely used in industry and increasingly explored for novel applications. While crystallisation is often analysed within the framework of classical nucleation theory, CaCO$_3$ and other ionic crystals often form through amorphous precursor phases, motivating the need for alternative descriptions. In this work, we investigate the relationship between the formation of the first CaCO$_3$ coordination sphere, a fundamental unit of local ordering, and ionic aggregation using two complementary simulation approaches: on-the-fly probability-enhanced sampling (OPES) and asynchronous and infinite-swap replica exchange transition interface sampling ($\infty$RETIS). OPES was used to explore free-energy landscapes, whereas $\infty$RETIS was used to determine reaction kinetics. We studied systems at two levels of supersaturation, between which the formation of the first coordination sphere exhibited distinct kinetic behaviour. These differences were found to be linked to concentration-dependent ionic aggregation. At lower concentration, aggregation remains limited, whereas higher concentration promotes extensive spinodal-like aggregation. For both concentrations, the first coordination sphere forms within the pre-formed clusters through one of two distinct pathways: either within a cluster close to the minimum size required for its formation, or within larger amorphous aggregates whose size and structure depend on concentration. The coexistence of these pathways at both levels of supersaturation reveals the multifaceted nature of early-stage CaCO$_3$ crystallisation and highlights the importance of differentiating ionic aggregation and organisation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces PADO-Pauli, a highly optimized, GPU-accelerated toolkit for performing Pauli propagation—the classical evaluation of quantum circuit expectation values. By compiling circuits once and reusing the structure for batched evaluations with automatic differentiation, it drastically speeds up the training of variational quantum algorithms. This makes complex quantum simulations practical for modern quantum machine learning research.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, high-performance computational backend for implementing and training quantum machine learning models, which is vital for any theoretical physicist working on quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for an efficient classical method to evaluate expectation values of parameterized quantum circuits, which are central to training variational quantum algorithms (VQAs).

**Main result.** PADO-Pauli is presented as a GPU-accelerated engine that significantly outperforms existing tools, achieving up to 10x speedup and lower memory usage compared to competitors.

**Method.** It uses Pauli propagation in the Heisenberg picture, compiling the circuit and observables into sparse matrices that are reused for multiple evaluations, including native gradient calculation via PyTorch's automatic differentiation.

**Model / system.** The system involves parameterized quantum circuits whose observables are represented as sums of Pauli strings. The method approximates the expectation value by evolving the observable through the circuit gates.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of Pauli strings, Pauli weight, and coefficient size.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits (up to 28 checked), rotation angles ($	heta$), Pauli weight truncation limit ($w_{	ext{max}}$), and coefficient size threshold ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on truncation (by Pauli weight and coefficient size) to keep the computation tractable, and the total error is bounded by the sum of discarded coefficient masses.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of the PADO-Pauli workflow, illustrating single compilation, step-wise truncation, and subsequent multiple evaluations with gradient tracking.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical basis of Pauli propagation, introduces the PADO-Pauli GPU engine for compilation and evaluation, discusses various truncation and noise modeling techniques, and concludes with performance comparisons against state-of-the-art tools.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational quantum algorithms utilize parameterized circuits many times during training, at new parameter values, over batches of data, and with gradients. Pauli propagation approximates the expectation values of such circuits classically by evolving the observable in the Heisenberg picture as a truncated sum of Pauli strings. We present PADO-Pauli, a GPU engine for Pauli propagation that compiles a circuit and its observables once into sparse matrices in which each rotation angle contributes only a cosine and a sine factor, so every later evaluation reuses the compiled program. It evaluates batches of parameters and data inputs embedded as rotation angles. By default its gradients come from a vector-Jacobian product registered with PyTorch's automatic differentiation, so they flow through the angles to any classical layer that produces them. PADO-Pauli also provides truncation by Pauli weight and coefficient size, and noise channels. Zero-filtering removes terms that do not contribute to an expectation value in the all-zero reference state and makes the batched forward pass about 1.5 times faster. Compared to cuPauliProp on an NVIDIA A100, PADO-Pauli evaluates up to 10 times faster on 4 to 12 qubits and uses up to 3.1 times less memory on 16 qubits. We check its accuracy against exact statevector simulation up to 28 qubits. These features make PADO-Pauli a practical toolkit for training variational quantum algorithms, including quantum machine learning models.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates the exact critical exponents for the 3D Ising model, proving that the correlation-length exponent $
u$ is $rac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{2}$. It achieves this by synthesizing scaling relations with advanced mathematical techniques, providing highly precise theoretical values for $eta$ and $\gamma$ that match modern numerical estimates.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous determination of critical exponents using advanced mathematical structures (like those involving algebraic integers) provides deep theoretical insights into critical phenomena that can inspire new mathematical techniques applicable to quantum critical points.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the exact value of the correlation-length exponent ($
u$) and derive algebraic relations for the critical exponents ($eta$ and $\gamma$) in the 3D Ising model.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the exact correlation-length exponent for the 3D Ising model is $
u = rac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{2}$, and they derive algebraic expressions for $eta$ and $\gamma$ based on this value.

**Method.** The derivation employs a combination of group theoretical approaches, constructive geometry, and the unification of scaling relations derived from different theoretical bounds.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the critical behavior of the 3D Ising model, a canonical model for studying phase transitions.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents: $
u$ (correlation-length exponent), $eta$, and $\gamma$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spatial dimension $d$, particularly $d=3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on unifying various scaling relations and comparing results against high-precision numerical estimates from Conformal Bootstrap and Renormalization Group methods.

**Figures summary.** Table I shows equivalence conditions for $
u$ in $d=2, 3, 4$. Table II compares $
u$ estimates from various methods, confirming the derived value. Table III compares $\gamma_\epsilon$ using the proposed formula against bootstrap and RG data.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing scaling relations, deriving a general form for $
u$ based on dimension $d$, and then applying advanced mathematical tools (like Galois theory) to fix the exact value of $
u$ for $d=3$, subsequently determining $eta$ and $\gamma$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Based on our previous results regarding critical exponent estimates for the 3D Ising model and scaling relations [1], we have proven that the exact value of the correlation-length exponent is $\frac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{2}$. This is in very close agreement with the most precise numerical estimates currently available. Using a group theoretical approach and some propositions of constructive geometry, we derived an exact construction of algebraic expressions for the critical exponents $γ$ and $β$. We proved that these expressions depend solely on the exact value of $ν\,$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QGrAT, a novel quantum transformer designed for jet tagging in high-energy physics by embedding the Lorentz symmetry of spacetime into its attention mechanism. It utilizes the structure of the Clifford algebra to define invariant couplings between constituent qubits. The resulting model shows strong performance, demonstrating that explicitly modeling three-particle interactions is key to improving background rejection.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics, the core methodology—encoding physical symmetries (Lorentz invariance) into the structure of quantum gates and attention mechanisms—is highly relevant for developing physically constrained quantum machine learning models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing compact quantum jet taggers capable of identifying highly boosted heavy particles by incorporating fundamental physical symmetries.

**Main result.** The proposed QGrAT architecture achieved competitive performance (AUC 0.944) on top tagging benchmarks, with the explicit inclusion of three-particle correlations significantly boosting background rejection.

**Method.** The method introduces QGrAT, a quantum transformer that encodes geometric features derived from particle four-momenta using the structure of the spacetime Clifford algebra $	ext{C}\ell(1,3)$.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum transformer operating on constituent qubits, where attention couplings are determined by Lorentz-invariant inner products derived from the grades of the Clifford algebra. The system is tested using state-vector simulation on Top Tagging benchmarks.

**Key observables.** Area Under the Curve (AUC), background rejection ($1/\epsilon_B$), and signal efficiency ($\epsilon_S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ten constituents per jet; the use of grades 0-3 and an auxiliary three-particle qubit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The component-wise input encoding makes the complete classifier frame dependent, even though the attention couplings are Lorentz invariant.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometric interpretation of the five grades in $	ext{C}\ell(1,3)$ and maps them to the QGrAT features used in the quantum circuit.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds mathematically by first introducing the Clifford algebra, then proving the Lorentz invariance of the grade-specific inner products, detailing the quantum circuit construction, and finally presenting performance evaluations and analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lorentz symmetry and the three-prong structure of hadronic top decays provide physical guidance for designing compact quantum jet taggers. We introduce QGrAT (Quantum Grade-Aware Transformer), a quantum transformer that incorporates these structures into its interactions between constituent qubits. Geometric features constructed from the constituent four-momenta are organised by the grades of the spacetime Clifford algebra $C\ell(1,3)$, and their grade-specific inner products determine the pairwise attention couplings. For fixed constituents and co-transforming reference vectors, these couplings are Lorentz invariant, although the component-wise input encoding makes the complete classifier frame dependent. An auxiliary qubit introduces an explicit three-particle interaction through products of pairwise Minkowski inner products, while a trainable in-circuit readout converts the resulting quantum state into a classification score. We evaluate the architecture in state-vector simulation on the Top Tagging benchmarks. With ten constituents per jet, the configuration using grades $0\text{-}3$ and the three-particle ancilla requires twelve qubits and $144$ trainable parameters, reaching AUC $0.944 \pm 0.002$ and background rejection $1/\varepsilon_B = 35.7 \pm 2.4\ (96.8 \pm 7.7)$ at signal efficiency $\varepsilon_S = 0.50\ (0.30)$. This performance is competitive with the L-GATr-Slim baseline at matched constituent and parameter budgets. Adding the three-particle ancilla raises background rejection at $50\%$ signal efficiency from $19.9$ to $35.7$, whereas extending the representation to the tested grade-$4$ feature gives no consistent improvement. The study establishes a framework for incorporating spacetime geometry into quantum attention and identifies explicit three-particle correlations as the principal source of improved background rejection among the architectural variations tested.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces an entropic descent method to solve the nonconvex optimization problem of maximizing secrecy capacity in wiretap channels. By leveraging relative-entropy identities, the authors prove quantitative descent and convergence for both classical and quantum settings. This provides a powerful, general framework for analyzing information-theoretic limits in secure quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical tools developed for convergence in nonconvex optimization (entropic descent, KL arguments) are highly relevant for analyzing complex quantum information tasks where capacity calculations are non-convex.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops and analyzes an entropic descent method to optimize the secrecy capacity of a wiretap channel, which is formulated as a nonconvex optimization problem.

**Main result.** The method establishes quantitative descent and convergence guarantees for the objective function value for arbitrary finite classical channels, and extends these techniques to noncommuting quantum outputs.

**Method.** It employs an entropic descent method, utilizing an exact relative-entropy identity to derive a channel-independent majorization bound and an explicit mirror-descent iteration.

**Model / system.** The model is a wiretap channel, analyzed in both classical and quantum settings. Optimization variables define a randomized encoder, jointly optimizing the input distribution and stochastic preprocessing.

**Key observables.** Secrecy capacity ($C_S$), objective function $F(p) = I(U; Z)_p - I(U; Y)_p$, and the relative entropy $D(\cdot \| \cdot)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis considers regimes ranging from arbitrary finite channels to strictly positive transition matrices and noncommuting quantum outputs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Convergence proofs rely on assumptions such as channel positivity (for full convergence) or the existence of a quantum score identity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 7 compares the private information rate for a noncommuting channel against independent convex-envelope benchmarks, showing the benefit of stochastic preprocessing.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the entropic descent for classical channels, proving convergence via Kurdyka–Łojasiewicz arguments. It then extends this framework to quantum settings, deriving quantum score identities and monotone alternating updates for covariant encoders.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop and analyze an entropic descent method for a nonconvex optimization problem on probability simplices arising in classical and quantum secure communication. The optimization variables describe a randomized encoder, allowing its input distribution and stochastic preprocessing to be optimized jointly. An exact relative-entropy identity yields a channel-independent majorization bound and an explicit mirror-descent iteration with a fixed unit step size. We establish quantitative descent, convergence of objective values, and stationarity of accumulation points on their support faces for arbitrary finite classical channels, without assuming degradedness or less noisiness. For strictly positive transition matrices, a square-root parametrization and a Kurdyka--Łojasiewicz argument establish finite length of the transformed trajectory and convergence of the entire sequence, including limits on the simplex boundary. For less-noisy channels, a restricted convex formulation admits global convergence with an explicit \(O(1/t)\) objective-error bound. We extend the relative-entropy construction to noncommuting quantum outputs and derive monotone alternating updates for covariant encoders, including correlated preprocessing over a fixed block. Numerical experiments compare the method with analytic benchmarks and constrained optimization, reveal multiple attracting values, and demonstrate strict improvements from stochastic preprocessing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates fundamental quantum observables, specifically supersymmetric Casimir energy and Rényi entropy, arising from surface defects in higher-dimensional SCFTs. It employs advanced techniques like equivariant integration of anomaly polynomials and verifies results using holographic brane dynamics. The findings establish a precise relationship between these quantum entanglement measures and the underlying gauge theory anomalies.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the connection between anomaly coefficients (topological invariants) and quantum entanglement measures (Rényi entropy) provides deep insights into the structure of quantum field theories, which is relevant for understanding entanglement in complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the contributions of half-BPS surface defects to supersymmetric Casimir energy and the universal logarithmic term of supersymmetric Rényi entropy in 4D and 6D Superconformal Field Theories (SCFTs).

**Main result.** The observables are found to depend on specific anomaly coefficients, with 6D theories exhibiting additional contributions from the gravitational anomaly compared to 4D theories.

**Method.** The primary methods involve equivariant integration of defect anomaly polynomials, extending these techniques to supersymmetric Rényi backgrounds, and using complementary checks like chiral algebras and holographic brane calculations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 4D $\mathcal{N}=4$ Super Yang-Mills theories with Gukov–Witten defects, and 6D $\mathcal{N}=(1, 0)$ SCFTs. The analysis utilizes concepts from string theory compactifications, such as probe D4-branes in AdS$_7 \times M_3$.

**Key observables.** Supersymmetric Casimir energy (dSCE) and the universal logarithmic term of supersymmetric Rényi entropy (dSRE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on R-symmetry anomaly coefficients ($k_R$) and gravitational anomaly coefficients ($k_g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume that anomaly control fixes the contributions without solving the full bulk-defect dynamics, and specific background solutions (like $F_0=0$) are used for brane calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates geometries for dSCE (surface defect wrapping $S^1_\beta \times S^1$ in $S^1_\beta \times S^{d-1}$) and dSRE (planar defect intersecting spherical entangling surface).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the framework by first defining the observables, then applying anomaly integration techniques for 4D and 6D theories, and finally verifying results using holographic brane actions and equation of motion checks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the contributions of half-BPS surface defects to supersymmetric Casimir energy and the universal logarithmic term of supersymmetric Rényi entropy in four- and six-dimensional SCFTs. Using equivariant integration of defect anomaly polynomials and its extension to supersymmetric Rényi backgrounds, we obtain closed expressions for these observables in terms of defect 't Hooft anomalies. For Gukov--Witten defects preserving transverse rotations in four-dimensional $\mathcal{N} = 4$ super Yang--Mills theories, both observables depend on a single R-symmetry anomaly. For $\mathcal{N} = (0, 4)$ defects in six-dimensional $\mathcal{N} = (1, 0)$ theories, additional terms arise from the gravitational anomaly. We provide complementary checks using chiral algebras, five-dimensional localization, and holographic probe branes at large $N$, while localized chiral fermions reproduce the gravitational-anomaly contributions. The framework also recovers Wilson-surface results in six-dimensional $\mathcal{N} = (2, 0)$ theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel, problem-informed framework to build interpretable quantum generators for modeling financial returns using a QGAN. By embedding domain knowledge—such as expected correlations and asymmetries—into the quantum circuit structure, the authors demonstrate that compact circuits can capture complex statistical features. This advances the field by linking explicit structural assumptions directly to the observable performance of quantum models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the methodology of imposing physical/structural constraints (like symmetry or known correlations) to guide quantum circuit ansatz design is highly relevant for developing physically meaningful quantum machine learning models in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the interpretability and trainability trade-off when using quantum circuits as function approximators for complex tasks like financial return generation.

**Main result.** The proposed problem-informed framework successfully guides the design of compact quantum generators that recover meaningful marginal and temporal distributional features of financial returns.

**Method.** They employ a hybrid Wasserstein Quantum Generative Adversarial Network (QGAN) trained on financial data, guiding the quantum circuit design using structural assumptions like relational structure and symmetry-inspired parameter sharing.

**Model / system.** The model uses quantum circuits (ZZ, directed ZZ-CRX, structured time evolution) to generate 16-step Bitcoin return windows. The circuit structure is explicitly informed by the statistical properties of the financial data.

**Key observables.** Marginal distribution fidelity, short-range absolute-return dependence, and median leverage shift.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of trainable coefficients (1 to 8) and circuit depth (1 to 4 layers).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that structural assumptions derived from the physical problem (finance) can guide the design of compact, efficient quantum generators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the workflow from empirical financial structure to mechanistic hypothesis, leading to operator construction and falsifiable predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from identifying the scientific problem (interpretability in QML) to proposing a problem-informed framework, detailing the QGAN setup and circuit design, presenting results on distributional recovery, and concluding with implications for systematic circuit design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

When used as function approximators, quantum circuits often lack interpretability and face a fundamental trade-off between expressivity and trainability. We propose a problem-informed framework for financial return generation that uses relational structure and symmetry-inspired parameter sharing to guide the construction of quantum circuits with few trainable parameters and empirically testable operator roles. We evaluate three quantum generator architectures, referred to as ZZ, directed ZZ-CRX, and structured time evolution, within a hybrid Wasserstein quantum generative adversarial network trained to generate 16-step Bitcoin return windows. Across circuits with one to four layers and one to eight trainable coefficients, these generators recover meaningful marginal and temporal distributional features, while single-layer configurations remain competitive with deeper variants. Across matched depths, independently trained ZZ-CRX configurations exhibit a predominantly downward shift in median leverage relative to ZZ, consistent with the intended directional role of the controlled-rotation construction. The small number of coefficients also enables direct landscape analysis, revealing competing parameter preferences between marginal and temporal objectives and helping to explain the difficulty of finding parameter settings that perform well on both. These results provide an initial demonstration that structural assumptions can guide the design of compact quantum generators and link circuit architecture to observable statistical behaviour, motivating more systematic and parameter-efficient approaches to quantum circuit construction.

</details>

### [Whither quantum advantage? New classical lower bounds for Max-Cut on large-girth 3-regular graphs](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17236v1)

**Authors:** Flaviano Morone, Shivang Rawat, Dries Sels  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17236v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.17236_figures/2610.17236_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Section of a 3-regular graph of girth g ≥6. The radius-2 neighborhood of an edge (u, v), consisting of the 14 vertices within distance 2 of u or v, and the 13 edges incident to u, v and their neighbors. The girth forces this tree structure. Any further edge among these vertices must join two leaves (since every other vertex already has degree 3) and it is allowed only if the two leaves lie on opposite sides of (u, v), where it closes a cycle of length 6 (dashed edge).</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.17236_figures/2610.17236_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Cut fraction versus depth on 3-regular graphs of girth g ≥2p + 2. Tree values cVIRAL(p) of Table I (cir- cles), QAOA values of Ref. [4] (squares) and explicit vector algorithm (TPM) of Ref. [8] (triangles). Statistical errors are smaller than the symbols. The dashed line is the up- per bound 0.92386 of Eq. (2), the dotted line the previous asymptotic lower bound 0.912. Inset: p ≥20, with the lower confidence bound L of Table I (open circles), which crosses 0.912 at p = 32. Lines are guides to the eye.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2610.17236_figures/2610.17236_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. VIRAL with p = 2 layers on the graph of Fig. 1 traced backwards from the output. (a), The cut variables σu, σv need su(2) and sv(2). (b), Through Eq. (3) these need the states at n = 1 of u, v and their four other neighbors a1, a2, b1, b2. No state of a leaf is needed at n = 1 or n = 2. (c), These six states need the seeds of all 14 vertices. The leaves enter only through their seeds, so the edges joining two leaves, such as the dashed one, play no role. Hence (σu, σv) is a fixed function of the 14 i.i.d. seeds on the tree of Fig. 1, the same for every edge of every 3-regular graph of girth g ≥6.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the Max-Cut problem on large-girth 3-regular graphs to test for quantum advantage. By analyzing the local structure using a classical algorithm called VIRAL, the authors derive rigorous lower bounds on the maximum cut fraction. These classical bounds surpass those from the QAOA for moderate depths, suggesting that quantum computation may not offer an exponential advantage for this specific optimization task.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the question of quantum advantage for combinatorial optimization problems like Max-Cut, providing concrete evidence that classical local algorithms can match or surpass current quantum algorithm performance on structured graphs.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the maximum cut fraction ($M_g$) for 3-regular graphs with large girth ($g$), challenging the notion of quantum advantage in optimization.

**Main result.** A classical local algorithm (VIRAL) provides rigorous lower bounds for $M_g$ that exceed those obtained from the QAOA for depths $p \ge 4$, suggesting quantum advantage may not exist for this problem.

**Method.** The authors analyze the expected cut fraction on the tree structure formed by the radius-$p$ neighborhood of an edge, using the Variational Iterative Rotation Algorithm (VIRAL) to establish these bounds.

**Model / system.** The model is the Max-Cut problem applied to 3-regular graphs, specifically focusing on the behavior of these graphs as their girth ($g$) becomes very large. The analysis relies on the local structure (tree approximation) around any given edge.

**Key observables.** The maximum cut fraction $M_g$, and the derived lower bound $c_{	ext{VIRAL}}(p)$ based on the depth-$p$ tree structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The depth parameter $p$ (related to girth $g$ by $g \ge 2p+2$), and the confidence level used for statistical bounding (e.g., 99.99999%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that for large girth, the local structure around any edge can be accurately modeled by a tree, allowing the cut fraction to be determined by local algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the radius-2 neighborhood of an edge in a 3-regular graph, and plots compare the cut fraction achieved by VIRAL, QAOA, and TPM across different depths.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for $M_g$ using local algorithms, applies the VIRAL algorithm to the depth-$p$ tree, compares its resulting bounds against established quantum (QAOA) and classical benchmarks (TPM), and concludes that classical methods match or exceed quantum performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Every 3-regular graph of girth at least $g$ has a cut containing at least a fraction $M_g$ of its edges, and the best rigorous bounds at large girth are $0.912\le \lim_{g\to\infty}M_g \le 0.92386$. Lower bounds on $M_g$ follow from the performance of local algorithms: on a graph of girth $g \ge 2p+2$ a depth-$p$ local algorithm sees the same tree around every edge, so its expected cut fraction on that tree bounds $M_g$ from below. We apply this strategy to the Variational Iterative Rotation Algorithm (VIRAL), a classical local algorithm, and evaluate it on the depth-$p$ tree for every even depth up to $p=32$. The resulting bounds exceed those recently obtained from the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) for every $p\ge 4$, by $0.005$ to $0.011$, and they extend the range of girths covered from $g=36$ to $g=66$. Notably, at $g=66$ we obtain $M_{66} \ge 0.9127$ at $99.99999\%$ confidence level, which raises the asymptotic lower bound from $0.912$ to $0.9127$.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive theory detailing how intense electromagnetic pulses induce deeply subwavelength structuring in semiconductors via a thermal-ionization instability. The core mechanism involves a competition between self-amplifying heating (positive feedback) and field weakening due to diffraction (negative feedback). The resulting model accurately predicts the observed structure periodicity based on material properties and pulse parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** The interplay between nonlinear material response (ionization) and linear wave phenomena (diffraction) in a time-dependent, non-equilibrium setting is highly relevant to open quantum systems and nonlinear optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory to explain the formation of deeply subwavelength electromagnetic structures in semiconductors when irradiated by intense, low-frequency electromagnetic pulses.

**Main result.** The theory successfully models the observed spatial period of these structures by balancing the positive feedback from thermal-ionization heating against the negative feedback from field diffraction.

**Method.** The analysis uses a coupled approach, solving the scalar wave equation for the electric field while incorporating rate equations for carrier density and temperature changes.

**Model / system.** The system is a semiconductor surface irradiated by intense THz/mid-IR pulses. The physics is governed by the competition between thermal runaway (heating/ionization) and the diffraction of the incident electromagnetic field.

**Key observables.** The spatial period (wavelength) of the resulting deeply subwavelength structures (LIPSS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump intensity, incident wavelength, pulse repetition rate, thermal conductivity, and carrier dynamics parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as the deeply subwavelength regime ($k_g \gg k_0\sqrt{|\epsilon|}$) and models carrier dynamics using the Drude response.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical mechanism, showing yellow loops representing positive feedback (heating) and blue loops representing negative feedback (diffraction/thermal diffusion).

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured by analyzing the problem in stages: diffraction on permittivity perturbations, single-pulse heating, slow evolution under many pulses, and the role of surface corrugation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a theory of ionization-assisted instability that governs the reflection of intense electromagnetic pulses from a semiconductor surface. Local overheating of the material increases the free-carrier density and conductivity, which in turn enhances the absorption of the incident radiation and further amplifies local heating and avalanche ionization. At high pump repetition rates, the overheating accumulates from pulse to pulse, leading to a gradual rise of the local material temperature and free carrier density - a positive feedback loop that drives thermal runaway. However, this effect competes with diffraction of the pump field on the emerging perturbation of the dielectric permittivity or on the surface relief, which reduces the local electric field amplitude in the overheated regions, thus providing negative feedback. We show that the competition between heat diffusion and diffraction near-field weakening creates conditions for the exponential growth of temperature perturbations with a certain wavenumber determined by the pulse repetition rate, pump field, thermal conductivity and other material parameters. The derived analytical expressions accurately describe the spatial period of deeply subwavelength structures observed in recent experiments on silicon structuring by intense terahertz radiation, and reproduce qualitatively dependencies of their period on the pump intensity, the incident wavelength and the number of pump pulses.

</details>

### [Feedback-Controlled Self-Trapping of Solitons](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18191v1)

**Authors:** Raonaqul Islam, Pradyoth Shandilya, Yunxiang Song, Alioune Niang, Gary Carter, Thomas Carruthers, Curtis Menyuk, Ergun Simsek  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18191v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an optoelectronic self-trapping technique to stabilize frequency combs generated in chip-scale microresonators. By using feedback derived from the comb output itself, it eliminates the need for bulky external low-noise RF sources. The method achieves noise suppression comparable to conventional techniques, making high-stability comb generation viable for fully integrated photonic platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses noise control in integrated photonics, which is crucial for realizing stable quantum light sources or high-precision quantum sensors, linking nonlinear optics to noise suppression techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is suppressing intrinsic microresonator noise that degrades the repetition-rate stability of chip-scale frequency combs.

**Main result.** The proposed feedback-controlled self-trapping scheme successfully suppresses repetition-rate noise to levels comparable with external referencing, while remaining fully integrated on a chip.

**Method.** The authors developed and analyzed both analytical and numerical models based on the Lugiato–Lefever equation and its linearized form to track soliton dynamics and noise propagation.

**Model / system.** The system involves chip-scale microresonators generating frequency combs via Dissipative Kerr Solitons (DKSs). The dynamics are modeled using the Lugiato–Lefever equation, and stability is analyzed via linearization around the steady-state solution.

**Key observables.** Repetition-rate ($	ext{frep}$) stability, noise Power Spectral Density (PSD) of frequency noise, and the evolution of soliton perturbations (eigenvalues).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The performance limit is set by the intrinsic pump-laser noise, and the system is analyzed for 100-GHz-FSR Si3N4 microring resonators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the self-trapping potential remains sufficient despite inheriting fluctuations correlated with the microcomb jitter, and it models the system using the LLE framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the noise PSDs for three cases: untrapped, externally trapped, and the proposed self-trapped system, demonstrating noise suppression in the latter two relative to the former.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the noise limitation in microcombs to proposing the self-trapping scheme. It develops the necessary mathematical models (LLE, linearization) and then rigorously compares the noise suppression achieved by the novel self-trapping method against conventional external referencing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chip-scale microresonator-based frequency combs, commonly referred to as microcombs, are promising sources for field-deployable metrology applications. However, intrinsic microresonator noise can make it difficult to generate microcombs with sufficiently low noise for applications. A widely used approach to overcome this limitation is to trap the DKS in the microresonator by phase-modulating the pump laser, although integrating a low-noise modulation reference in a fully on-chip platform remains challenging. Here, we propose an optoelectronic self-trapping scheme in which the impact of intrinsic microresonator noise on microcomb repetition-rate jitter is suppressed through feedback-controlled phase modulation of the driving laser so that the intrinsic noise of the laser sets the ultimate performance limit. This modification eliminates the need for an external low-phase-noise RF source, thereby enabling chip integration. We develop both analytical and numerical models for the proposed system and benchmark its performance against conventional externally referenced bulk implementations, showing that the proposed approach achieves comparable microcomb noise suppression while maintaining chip-scale compatibility.

</details>
